Key: TO = Target Ortho fact (must-know) · ↑ increased · ↓ decreased · → leads to / then · ✗ Wrong options = MCQ distractors · ✓ correct answer

Adolescent Idiopathic Scoliosis

Spine · 1 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitioncoronal deformity, Cobb > 10°, age 10–18 yrs; commonest type of scoliosis

Incidence3% for 10–20° curves · 0.3% for curves > 30°

Sex ratio1:1 small curves · 10:1 female for curves > 30°

Commonest patternright thoracic curve + thoracic hypokyphosis (T5–T12 ≈ 8°; normal 20–50°)
✗ Wrong options: right thoracic + hyperkyphosis · left thoracic

Family historymost patients positive

Aetiology

Pathophysiologyunknown / multifactorial: hormonal (melatonin), brain stem, proprioception, platelet, calmodulin

Neurocentral synchondrosis (NCS)cartilaginous plate between centrum + posterior neural arches
✗ Wrong options: anterior arches

NCS closure order Age
Cervical 5–6 yrs
Lumbar 11–12 yrs (~100% closed at age 10)
Thoracic 14–17 yrs (~50% closed at age 10)

NCS order trapcervical → lumbar → thoracic
✗ Wrong options: cervical → thoracic → lumbar

Skeletal maturity & progression risk

Best predictor of progressioncurve magnitude at peak height velocity (PHV); > 30° at PHV → > 70% reach surgical range
✗ Wrong options: > 20° at menarche · > 30° at Risser 2 · skeletal age 12

PHV timingbefore menarche and before Risser 1 (occurs during Risser 0) · menarche ≈ 1 yr after PHV · girls mature ~1.5 yrs (18 months) after menarche

Skeletal method best matching curve acceleration / PHVTanner-Whitehouse III RUS (radius, ulna, metacarpals + phalanges of digits 1, 3, 5) (✗ Wrong options: Greulich-Pyle · Oxford · Risser · Lenke) · TW stage III (✗ Wrong options: stage 2)

PHV correlates closest with (TO)olecranon apophysis fusion; olecranon stage 5 = PHV (stage 1 = 3.0 yrs before, stage 7 = 3.4 yrs after)
✗ Wrong options: triradiate closure · Risser 1 · menarche

Other PHV surrogates (TO)modified Oxford score 19–21 · calcaneal apophysis stage 3 · humeral head ossification between stages 2–3 · Sauvegrain elbow score · TW2 = most accurate/reproducible, GP = simpler, less reliable in Asians

Risk factor for progression Detail
Curve magnitude (immature) > 25° before maturity → continues to progress
Curve magnitude (mature) Thoracic > 50° · lumbar > 40° → progress 1–2°/yr
Remaining growth Age < 12 yrs · Tanner < 3 (F) · Risser 0–1 · open triradiate cartilage · PHV
Curve type Thoracic > lumbar · double > single
Risser Iliac apophysis Growth
0 No ossification Covers first 2/3 of pubertal spurt → greatest velocity of linear growth; highest progression risk with open triradiate
I 25% Velocity descending
II 50% GT apophysis unites; ~3 cm sitting growth left, no more LL growth
III 75% ~2 cm sitting growth
IV 100% ossified, not fused ~1 cm sitting growth; = skeletal maturity for bracing
V Fused Complete maturity

Open triradiate + < 18 h brace wearfactor most correlated with progression (Karol) → Risser 0 needs ≥ 18 h/day
✗ Wrong options: curves < 25° · Risser 1 · Sanders 6

Karol – progression to ≥ 50° in braceRisser 0 44.2% · Risser 1 6.9% · Risser 2 0%

Natural history into adulthood

Untreated AIS (Weinstein 50-yr)↑ chronic back pain (✓)
✗ Wrong options: difficult vaginal delivery / ↑ caesarean · pulmonary dysfunction at 38°

Cobb > 60° at maturity↑ acute + chronic back pain vs controls
✗ Wrong options: premature death · disability · depression · ADL limitation

Curves < 30° at maturityrarely progress · thoracic > 50° most likely to progress

Curves > 90°cardiopulmonary dysfunction, early death, pain, ↓ self-image

3Presentation

School screening7° on scoliometer (Adams forward bend) abnormal → correlates with ~20° Cobb

Adams forward bendingaxial rotation (rib prominence) = structural curve · sitting forward bend eliminates leg-length inequality

Back pain25–30% of all adolescents; mild, intermittent, non-limiting + typical right curve → stretching + lighter backpack · atypical pain → MRI / bone scan
✗ Wrong options: MRI · bone scan · TLSO for 20° · pain clinic

Atypical features requiring MRI

Exam finding (non-idiopathic) Suggests
Hairy patch Spina bifida / diastematomyelia
Subcutaneous tumours · pigmented lesions · café-au-lait Neurofibromatosis
Excessive flexibility Ehlers-Danlos / Marfan
Joint contractures CP / arthrogryposis
Craniofacial anomalies Syndromic scoliosis
Limb weakness Neuromuscular scoliosis
Cavovarus foot Neural axis anomaly → MRI
Asymmetric abdominal reflexes Syringomyelia (curve with little rotation)

Truncal shift (TO)loss of coronal balance in large idiopathic curves → NOT a sign of non-idiopathic scoliosis (EXCEPT answer)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

First films (TO)standing PA + lateral whole spine; PA = ↓ breast radiation (AP gives better quality)
✗ Wrong options: standing AP · supine · segment only

Cobb anglelines along the most tilted end vertebrae · > 10° = scoliosis · error 3–5°

Radiographic term Definition
Coronal balance C7 plumb line vs central sacral vertical line (CSVL)
Sagittal balance C7 plumb vs posterosuperior corner of S1
Stable zone Between vertical lines from lumbosacral facets
Stable vertebra Most proximal vertebra most closely bisected by CSVL
Neutral vertebra Rotationally neutral (spinous process equidistant from pedicles)
End vertebra Most tilted from horizontal apical vertebra
Apical vertebra Disc / vertebra deviated farthest from centre
Touched vertebra (TV) Last cephalad vertebra touched by CSVL
Clavicle angle Best predictor of post-op shoulder balance

MRIposterior fossa → conus; rules out Chiari / syrinx / cord cyst / tether

MRI indicated NOT an MRI indication
Left thoracic curve Left thoracolumbar / lumbar curve (TO – common pattern)
Short angular curve · apical kyphosis / excessive kyphosis Age 10–18
Rapid progression Risser stage
Neurologic signs, pain (atypical) Curve magnitude alone (without rapid progression)
Asymmetric abdominal reflexes · foot deformity (cavovarus) Mild activity-related back pain
Male, structural abnormality Routine initial work-up

Left thoracic curve in a neurologically normal childtotal spine MRI = next step
✗ Wrong options: bracing · PT · bending films · CT

Lenke classification

Components (TO)curve type 1–6 + lumbar modifier A/B/C + sagittal (thoracic) modifier −/N/+
✗ Wrong options: "thoracic modifier A/B/C"

Regionsproximal thoracic: apex T3–T5 · main thoracic: apex T6 → T11–12 disc · thoracolumbar: apex T12–L1 · lumbar: apex L1–2 disc → L4

Major curvelargest Cobb, always structural

Structural minor curveresidual ≥ 25° on supine side-bending OR kyphosis ≥ 20° (T2–T5 for PT; T10–L2 for TL/L)

Lenke type Name Structural curves
1 Main thoracic MT only
2 Double thoracic (TO) MT (major) + PT structural; TL/L non-structural
3 Double major MT (major) + TL/L structural; PT non-structural
4 Triple major All three structural
5 TL/L TL/L only (major)
6 TL/L–MT TL/L major (≥ 5° > MT) + MT structural
Lumbar modifier CSVL at lumbar apex
A Between pedicles
B Touches apical pedicle
C Completely medial to apical body/pedicle
Sagittal modifier (T5–T12) Kyphosis
− < 10° (hypokyphosis)
N 10–40°
+ > 40° (hyperkyphosis)

King-Moe5 thoracic curve types for Harrington instrumentation; coronal only, no sagittal plane → replaced by Lenke

5Management

AIS by Cobb angle + maturity
  • < 25° (any maturity)→OBSERVE, serial X-rays
  • 25–45°, Risser 0–2 (flexible, immature)→BRACE
    (goal = stop progression, not correct)
  • 25–45°, Risser 4–5 / mature→OBSERVE
  • > 45–50°→POSTERIOR SPINAL FUSION
    • Very young (Risser 0, girls < 10, boys < 13) / > 75° / stiff→ANTERIOR + POSTERIOR

Bracing – indications, wear time & efficacy

Parameter Value
Cobb range 25–45° (Orthobullets) · 20–40° (OB) · 20–25° with documented progression → brace — read the options
Maturity Risser 0, 1, 2 · Sanders 1–3 · age 10–15
Prescribed 16–23 h/day until maturity or surgery
Minimum effective (actual) > 12 h/day (12.9 h → 90–93% success, BrAIST) (✗ Wrong options: 6–8 h · 9–10 h)
Dose-response 17.7 h → 93%; > 12 h → 82% no progression; < 7 h → 69% progressed; prescribed 16 vs 23 h had no effect
Effect 50% ↓ need for surgery · NNT = 4 (compliant)
Best predictor of brace success Time in brace (✗ Wrong options: in-brace correction · PT first)

Brace success / failure (TO)success = < 5° progression · failure = ≥ 6° progression at discontinuation, > 45° before/at maturity, or switch to surgery
✗ Wrong options: "success = correction > 5°"

Skeletal maturity = stop braceRisser 4 · < 1 cm height change over 2 visits 6 months apart · 2 yrs post-menarche

Poor brace prognosispoor in-brace correction · hypokyphosis (relative CI) · male · obese · non-compliant

Brace (TO) Curve apex
Milwaukee (CTLSO) Above T7 (TO, Orthobullets) · at/proximal to T6 (OB) · double thoracic / apex above T8 do not respond to TLSO
TLSO / Boston (underarm) T7 or below (e.g. apex T12)
Charleston bending Night-time only

Sanders stagingAP hand X-ray + curve magnitude → risk of progression > 50° despite bracing in Lenke 1 + 3 curves · stage 3 = complete capping of phalangeal epiphyses

25° curve + Sanders stage Risk > 50° despite brace
1 99%
3 29% → brace most likely to change natural history (✓)
4–8 0%
45° curve ~100%

Bracing trapsscoliosis-specific exercises / PT not effective · spinal manipulation unsupported · 12° curve with 6° progression → observe · Risser 4 / Sanders 7 + 30° → observe · boy with 50° → surgery · bracing large (> 45°) curves can still delay surgery to maturity · congenital curve → no bracing

Fusion level selection

PSF indicationsCobb > 45° (Orthobullets) / > 50° (OB) · gold standard for thoracic + double major curves · usable for all idiopathic types

ASFthoracolumbar / lumbar curves with normal sagittal profile; saves lumbar levels; fuse end → end vertebra; not if thoracic hyperkyphosis > 40° (↑ pseudarthrosis)

Technique Fusion levels
Typical Proximal end vertebra → 1–2 levels cephalad to stable vertebra; double/triple major → distal end vertebra
Harrington 1 above + 2 below end vertebrae if within stable zone
Moe To neutral vertebra
Lenke All major curves + minor curves that are stiff or kyphotic
Touched vertebra Fuse to or caudad to TV → best correction; cephalad to TV → truncal shift, poor long-term outcome

Avoid L4 / L5Cochran: ↑ low back pain with fusion to L5 (less with L4) · pelvis almost never needed in AIS

Lenke 1B (65° MT, flexible PT + lumbar)PSF of thoracic curve only
✗ Wrong options: T2–L4 · brace · stapling · A/P

Selective thoracic fusionAVT ratio = thoracic AVT (C7 plumb → thoracic apex) ÷ TL/L AVT (CSVL → lumbar apex); > 1.2 → selective thoracic OK (e.g. 45/15 = 3.0 ✓)

LIV to prevent DJK / adding-oninclude stable sagittal vertebra (≥ 50% of body anterior to posterior sacral vertical line) · Lenke 1A-R (L4 tilted right) → ↑ adding-on · e.g. TL kyphosis T8–L2 with L3 the first SSV → LIV = L3
✗ Wrong options: T12 · L2 · L5 · pelvis

Isolated long PSF indicated13-yr Risser 3 with 55°
✗ Wrong options: JIS → needs anterior too · IIS → Mehta cast · hemivertebra + bar → bar excision · 27° → brace

Vertebral body staplingsmall thoracic curves < 35°, immature

Surgical technique

Pedicle screwsinsertional torque ∝ pullout resistance; ↑ by undertapping 1 mm + straightforward trajectory; not tapping may be stronger still · segmental screws → ↑ coronal correction, ↓ need for anterior release
✗ Wrong options: anatomic trajectory ↓ torque

Pedicle subtraction osteotomy (PSO)posterior closing wedge hinged on anterior column, usually L2 / L3 · commonest complication pseudarthrosis 25–30% · 1 PSO ≈ 3 Smith-Petersen osteotomies, more blood loss
✗ Wrong options: cord ischaemia · aorta < 1–2% · neuro deficit < 10%

Opioid-sparingshort-course post-op steroids ↓ opioid use
✗ Wrong options: bracing · avoiding drain (drains ↓ opioids) · delaying PT · pre-op opioids ↑ use

ED visits after PSF~10%, mostly minor → older age + more fusion levels
✗ Wrong options: BMI · Lenke type · ERAS ↓ visits · pelvic fixation/Ponte

Neuromonitoring

Standard of careSSEP + MEP; MEPs warn earliest of cord dysfunction, change faster than SSEPs

Alert thresholdsamplitude drop > 50% (Orthobullets) · critical: MEP 80%, SSEP 50% / latency 10% (OB)

Neuromonitoring signal drop
  • Technical / anaesthetic cause?→CHECK leads + IV agents
  • Not yet critical (e.g. MEP 60%, SSEP 30%)→REPOSITION, raise BP, check Hb
    (do not deepen anaesthesia — suppresses MEPs)
  • Persistent / critical→REVERSE CORRECTION
    • Still lost → STAGNARA WAKE-UP TEST→remove implants if spine stable

6Complications

Complication Key facts
Neurologic injury Paraplegia 1:1000; ↑ with kyphosis, excessive correction, sublaminar wires
Pseudarthrosis (1–2%) Late pain, progression, rod breakage; asymptomatic + no loss of correction → observe
Infection (1–2%) Late pain, clean wound; P. acnes commonest delayed organism (2-week culture); I&D + retain hardware if not loose and < 6 months
Flat back Loss of lumbar lordosis → fatigue + back pain; rare with sagittal rod contouring; Rx posterior closing wedge osteotomies (± anterior release)
Crankshaft Continued anterior growth after PSF alone in very young → rotation; prevent with anterior discectomy + fusion
SMA syndrome 3rd part of duodenum compressed between SMA (arises L1) and aorta → bilious vomiting in 1st post-op week, electrolyte disturbance; risk: height < 50th, weight < 25th centile, kyphosis; Rx NG tube + IV fluids

X vs Y

Typical AIS Needs MRI
Curve Right thoracic, left TL/L Left thoracic, short angular
Sagittal Hypokyphosis Apical kyphosis / hyperkyphosis
Progression Slow Rapid
Exam Normal neuro, symmetric reflexes Asymmetric abdominal reflexes, cavovarus, skin signs
Milwaukee (CTLSO) Boston TLSO Charleston
Apex Above T7 T7 or below Any (night)
Wear Full time Full time Night only
Risser Sanders TW-III RUS
Site Iliac apophysis Hand Radius, ulna, MC/phalanges 1, 3, 5
Use Common; PHV during Risser 0 Predicts > 50° despite brace Best correlate of curve acceleration

How it's asked

"Which is NOT an MRI indication in AIS?"left thoracolumbar / lumbar curve (TO)
✗ Wrong options: left thoracic · rapid progression · neuro signs

"Exam finding NOT suggesting non-idiopathic scoliosis?"truncal shift (TO)
✗ Wrong options: hairy patch · pigmented lesions · hyperflexibility

"12-yr girl, left thoracic curve, absent left abdominal reflexes — next step?"MRI
✗ Wrong options: observe · TLSO · PSF

"First-visit evaluation EXCEPT?"MRI (✓ leg lengths, abdominal reflexes, waist asymmetry, café-au-lait)

"Curve magnitude at which time point best predicts progression?"peak height velocity
✗ Wrong options: menarche · Risser 3 · TW stage 2

"PHV correlates closest with?"olecranon apophysis fusion (TO)
✗ Wrong options: triradiate closure · Risser 1 · menarche

"Which Risser grade = greatest velocity of linear growth?"Risser 0

"Which X-ray shows highest progression risk?"Risser 0 with open triradiate cartilage
✗ Wrong options: late Risser 0 with fusing triradiate · Risser 3–5

"Which statement on curve progression is WRONG?"rib phase 1 worse than rib phase 2 (TO — phase 2 progresses rapidly; RVAD > 20° → progression)

"NCS lies between and closes in which order?"centrum + posterior arches; cervical, lumbar, thoracic

"Brace indicated?"premenarchal girl, 30° curve · 28°, Risser 1 · 30° MT, Risser 1
✗ Wrong options: 38° Risser 4 · 52° · 19°

"Minimum wear for 90–93% success?"12–13 h actual wear
✗ Wrong options: 6–8 · 15–16 · 19–20

"Correct statement on bracing (TO)?"Milwaukee for apex above T7
✗ Wrong options: Boston only below T12 · 8 h minimum · success = > 5° correction

"Expected effect of bracing a 32° immature curve?"rigid TLSO 13 h/day → 50% ↓ need for surgery

"Bracing true statement (Sanders 2, 32°)?"NNT = 4

"Full-time TLSO most likely to improve natural history?"26° MT, Sanders 3

"Braced 28° → 11° curve, now Risser 4 — next?"discontinue brace
✗ Wrong options: continue full-time/night · PSF · MRI

"Which curve suits a TLSO (Risser 0)?"30°, apex T12
✗ Wrong options: apex T6 → Milwaukee · 15° · 53°

"Lenke classification includes all EXCEPT?"thoracic modifier (A, B, C) (TO)

"Double thoracic curve = ?"Lenke type 2: structural MT (major) + PT, non-structural TL (TO)

"Lenke 1B, 65° — management?"PSF of thoracic curve only

"AVT 45 mm / 15 mm?"ratio 3.0 → selective thoracic fusion

"LIV relative to touched vertebra?"to or caudad to TV
✗ Wrong options: cephalad → truncal shift

"Anterior single-rod + cages — pseudarthrosis risk?"thoracic hyperkyphosis > 40°

"Commonest complication of PSO?"pseudarthrosis
✗ Wrong options: neuro deficit · aortic injury

"Undertapping 1 mm + straightforward trajectory?"↑ maximal insertional torque

"MEP ↓ 60%, SSEP ↓ 30%, anaesthesia unchanged — immediate step?"reposition patient
✗ Wrong options: deepen anaesthesia · remove screw · wake-up test · abort

"Reduces post-op opioid use?"short-course adjunctive steroids

"Untreated AIS, 38° at maturity — counsel?"↑ lifetime chronic back pain
✗ Wrong options: caesarean · pulmonary decline

"Bilious vomiting day 5 after PSF in a thin girl?"SMA syndrome → NG tube + IV fluids

"Late pain, clean wound, months after PSF?"P. acnes infection (hold cultures 2 weeks)

Adult Isthmic Spondylolisthesis

Spine · 2 / 69

1Basics

Pathogenesis, prevalence & classification

Definitionforward slip of a vertebra on the one below due to a pars interarticularis defect (spondylolysis)

Parsthin bicortical bridge where lamina + inferior articular process meet pedicle + superior articular process; defect = fibrocartilaginous cleft

Prevalencespondylolysis 4–6% of North American adults · 4.4% at age 6 → 6% adult (Beutler)
✗ Wrong options: < 2% · 8–10%

Sexmale (2/3 of defects); slip progression commoner in females

LevelL5-S1 82% (Orthobullets) · 86–90% (OB) · L4-5 11%
✗ Wrong options: thoracolumbar junction

Why L5-S1highest lumbar forces + facet orientation: "more sagittal" (Orthobullets) · "more coronal" (OB) — read the options

Sportsrepetitive hyperextension: gymnasts, weightlifters, football linemen · high incidence in Inuits (genetic)
✗ Wrong options: hyperflexion

Natural historyonly 5% of spondylolysis → spondylolisthesis · progression mostly in adolescence, rare after maturity
✗ Wrong options: adults progress more

Wiltse-Newman classification

Type Name Lesion
I Dysplastic Congenital L5 arch / upper sacrum anomaly
IIA Isthmic – lytic Pars fatigue (stress) fracture — commonest adult
IIB Isthmic Elongated intact pars (healed stress fractures)
IIC Isthmic Acute pars fracture
III Degenerative Facet instability, pars intact (L4-5)
IV Traumatic Acute fracture of arch other than pars (e.g. pedicle)
V Pathologic Local / generalised bone disease

Meyerding grade

Grade Slip Group
I < 25% Low grade
II 25–50% Low grade
III 50–75% High grade
IV 75–100% High grade
V > 100% Spondyloptosis

2Mechanism

Foraminal stenosis (main cause of leg pain)exiting L5 root in L5-S1 foramen compressed by hypertrophic fibrocartilage of pars defect · uncinate spur of posteroinferior L5 body · bulging L5-S1 disc · facet hypertrophy

Lateral recess stenosisfacet arthrosis + hypertrophic ligamentum flavum

Central stenosis = rareposterior arch stays behind as body slips → canal enlarges; only in spondyloptosis
✗ Wrong options: "central stenosis compresses traversing root"

Discbilateral pars defect → ↑ shear across disc → early degeneration → pain

Nutcracker vs shear (TO)low PI + low SS → L5 posterior elements clamped between L4 and S1 in extension = nutcracker (low progression risk) · high PI + high SS → ↑ shear on L5 pars

3Presentation

Axial back paincommonest; long history, episodic

Leg painL5 radiculopathy (foraminal) → EHL + ankle dorsiflexion weakness, dorsum-of-foot dysaesthesia, hip abductor weakness
✗ Wrong options: FHL · quadriceps · gastrocsoleus · iliopsoas

Tight hamstringscommon accompanying sign

Neurogenic claudication / cauda equinarare (slips rarely exceed grade II)

Root logic trapscentral L3-4 / far-lateral L4-5 HNP → L4 · central L5-S1 HNP → S1 (plantar flexion, lateral foot) · isthmic L3-4 → not L5

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
Lateral X-ray Slip + pars defect (diagnosis)
Oblique X-ray Pars defect ("Scottie dog collar")
Flexion-extension Instability = 4 mm translation or 10° angulation vs adjacent segment
AP X-ray Coronal deformity · inverted Napoleon hat sign = marked anterolisthesis L5 on S1 (TO) (✗ Wrong options: retrolisthesis · facet dislocation · sacralisation)
CT Best bony pars defect
MRI – T2 parasagittal Best for foraminal stenosis / neural compression

Pelvic incidence & spinopelvic parameters

Pelvic incidence (PI)angle between perpendicular to S1 endplate at its midpoint and line from S1 endplate midpoint → femoral head centre

PI = PT + SSPT = PI − SS · SS = PI − PT

Parameter Measured Type
Pelvic incidence Perpendicular to S1 endplate vs line to hip axis Morphologic – fixed, same standing/sitting/lying (TO)
Pelvic tilt Vertical through hip axis vs line to S1 midpoint Positional
Sacral slope S1 endplate vs horizontal Positional
Slip angle L5 inferior endplate vs perpendicular to posterior sacral cortex Local L5-S1 kyphosis; > 30° predicts progression
Lumbosacral angle L5 upper endplate vs perpendicular to upper sacrum > 10° predicts progression
Lumbar index Anterior ÷ posterior L5 height L5 wedging

PI valuesnormal 55° ± 10° (TO) · ~47° childhood → ↑ in adolescence → ~57° adult, then constant
✗ Wrong options: "PI greater sitting than standing"

PI correlates withseverity / Meyerding grade of slip (direct linear) · determines SS, PT, lumbar lordosis · correlates with sacral slope (TO)

Pelvic tilt (TO)varies with position · opens at the back · mean 13° ± 6° standing · ↑ PT → sacral plateau becomes more horizontal
✗ Wrong options: "more vertical"

Balanced pelvis (TO)high SS + low PT · unbalanced = low SS + high PT (retroverted pelvis, vertical sacrum) · PT is the better indicator (SS changes secondary to PT)

Slip progression predictorslip angle (Orthobullets, OB — most predictive of progression + surgical outcome) · pelvic incidence (TO answer) — read the options

Global balanceSVA (C7 plumb vs posterosuperior S1); SVA > 50 mm → worse outcome after decompression-fusion

SDSG classification (L5-S1)

Grade Type Defining feature
Low (< 50%) 1 – Nutcracker PI < 45°
Low 2 PI 45–60°
Low 3 – Shear PI > 60° → prone to progress
High (> 50%) 4 Balanced pelvis (high SS, low PT)
High 5 Retroverted pelvis, balanced spine (C7 plumb on/behind hips)
High 6 Retroverted pelvis, unbalanced spine

High-grade slipsmean PI > 60°

5Management

First lineactivity restriction, NSAIDs, PT; bracing in acute phase; injections unclear · acute pars defect after fall in adult → observe + mobilise
✗ Wrong options: cast · TLSO 6–8 wks · ORIF · fusion

Operative criteria

Indication for decompression + fusion
Incapacitating pain failing 6 months non-op (commonest)
Progressive neurologic deficit
Slip progression
Cauda equina syndrome
Isthmic spondylolisthesis failing 6 months non-op
  • Low grade (I–II)→L5-S1 DECOMPRESSION + INSTRUMENTED FUSION
    (Gill laminectomy + foraminal decompression must be fused)
  • High grade (III–V)→L4-S1 DECOMPRESSION + FUSION + ANTERIOR COLUMN SUPPORT
    • Reduction?→PARTIAL (correct slip angle)

Surgery – decompression & fusion; ALIF vs TLIF

Gill laminectomyremoves loose lamina + scarred pars → decompresses root; destabilising → always fuse
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy alone → further slip · decompression alone in high grade = poor

Pars repairnot if > grade I slip

Contraindicatedstand-alone ALIF / stand-alone PLIF (high failure) · total disc replacement (not for isthmic slip)

Recurrent L5 radiculopathy after L5-S1 discectomy in isthmic slipposterior foraminal decompression + instrumented fusion + graft
✗ Wrong options: revision hemilaminotomy · TDA · Gill alone

Best techniquesurgeon preference; outcomes similar
✗ Wrong options: PSF highest fusion · reduction lowest L5 injury · TLIF lowest dural tear (TLIF ↑ root injury + dural tears)

ALIF (+ posterior fixation) TLIF
Approach Retroperitoneal; ALL released Posterior; facetectomy, lateral to dura
Decompression Indirect (disc height → foraminal distraction) Direct visualisation of roots
Disc height / segmental lordosis Greater (✓) Less
Fusion rate Similar (some ↑) Similar
1-yr pain / overall complications Similar Similar
Specific risks Retrograde ejaculation (superior hypogastric plexus), retroperitoneal haematoma, persistent radiculopathy, pars-defect pain fibres left behind Dural tear, root injury
Use Low-grade slips, even with radicular pain (87–94% good at 2 yrs) · not high grade Any

PLIF vs TLIFPLIF cage medial to facets · TLIF = facetectomy, more lateral · interbody ↑ time, blood loss, stay

Reduction – L5 nerve root injury

Root at risk with reductionL5 (exits under L5 pedicle; S1 traverses) → post-op EHL / great-toe extension weakness
✗ Wrong options: S1 plantar flexion · L2–3 hip flexion · L4 patellar reflex · genitofemoral (anterior approach) · cauda equina

Strain biomechanics71% of L5 strain in the second half of complete reduction (non-linear after > 50%) → partial reduction · ↑ disc height and ↑ lordosis protect L5

Reduction goalcorrect slip angle / lumbosacral kyphosis first; slip % second

High-grade slip (SDSG)
  • Balanced pelvis (type 4)→IN-SITU FUSION
  • Retroverted pelvis, spine clinically balanced (type 5)→PARTIAL REDUCTION + FUSION
  • Retroverted pelvis + sagittal imbalance / stooping (type 6)→REDUCTION MANDATORY (360°)

Complication Note

Pseudarthrosis —
Dural tear ↑ with TLIF

Retrograde ejaculation ALIF

Adult Pyogenic Vertebral Osteomyelitis

Spine · 3 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology – lumbar spine most common

Spectrum (spondylodiscitis)discitis · vertebral osteomyelitis · epidural abscess

Agemedian 50–60 yrs

Region Share
Lumbar 50–60%
Thoracic 30–40%
Cervical ~10% (Orthobullets) · 20% (OB)
Sacrum Rare

Orderlumbar > thoracic > cervical > sacrum
✗ Wrong options: lumbar = thoracic · cervical first

Risk factorsIV drug use · diabetes · recent systemic infection (UTI, pneumonia) · malignancy · immunosuppression · obesity · malnutrition (albumin < 3 g/dL) · trauma · smoking

Organisms

Organism Setting
Staph aureus Commonest (50–65%)
Staph epidermidis 2nd commonest
Gram-negatives ↑ last decade; GU + respiratory sources
Pseudomonas IV drug users
Salmonella Sickle cell disease
Fungal · TB Immunocompromised

Commonest fungal infection of spine (TO)Aspergillus; Candida + Aspergillus = commensals entering via IV lines/implants/surgery · ↑ during COVID (steroid overuse) · Coccidioides + Blastomyces = geographic
✗ Wrong options: Candida · Cryptococcus · Blastomyces

2Mechanism

Haematogenous seeding (commonest)arterial or venous → endplate (low-flow vascular anastomosis) → disc → adjacent endplate

Direct inoculationpenetrating trauma, open fracture, surgery

Contiguous spreadretropharyngeal + retroperitoneal abscess

Neurologic deficit (10–20%)direct neural infection · epidural abscess compression · instability

Epidural abscesspus/granulation between dura and epidural fat · in ~18% of spondylodiscitis · 50% of abscess patients have neuro symptoms

Psoas abscessassociated

3Presentation

Painsevere, insidious, unrelenting, worse with activity; night pain → infection / malignancy

Feveronly 33%

HistoryUTI, pneumonia, skin infection, transplant

Neuroradiculopathy / myelopathy in 10–20% → careful neuro exam

4Diagnosis & Classification

Genes & lab markers

Test Fact
WBC ↑ in only ~50% → insensitive
ESR ↑ in 90%; serial monitoring, less reliable than CRP
CRP ↑ in 90%; best for tracking treatment response
Blood cultures All patients before antibiotics; least invasive; positive ~33% (25–66%); 85% accurate when positive; ↑ yield off antibiotics + during fever
CT-guided biopsy If blood cultures negative + no urgent surgery; diagnostic 68–86% (Orthobullets) · needle ~70%, open > 80% (OB); send aerobic, anaerobic, fungal, AFB
Open biopsy Non-invasive fails; anterior, costotransversectomy, transpedicular

Post-discectomy: severe pain + spasm, no fever, normal TLC + ESR, CRP very high (TO)infective discitis
✗ Wrong options: instability · pseudomeningocele · residual disc

Key radiology findings

Modality Findings
X-ray Delayed by weeks · loss of psoas shadow (first days) · disc space narrowing at 7–10 days · endplate erosion/sclerosis at 10–21 days · local osteopenia
CT Bony destruction, abscess extent
MRI with gadolinium Gold standard; most sensitive (96%) + specific (93%); best to separate infection from tumour; early scan may mimic degeneration → repeat
Tc-99m bone scan If MRI impossible; 90% sensitive, not specific; + gallium-67 → ↑ sensitivity + specificity
Indium-111 Poor sensitivity (17%)

Classic discitis MRI triadT2 bright disc (fluid/pus) · T1 dark, disc–body margin lost · T1 fat-sat + gad: rim enhancement of abscess + adjacent bodies → pathognomonic
✗ Wrong options: lymphoma · primary epidural abscess · DDD · early AS

MRI limitless useful than labs for treatment response

Infection vs tumourinfection = disc involved, endplate erosion, marked inflammation · tumour spares disc

Discitis vs Modic IModic I: T1 dark, T2 bright marrow but disc desiccated (normal / dark T2), no endplate erosion, no soft-tissue mass, normal ESR/CRP

Pyogenic vs TB on MRI (TO)collection with relatively preserved disc → pyogenic (acute, presents early) · collapsed disc, subligamentous spread → TB (late presentation) · end result of spinal TB = bony ankylosis (weight-bearing + cancellous surfaces)
✗ Wrong options: fibrous ankylosis as in limb joints

5Management

Suspected spondylodiscitis
  • Septic / critically ill→CULTURES (± CT aspirate) THEN BROAD-SPECTRUM IV
    (vancomycin + 3rd-gen cephalosporin)
  • Stable, neuro intact→MRI + BLOOD CULTURES first
    • Organism found→ORGANISM-SPECIFIC IV ABX
    • Cultures negative→CT-GUIDED BIOPSY
  • Deficit / instability / abscess / cervical→DEBRIDE + STABILISE

Diagnosis – MRI, blood cultures & CT-guided biopsy

Organism ID is mandatory before targeted therapy · stable patient → hold antibiotics until cultures/biopsy
✗ Wrong options: empirical IV antibiotics · INH-RIF-PZA before biopsy in suspected TB

IVDU with UTI, back pain, ↑ ESRadmit + blood cultures + MRI with/without gad
✗ Wrong options: oral abx · bone scan · renal US

Blood cultures negative, neuro intactCT-guided closed biopsy
✗ Wrong options: IV abx · open surgery · repeat MRI

MSSA in blood culturesorganism-specific IV antibiotics
✗ Wrong options: CT biopsy · debridement

Treatment – organism-specific antibiotics

Course6–12 weeks ± bracing · IV until improvement (~4–6 wks) → oral · success 80%

Bracingpain + deformity prevention · cervical → rigid cervicothoracic orthosis or halo

Resistant organismsMRSA, VRSA, VRE → linezolid, daptomycin

Infection Agent
Gram-positive Cefazolin, vancomycin
Gram-negative Fluoroquinolone (ciprofloxacin), 3rd-gen cephalosporin
Fungal Amphotericin B first line (± azoles)
TB INH + rifampicin + PZA + ethambutol

Operative criteria

Debridement + stabilisation indicated
Cervical osteomyelitis / cervical epidural abscess
Progressive neurologic deficit
Progressive deformity / gross instability
Refractory to medical therapy
Large abscess

Open biopsy alonecultures + CT biopsy fail; lumbar disease without canal abscess

Surgical technique

Gold standardanterior debridement + strut graft ± posterior instrumentation · posterior debridement alone usually inadequate

Graftautograft (tricortical iliac crest, rib, fibula) safe in acute infection · allograft OK · titanium mesh cage + autograft + posterior instrumentation → best deformity correction

Instrumentation in active infectioncontroversial (staged vs single stage) · titanium > stainless steel

Posterior instrumentationsevere kyphosis or multilevel anterior construct

Two-level cervical osteomyelitis + rapidly progressive gait loss + ↑ WBC/CRP/ESRanterior corpectomy + posterior instrumentation (A + P) · cervical abscess → early surgery (little space for cord) · lumbar abscess → often antibiotics alone

X vs Y

Pyogenic Tuberculous
Onset Acute, painful, early Insidious, late
Disc (TO) Relatively preserved at presentation Collapsed / destroyed
Spread Endplate → disc → endplate Subligamentous, skip lesions
End result — Bony ankylosis
Infection Tumour
Disc Involved Spared
Endplate Eroded Preserved
Best test MRI + gad MRI

Adult Spinal Deformity

Spine · 4 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology & types

Demographicsmean age 60; M = F

Coronal imbalancelateral deviation of vertical line > 10°

Sagittal imbalanceradiographic > 5 cm (C7 plumb vs posterosuperior S1)

Degenerative pathoanatomyasymmetric degeneration of discs ± facets

Loss of sagittal balanceosteoporosis · pre-existing scoliosis · iatrogenic instability · DDD

Idiopathic (residual) Degenerative (de novo)
Origin Untreated AIS Degeneration, iatrogenic, paralysis
Curve pattern Classic patterns Lacks classic pattern
Segments More Fewer
Location Thoracic Lumbar
Magnitude Larger Smaller

Natural history & progression

Progression by curvethoracic > lumbar > thoracolumbar > double major · right thoracic 1°/yr · right lumbar 0.5°/yr · TL 0.25°/yr

By magnitude< 30° rarely progress · > 50° commonly progress

Other risksintercrestal line below L4-5 · pre-existing rotation

Symptoms progress toside of curve convexity

3Presentation

Low back paincommonest (40–90%); spondylosis, instability, discogenic; more severe/recurrent than general population

Neurogenic claudicationstenosis on concave side; no relief with sitting / flexion (unlike classic claudication)

Radicular painforaminal + lateral recess stenosis, worse in concavity (rotation + translation)

Examthoracic prominence on forward bend, compensatory posture, weakness

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
36-inch standing AP + lateral + R/L bending Cobb, coronal (C7PL vs CSVL) + sagittal balance, flexibility
CT Bony deformity, facet arthrosis
CT myelogram Best for stenosis + bony anatomy when rotation makes MRI hard
MRI When leg pain present: stenosis, facet hypertrophy, foraminal encroachment
DEXA Bone density for planning

Sagittal balance – SVA, PI-LL mismatch, pelvic parameters

Sagittal vertical axis (SVA)horizontal distance C7 plumb line → posterosuperior corner of S1; neutral = through it · positive = anterior · negative = posterior

Strongest predictor of symptoms / HRQOL / PROMs / poor surgical resultsagittal balance (C7 SVA)
✗ Wrong options: coronal imbalance · Cobb · levels · apex level · residual scoliosis > 25° · L5-S1 disc left out · truncal shift · PI

Radiographic parameter correlating with pain + disability (VAS, ODI)thoracolumbar kyphosis (✗ Wrong options: SS · TK · PT · PI) · TL/lumbar curves also correlate (✗ Wrong options: thoracic scoliosis · thoracic hypokyphosis · lumbar hyperlordosis · disc degeneration)

Pelvic incidenceonly permanent anatomic parameter; PI = PT + SS; not posture dependent (TO); stabilises after adolescence; determines lordosis needed

Compensation for positive SVApelvic retroversion (↑ PT, ↓ SS) · thoracic hypokyphosis · cervical hyperlordosis · knee flexion → SVA may look normal (< 5 cm) → look for regional imbalance

T1 pelvic angle (T1PA)angle T1 centre → femoral head → S1 endplate centre = T1 spinal inclination + PT; accounts for spinal inclination + pelvic retroversion; no calibration needed; usable intra-op · goal < 14° · > 20° ≈ ODI 40 (severe) · ↑ T1PA = ↑ ODI

Diagnostic criteria – surgical alignment targets

Target Value
SVA < 5 cm (best reliable improvement correcting to +3 cm)
PI − LL LL = PI ± 9° (PI − LL < 10°) (✗ Wrong options: within 15° · LL = PI + 25° · LL = TK + 15°)
Pelvic tilt < 20° (OB) · < 25° (alternate goal)
T1PA < 14°
TK + LL + PI ≤ 45° — most predictive of maintaining correction
GAP score < 3 → fewer mechanical failures / revisions

Normal lumbar lordosis40–60° (~70% from L4-S1; apex L3-4 / L4)

5Management

Non-opcoronal curves < 30° rarely progress · NSAIDs · TCAs for sleep · PT core strengthening · injections / root blocks (diagnostic + therapeutic) · bracing (comfort)

Operative criteria

Indication for instrumented fusion
Curve > 50°
Sagittal imbalance
Curve progression
Intractable back / radicular pain failing non-op
Cardiopulmonary decline: thoracic > 60° → PFTs ↓; > 90° → mortality ↑
Cosmesis (controversial)
ASD surgical approach
  • Thoracic / double structural > 50°→POSTERIOR-ONLY FUSION
  • Isolated TL / lumbar, > 70°, rigid, L5-S1 interbody→ANTERIOR RELEASE + POSTERIOR

Goalsrestore spinal balance · relieve pain · solid fusion

Worse outcomesbaseline depression · obesity · ≥ 4 comorbidities · insufficient sagittal correction · pseudarthrosis
✗ Wrong options: ethnicity · pre-op ODI · pre-op SVA if corrected

Fusion level selection

Proximalextend to a neutral + horizontal vertebra above main curve

Distal level Indication / outcome
L5 Only if no L5-S1 pathology; lowest future L5-S1 degeneration with normal C7 plumb + normal sacral inclination
S1 Any L5-S1 pathology: spondylolisthesis, spondylolysis, facet arthrosis, prior laminectomy · ✓ better correction + maintenance of sagittal balance · (✗ Wrong options: ↑ pseudarthrosis, OR time, reoperation, sacral insufficiency fracture, altered gait)
Ilium (sacropelvic) Sacrum in fusion of > 3 levels; iliac screws/bolts → ↑ fusion success at L5-S1 + ↓ sacral fractures in osteopenic; prominent hardware

Maximise fusion rateilium + sacrum both in distal fixation
✗ Wrong options: ilium alone · sacrum alone · coronal balance

Pubic ramus fracturesoccur with sacral and iliac fixation

Osteotomies – correction per level

Osteotomy Correction Indication / prerequisite
Smith-Petersen (SPO) / Ponte ~10° per level (Orthobullets) · posterior column osteotomy 3–5° (OB) Mild–moderate imbalance; needs mobile disc + disc height, no anterior fusion; more in lumbar
Pedicle subtraction (PSO) 30–35° lumbar, 25° thoracic (Orthobullets) · 25–35° (OB) SVA > 12 cm (Orthobullets) · > 10 cm (TO); anterior fusion present (correction through body)
Vertebral column resection (VCR) Up to / > 45° (avg 62° coronal, 45° sagittal) Rigid sharp angular kyphosis (tumour, fracture, infection), rigid scoliosis, congenital kyphosis, hemivertebra
TLIF ~8° —
TLIF + ALL release + hyperlordotic cage 10–15° (22° with PCOs) —

3 SPO ≈ 1 PSOPSO more blood loss · neuromonitoring preferred

PSO levelL3 / L4 (apex of lordosis L3-4; 70% of LL from L4-S1)
✗ Wrong options: L1 → non-anatomic · SPOs above apex · thoracic SPOs ↓ TK

Flat back after prior lateral interbody / L3-S1 fusionL3 PSO + extension to pelvis
✗ Wrong options: SPOs through fused discs · T12 PSO · VCR

Bone health optimisation

Osteoporosis (T < −2.5) or ≥ 2 vertebral compression fractures (= osteoporosis without DEXA)teriparatide before surgery (≥ 6 months → ↓ pseudarthrosis + reoperation)
✗ Wrong options: continue/start alendronate (impairs fusion) · vit D/Ca alone · denosumab switch · repeat DEXA

Osteopenia (T −1.7)no anabolic referral needed

Cement (PMMA) augmentation of end vertebrae↑ fusion rate + ↓ junctional failure + ↑ pullout strength, no extra complications · osteoporotic spine: ≥ 3 fixation points above + below
✗ Wrong options: fewer levels fused

Active smokersmoking cessation clinic + nicotine testing, postpone elective fusion
✗ Wrong options: schedule now

Smoking↑ pseudarthrosis, infection, implant loosening, fracture risk; cessation programmes improve outcomes (EXCEPT answer: "cessation doesn't help" is FALSE)

Perioperative considerations

Neuromonitoring (SSEP ↓ 60% at correction)stop instrumenting + raise MAP ≥ 85 mmHg, recheck, then reverse correction
✗ Wrong options: wake-up test first · MAP 60 · steroids first · inhalational only (reduce inhalational agents)

Neuro deficit on monitoring after correctionremove instrumentation, consider wake-up test

Jehovah's Witnessclosed-circuit cell saver
✗ Wrong options: whole blood, PRBC, plasma, platelets

Open thoracotomy for anterior release (adult)pulmonary function ↓ and often stays ↓ long term

Pedicle anatomycortical:cancellous ratio thoracic pedicles > lumbar pedicles > vertebral bodies > sacrum (implant failure likeliest in sacrum) · lumbosacral pedicle diameter + medial angulation ↑ caudally (L3 more medial than L1; S1 > L1) · L5 root on sacral ala (at risk with S-I screws) · S1 screw start inferior + lateral to L5 inferior articular process tip

Posterior instrumentation system (TO)Cotrel-Dubousset (rods + hooks/screws, posterior)
✗ Wrong options: Dwyer · Zielke (anterior) · Boston brace

Ankylosing Spondylitis

Spine · 5 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitionchronic seronegative autoimmune spondyloarthropathy: bridging syndesmophytes, enthesitis, sacroiliitis, uveitis

Incidence~0.2% of Caucasians

Sex / age4:1 male · presents 3rd decade · juvenile form < 16 yrs
✗ Wrong options: more common in females

HLA-B27 carriers< 10% of B27-positive people develop AS · B27 in ~6% of white population

Pathoanatomy

Enthesitisinflammation at tendon/ligament insertion → erosion → soft-tissue ossification → ankylosis; targets SI joints, spinal apophyseal joints, pubic symphysis
✗ Wrong options: synovial process = RA

Discannulus inflammation → syndesmophytes

Theoriesautoimmune reaction to environmental pathogen in a genetically susceptible host; B27 peptide aggregation; cytotoxic T-cells against B27

SpreadSI fusion first → cephalad spinal progression → often entire spine
✗ Wrong options: "only pelvis + spine": large joints too

Genes & lab markers

Marker Fact
HLA-B27 Class I MHC antigen, B locus, chromosome 6; positive ~90% (Orthobullets, TO) · ~95% (OB)
B27 prevalence (TO) AS 90% · juvenile SpA 70% · RA 6%
RF Negative (seronegative)
ESR / CRP Non-specific mild ↑ in active disease (✗ Wrong options: normal)
✗ Traps COL1A1 = OI · CD20 = lymphoma · EXT1 = MHE · HLA-B8 = DISH + diabetes · HLA-DR3 = SLE · urine phosphoethanolamine = hypophosphatasia

Inheritancegenetic predisposition, mode unknown

Systemic manifestations

System Manifestation
Eye Acute anterior uveitis / iritis — commonest extra-articular; confirms diagnosis with B27 + sacroiliitis
Heart Conduction abnormalities
Aorta Aortitis, stenosis, regurgitation
Lung Pulmonary fibrosis (↓ FEV), costovertebral fusion → ↓ chest expansion
Kidney Amyloidosis
Synovitis Klebsiella pneumoniae (gram-negative bacillus) — B27-positive more susceptible (✗ Wrong options: Strep pneumoniae · gonococcus · C. acnes · Staph aureus)
Other IBD, psoriasis

Orthopaedicbilateral sacroiliitis · progressive kyphosis · cervical fractures · large-joint arthritis (hip + shoulder, asymmetric)

3Presentation

Lumbosacral pain + stiffnessinsidious, 3rd decade, worse in morning, improves with exercise

Neck / upper thoracic painlater; acute neck pain = fracture until proven otherwise

Othersciatica (piriformis), loss of horizontal gaze, dyspnoea (costovertebral)

Exam test Detects
Chest expansion < 2 cm More specific than HLA-B27
Schober test Lumbar stiffness
Wall (Fleche) test (TO) Cervical spine involvement in AS — occiput can't touch wall
FABER (Patrick) SI joint pain (✗ Wrong options: FADDIR = FAI)
Chin-brow to vertical angle (CBVA) Chin-on-chest deformity; standing lateral; osteotomy planning — correction ∝ outcome
Supine + sitting exam Separates hip flexion contracture from spinal kyphosis

Juvenile ASsacroiliitis, spondylitis, enthesitis (more than adult), kyphosis, stiffness, peripheral LL arthritis, uveitis · not urethritis (= reactive arthritis triad)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria

Orthobulletsbilateral sacroiliitis ± uveitis + HLA-B27

Modified New York criteria (TO)HLA-B27 is NOT a criterion

Clinical criteria Radiological criterion
LBP > 3 months, better with exercise, not relieved by rest Bilateral sacroiliitis grade 2–4 or unilateral grade 3–4
↓ lumbar motion in sagittal + frontal planes —
↓ chest expansion for age/sex —

Definite ASradiological + ≥ 1 clinical · Probable — 3 clinical alone, or radiological alone

SI joint injectionoften the most sensitive diagnostic test

Key radiology findings

Modality Finding
X-ray spine Squaring of vertebrae · marginal (vertical) syndesmophytes · bamboo spine (late) · ALL/PLL calcification · osteoporosis · dagger sign (AP: central line = ossified supra/interspinous ligaments)
X-ray pelvis – Ferguson view Beam 10–15° cephalad → anterior SI joint
SI joint Bilateral symmetric erosions; earliest = iliac side erosion → narrowing → ankylosis
CT Bony change, not inflammation; most sensitive for cervical fracture
MRI (STIR / gad) Detects inflammation → earliest diagnosis; Romanus lesion = "shiny corner" erosions at vertebral corners (TO: MRI of LS spine) · Andersson lesion = spondylodiscitis / pseudarthrosis · active sacroiliitis → dynamic fat-sat MRI
Bone scan SI uptake, not specific

Fractures on X-raynegative in 50% → CT

5Management

First lineNSAIDs / COX-2 + PT (flexibility) · no oral steroids (oral or IV — no effect on progression)

Second lineTNF-α inhibitors: infliximab, etanercept, adalimumab (also approved for RA + PsA → "husband AS + wife RA on same drug" = adalimumab)
✗ Wrong options: denosumab · tofacitinib (RA) · imatinib · rituximab (RA)

Early AS with fused SIJsNSAIDs + PT + HLA-B27 test
✗ Wrong options: SI injections · SI fusion · aspiration

Spine fractures – image whole spine, immobilise in existing posture

Epidemiologyrisk 4× general population · mid-cervical + cervicothoracic (C5–7) commonest (> 50%), then TL junction
✗ Wrong options: lumbar commonest

Patternextension, 3-column, behaves like a long-bone fracture (long lever arm + osteoporosis)

Outcomesneuro involvement ~75% (67% AS vs 40% DISH) · occult multi-level fractures 13% · diagnostic delay up to 40% · mortality up to 32–34% · complications up to 84% (pneumonia, respiratory failure, pseudarthrosis) · fusion rates high once fixed

AS patient + neck/back pain after trivial trauma
  • Immobilise in EXISTING KYPHOTIC (flexed) posture→HARD COLLAR, ADMIT
    (no neutral/extension, soft collar, backboard straightening or flex-ex films)
  • CT OF ENTIRE SPINE (X-rays miss 50%)
  • New / progressive deficit → MRI→EPIDURAL HAEMATOMA
    • Compressive haematoma→POSTERIOR DECOMPRESSION + LONG INSTRUMENTED FUSION

Neuro deficits often delayedadmit + serial exams even if CT negative

Epidural haematoma & neurological deterioration

Incidenceup to 15%; hypervascular epidural tissue + torn epidural veins in a rigid canal; high mortality

Delayed weakness hours after fall (CT negative or nondisplaced)epidural haemorrhage
✗ Wrong options: further displacement once immobilised · degenerative stenosis · vertebral artery · myelomalacia · cord infarct

Dorsal epidural haematoma + progressive deficitposterior laminectomy + instrumented fusion
✗ Wrong options: steroids · repeat MRI · anterior fusion · laminectomy alone · halo (↑ mortality)

Fracture fixation – long-segment posterior instrumentation

Operative indicationsprogressive deficit · epidural haematoma with compromise · unstable fracture (virtually all, even minimally displaced, neuro intact)

Constructlong posterior, ≥ 3 levels above + 3 below, multiple fixation points (osteoporosis + lever arm) · do NOT correct deformity · 360° if very osteoporotic / incomplete SCI with unstable C6 · (✗ Wrong options: short-segment · uninstrumented fusion · halo (septic + pulmonary issues in elderly))
✗ Wrong options: stand-alone anterior → high failure

Non-opstable, no deficit, or unfit: immobilise in existing kyphosis, admit; low-weight traction may reduce

Complicationsprogressive deformity, nonunion, hardware failure, infection

Kyphotic deformity correction

Exclude hip flexion contracture first · goal = sagittal balance + horizontal gaze

Osteotomy Key facts
Smith-Petersen (SPO) Posterior column (pars + facets), pivot = posterior annulus; needs mobile anterior disc; 10° per 10 mm resected (TO); simplest (TO); C7 plumb 6–8 cm positive; ↓ foraminal height
Ponte Modification of SPO; wider facet + lamina resection; originally thoracic (SPO originally lumbar, AS flat back) — Schwab grade 2 vs SPO grade 1 (TO)
Pedicle subtraction (PSO) Procedure of choice for TL kyphosis; closing wedge, hinge anterior cortex; 30–40° per level; single level, no disc resection; direct bony apposition → better fusion; works with fused discs / 360° fusion; SVA > 4 cm (> 12 cm ideal); transient deficits up to 20%
Opening wedge (single) Hinges on posterior body edge; splits ALL
Multi-segment opening Less bone loss, preserves ALL
Bone-disc-bone 35–60°; hard in AS (ossified annulus)
Vertebral column resection Most correction; requires disc resection
C7-T1 cervicothoracic extension osteotomy Chin-on-chest deformity; posterior extension osteotomy + posterior instrumented fusion (✗ Wrong options: anterior osteotomy (no access to C7 pedicle) · halo traction · in-situ fusion)

Why lumbar osteotomybelow conus, wide canal, avoids thoracic cage, long lever arm → more correction; upper lumbar level · spinal motion not increased (TO)

Why C7-T1vertebral artery outside transverse foramen at C7, wide canal · remove C7 lateral mass + parts of C7/T1 pedicles · aim slight undercorrection (CBVA ~10°) · halo post-op if poor bone · sitting position → venous air embolus; paradoxical embolus risk ↑ with patent foramen ovale
✗ Wrong options: N₂O · hypotension · obesity · emphysema

THA before spinal osteotomy (TO)THA carries less risk; operate both hips together if significant flexion contractures
✗ Wrong options: "cervicothoracic osteotomy before THA"

Hip arthroplasty in AS

Acetabulamore vertical + anteverted; altered spinopelvic relationship → ↑ dislocation, especially anterior (posterior approach still → anterior dislocation risk)
✗ Wrong options: infection · LLD · sciatic palsy (dysplasia) · abductor lurch (lateral approach)

Bilateral THAkyphosis due to hip flexion contractures

Reactive arthritis – pathogenesis & treatment

Definition (TO)HLA-B27-associated seronegative SpA after GU (Chlamydia trachomatis 40–60%) or enteric (Salmonella, Shigella, Yersinia, Campylobacter) infection; onset 1–6 wks (2–4) later; M:F 5–10:1

Featuresasymmetric lower-limb oligoarthritis, dactylitis, enthesitis (Achilles, plantar fascia), asymmetric sacroiliitis, conjunctivitis, anterior uveitis, circinate balanitis, keratoderma blennorrhagicum (soles), urethritis; full Reiter triad in only 1/3 · HLA-B27 60–80%

Pathogenesis (TO)local immune response to persistent, metabolically active, non-culturable bacteria / antigens in synovium; synovial fluid WBC 10–50k, culture negative
✗ Wrong options: "completely sterile"

Reactive arthritis treatment (TO)
  • Treat trigger→DOXYCYCLINE (+ treat partners)
  • Arthritis→FULL-DOSE NSAIDs ± intra-articular steroid
  • Persistent > 3–6 months despite NSAIDs→SULFASALAZINE (first csDMARD)
    • Refractory / axial / enthesitis→TNF INHIBITOR

Atlantoaxial Instability

Spine · 6 / 69

1Basics

Aetiology

C1-2"transitional zone" → instability from degenerative + traumatic causes

Degenerative / congenital Traumatic / infective
Adult Down syndrome · rheumatoid arthritis · os odontoideum Type I odontoid fracture (very rare) · atlas fractures · transverse ligament injury
Paediatric JRA · Morquio (lysosomal storage) Rotatory atlantoaxial subluxation · Grisel syndrome

Os odontoideum = ADULT cause (TO)not paediatric (EXCEPT answer) · aetiology debated, most support post-traumatic

Other causes (TO)skeletal dysplasias (pseudoachondroplasia – COMP), neurofibromatosis, Grisel (infection)

Pseudoachondroplasia vs achondroplasiapseudoachondroplasia → atlantoaxial instability · achondroplasia → lumbar stenosis

Anatomy & ligaments

Ligament Connects / role
Transverse atlantal ligament (TAL) Horizontal limb of cruciate; medial tubercles of C1; straps dens to anterior arch; most important stabiliser — strongest ligament of the spine
Alar ligaments Paired parasagittal; dens → occipital condyles (+ C1 lateral mass); "check" ligaments → limit rotation
Apical ligament Single midline; dens tip → foramen magnum; little strength
Tectorial membrane Cephalad continuation of PLL; dens/C2 → foramen magnum
Anterior / posterior atlanto-occipital membranes ALL continuation / posterior FM → posterior C1 arch

BiomechanicsC1-2 provides ~50% (Orthobullets) · 60% (TO) of cervical rotation; peg-in-ring; near-horizontal facets

3Presentation

Symptomsneck pain, neuro symptoms

Deficits appear latecapacious canal at C1 (Steel's rule of thirds)

Myelopathyhyperreflexia, weakness, broad-based gait, ↓ hand dexterity, loss of motor milestones, bladder dysfunction

Child, trivial fall → transient quadriparesis, recovered (TO)congenital atlantoaxial instability
✗ Wrong options: ossified LF (age) · syrinx (wouldn't recover) · bifid atlas

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Measure Definition Threshold
Atlantodens interval (ADI) Dens → posterior border of anterior C1 arch, flexion-extension Adult > 3.5 mm unstable (Orthobullets) · > 3 mm (TO) · child > 5 mm · > 10 mm → surgery in RA
Space available for cord (SAC / PADI) Posterior dens → anterior posterior arch Normal 17–29 mm · < 14 mm → ↑ neuro risk, surgery in RA
Sum of lateral mass displacement (open mouth) C1 masses over C2 > 8.1 mm → TAL rupture, unstable (Orthobullets) · > 6.9 mm Rule of Spence (TO)

Lateral masses can displace only ifC1 ring fractured or TAL ruptured

Flexion-extension viewsmandatory pre-op in all high-risk patients (Down, RA, dysplasia) before any surgery

Pseudoachondroplasia + myelopathyflexion-extension C-spine; ADI difference 6 mm
✗ Wrong options: lateral mass 9 mm = atlas fracture · hip OA · scoliosis · lumbar stenosis

CTessential for rotated C1 on C2 · MRI for TAL

Fielding & Hawkins classification (TO)

Type Displacement TAL Treatment
I Simple rotatory, no anterior shift Intact Collar 6–8 wks
II Anterior 3–5 mm, one lateral mass pivots Deficient Collar / surgery
III Anterior > 5 mm Disrupted C1-2 fusion (unstable)
IV Posterior displacement (dens erosion) — C1-2 fusion (unstable)

5Management

Determined by specific condition (RA, Down, os odontoideum, fracture, TAL injury, JRA, Morquio, AARS)

Operative criteria (TO)

Adults Children (≥ 1 of)
ADI > 5 mm Neurologic deficit
Moderate displacement / instability on dynamic films Persistent ADI > 4 mm
RA: ADI > 10 mm or SAC < 14 mm Deformity > 3 months
— Recurrence despite 6 weeks immobilisation

C1-2 instability – os odontoideum

Os odontoideum
  • Asymptomatic, stable→STOP CONTACT SPORTS
  • Neuro signs or widened ADI→POSTERIOR C1-2 FUSION
    (not anterior C1-2 fusion — osseous anatomy)

Grisel syndrome

Definition (TO)inflammatory atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation after upper respiratory / head-neck infection (or tonsillectomy, adenoid inflammation); hyperaemia damages facet capsules + TAL

Presentation (TO)child, painful torticollis ("cock-robin": chin rotated one way, neck tilted the other, slight flexion), facial asymmetry, change in voice / nasal resonance, painful attempted correction
✗ Wrong options: muscular torticollis (painless, infant) · Sandifer (reflux)

Grisel type Treatment (TO)
I Soft collar
II Hard collar
III Halo
IV Open fixation (neuro complications)

Trigger URTI / tonsillectomy Birth (intrauterine positioning)

"Single midline CVJ ligament?"apical ligament

Atlantoaxial Rotatory Displacement (AARD)

Spine · 7 / 69

1Basics

Aetiology

Definitionpaediatric C1-2 rotatory instability: spectrum from mild subluxation / muscle spasm → fixed facet dislocation (mechanical block)

Cause Share
Infection (pharyngitis, otitis media, URTI, retropharyngeal abscess) ~35% — Grisel's disease; lymphatic oedema around C-spine
Trauma (even trivial — fall from bed) ~24%
Recent head / neck surgery (tonsillectomy) ~20%
Idiopathic —
Associated Down syndrome, RA, tumours, congenital anomalies

Mechanismligamentous laxity

TAL intactcanal stenosis only with severe rotation + facet dislocation

TAL ruptured + anterolisthesis > 5 mmstenosis with less rotation (45°); vertebral arteries at risk

Anatomy

C2 ossification5 centres · subdental (basilar) synchondrosis between dens + body fuses ~6 yrs · secondary (ossiculum terminale) centre appears ~3 yrs, fuses ~12 yrs

Ligamentstransverse limits anterior translation of atlas · apical + alar limit rotation

3Presentation

Symptomshead tilt, neck pain, headache, ↓ rotation

Posture"cock-robin": chin rotated away from the subluxed facet, head tilted toward it (≈ 20° tilt, 20° rotation, 10° flexion)

SCM spasmon the SAME side as the chin (protective, secondary) — e.g. right facet subluxation → chin left → left SCM spastic

5-yr after tonsillectomyright neck bend, left head turn, SCM spasm → AARS

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
AP open-mouth Asymmetric size + distance from midline of lateral masses
Lateral Facet appears anterior + wedge-shaped (not oval)
Flexion-extension Exclude instability (hard to obtain)
Dynamic CT Gold standard — neutral + maximal right + left rotation → fixed C1-on-C2 rotation unchanged with turning
MRI Little value unless neuro signs

Diagnosis is primarily clinical (OB) · CT/MRI for persistent cases · labs (CBC, ESR, CRP) for infection

Fielding (& Hawkins) classification

Type Lesion TAL Note
I Unilateral facet subluxation; odontoid = pivot (one facet forward, one back) Intact Commonest, benign
II Unilateral, 3–5 mm anterior; one facet = pivot Injured —
III Bilateral anterior > 5 mm Disrupted Rare; neuro risk / sudden death
IV Posterior C1 displacement (odontoid fracture / hypoplastic dens) — Rare; neuro risk / sudden death

5Management

Chronicity is the key factorearlier treatment → better conservative success; many reduce spontaneously before presentation

AARD by duration (Orthobullets)
  • < 1 week→SOFT COLLAR + NSAIDs + exercises
  • > 2 weeks / failed collar × 2 wks → HALTER TRACTION (~5 lb) + BENZODIAZEPINES→hard collar 3 months
  • > 1 month / failed halter × 2 wks → HALO TRACTION→halo vest 3 months
  • > 3 months / neuro deficit / failed halo × 2 wks / recurrent→POSTERIOR C1-2 FUSION

Timing variants (OB)soft collar < 7 days · halter 1 wk–1 month · halo 1–3 months · or sequential 2-week trials (collar → halter → skeletal traction → arthrodesis) — at least 2 weeks of collar before traction

Early management – collar, NSAIDs & traction

Acute (3–5 days, post-URTI / tonsillectomy)soft collar + NSAIDs + antispasmodic
✗ Wrong options: halter · halo · fusion · observation alone

1 week of collar without resolutioncontinue collar ≥ 1 more week then reassess
✗ Wrong options: immediate halter · stretching

Fixed torticollis 4 weeks after fallcervical (halter) traction · halo needed when child's skin can't tolerate halter weight
✗ Wrong options: soft collar · SCM release · fusion

Chronic / irreducible – C1-2 arthrodesis

Failed halter + halo traction, still subluxed on dynamic CTposterior atlantoaxial (C1-2) fusion
✗ Wrong options: no treatment · closed reduction under sedation/GA · occipitocervical fusion (loses motion) · more sedation · more weight · halo vest/C-collar unless reduced

Halo vestonly to maintain a reduction achieved by halo traction

6Complications

Missed / delayed diagnosiscommonest problem

Type III/IVneuro injury, vertebral artery compromise, death

X vs Y

SCM spasm vs chin Same side as chin Opposite side to chin
Age Child, acute after URTI / trauma / surgery Infant
Pain Painful Painless

How it's asked

"5-yr, fixed torticollis 4 weeks after fall, CT rotatory fixation — next?"cervical traction
✗ Wrong options: soft collar · SCM release

"6-yr, head turned 3 days after URTI, no deficit?"soft collar
✗ Wrong options: halter · halo · arthrodesis

"7-yr, 1 week of collar, still tilted?"continue soft collar ≥ 1 week, re-evaluate

"Painful head tilt 5 days after tonsillectomy?"cervical collar + NSAIDs + antispasmodic

"3-yr, 8 months of torticollis, failed halter + halo, still subluxed?"posterior atlantoaxial fusion
✗ Wrong options: occipitocervical fusion

"12-yr, 10 weeks, failed 2 wks halter + 3 wks halo?"C1-2 arthrodesis

"Commonest Fielding type?"type I — unilateral, TAL intact, dens pivot

"Right facet subluxation — chin + SCM spasm?"chin left, left SCM spastic

Atlas Fractures & Transverse Ligament Injuries

Spine · 8 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence~7% of cervical fractures · 25% of CVJ injuries · 1–3% of all spinal injuries · commonly missed

Bimodal20–30 yrs high-energy axial load (~85% MVC) · elderly low-energy ground-level fall (OA, osteoporosis, gait imbalance)

Associated injuries50% other spine injury · 40% axis (C2) fracture · head injury

Neuro injury rarecapacious canal: cord occupies only ~35% at C1 (vs 50% lower) · burst increases space at C1

Mechanism

Force Injury
Axial compression (blow to vertex, diving) Jefferson burst
Hyperextension (blow to forehead) Posterior arch fracture; low-energy falls; ↑ with odontoid fractures; 30% less energy needed in extension
Lateral compression Anterior arch fracture
Lateral distraction Comminuted lateral mass

Anatomy

Atlasring with 2 lateral masses, no body, no spinous process; 3 ossification centres

Incomplete posterior archcommon congenital variant, not trauma

Vertebral arteryC1 transverse foramen → bends posteromedially across sulcal groove (common site of posterior arch fracture)

Ligament Role
Transverse (TAL) Main stabiliser of C1-2 (TO); posterior dens → anterior arch, inserts on lateral mass tubercles; prevents dens migrating into canal
Alar (paired) Dens → occipital condyles; strong (5–6 mm); occipitocervical stability
Apical Weak midline, dens → foramen magnum
Tectorial membrane Posterior C2 body → anterior foramen magnum; cephalad PLL

Layers anterior to dura (dorsal → ventral)tectorial membrane → cruciate ligament → odontoid (alar + apical) ligaments

Motionocciput-C1 ~50% of flexion-extension · C1-2 ~50% of rotation

3Presentation

Symptomsneck pain, spasm, ↓ motion, suboccipital pain, sense of instability

C2 / greater occipital nerveoccipital neuralgia (TO: commonest nerve injury — emerges below posterior arch), occipital numbness, rarely alopecia, neck flexion/extension weakness

Vertebral artery dissectionLOC, diplopia, vertigo, blindness, ataxia, dysphagia, nausea

Neuro deficituncommon in isolated C1; ↑ with associated C2 fracture

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Measure Normal / abnormal
ADI Adult < 3 mm normal (child < 5) · 3–5 mm = TAL torn, alar + apical intact · > 5 mm = TAL + alar + tectorial membrane (OB: > 7 mm)
Sum of lateral mass displacement (open mouth / coronal CT) > 6.9 mm (rule of Spence) or > 8.1 mm with magnification (rule of Heller) → TAL rupture, unstable (OB: ≥ 7 mm / 8.2 mm)
SAC (PADI) Normal ≥ 13–14 mm; doesn't predict TAL injury
Retropharyngeal soft tissue > 9.5 mm → anterior arch injury
Basion-dens interval < 12 mm normal (occipitocervical)
Power's ratio Occipitocervical instability
C2 pars displacement Hangman fracture

X-rayslateral, 60° obliques (posterior arch), open-mouth · flexion-extension only for late instability after non-op (not in ED)

CTevery suspected injury; sagittal recon = occult anterior arch fracture · axial = Dickman II avulsion · coronal = LMD · CTA for vertebral artery

Paediatric pseudospreadasymmetric atlas vs axis growth → lateral mass overhang without fracture (CT)

MRIevery confirmed C1 fracture; ↑ T2 signal in TAL = intrasubstance tear; more sensitive than CT for TAL

LMD caveatmeasurements may underestimate due to rebound after injury

Landells classification

Type Pattern Treatment
1 Isolated anterior or posterior arch — commonest; "plough" = isolated anterior arch from dens driven through it Hard collar
2 Jefferson burst — bilateral anterior + posterior arch, axial load TAL intact → hard collar · intrasubstance tear → C1-2 fusion · bony avulsion → halo
3 Unilateral lateral mass TAL intact → hard collar · disrupted → halo

Dickman classification (TAL injury)

Type Lesion Treatment
I Intrasubstance tear (won't heal) C1-2 fusion
II Bony avulsion of C1 lateral mass tubercle Halo (75% success)

5Management

Atlas fracture
  • TAL intact (LMD < 7 mm, normal ADI)→RIGID COLLAR 6–12 WKS
    (not soft collar or observation)
  • TAL bony avulsion (Dickman II)→HALO 3 months
  • TAL intrasubstance tear (Dickman I)→POSTERIOR C1-2 FUSION
    • Comminuted C1 / no C1 purchase→OCCIPUT-C2 FUSION

Elderlynever halo (↑ mortality) → hard collar / surgery · clear C-spine expeditiously

Non-opreduce with halo traction first if displaced · post-treatment flexion-extension films for late instability

Operative indicationsunstable type 2/3 (controversial) · Dickman I · combined C1 + C2 fractures (type II odontoid, hangman) · some prefer occiput-C2 (lower revision)

C1 internal fixationlateral mass split; preserves C1-2 motion (small series)

Fusion technique

Technique Fact
Harms / Goel (C1 lateral mass + C2 pedicle screws) 10° medial trajectory protects internal carotid (bicortical) (✗ Wrong options: vertebral artery · cord (at risk if too medial))
C1-2 transarticular screw (Magerl) Vertebral artery at risk in C2 groove (~10% on CT planning) (TO)
Gallie sublaminar wiring Needs intact posterior arch → valid for TAL disruption (✗ Wrong options: OC dissociation · comminuted C1 · type I/III odontoid)
Occiput-C2 Comminuted C1; occipital plate

Motion lossC1-2 fusion → ~50% rotation · occiput-C2 → 50% rotation + 50% flexion-extension (TO)

6Complications

Complication Fact
Vertebral artery Displaced posterior ring through sulcal groove
Cock-robin deformity Displaced unilateral sagittal split lateral mass → occipital condyle settles on C2 → occipitocervical fusion ± osteotomy
Nonunion ~20% non-op
Neck pain / stiffness 14–80% pain, 8–20% stiffness, 34% activity limits
Infection ↑ with posterior approach
Prognosis Worse PROMs with > 7 mm displacement

ADI Ligaments torn
< 3 mm None
3–5 mm TAL only
> 5 mm (> 7 mm OB) TAL + alar + tectorial membrane
Dickman I Dickman II
Lesion Midsubstance Bony avulsion

How it's asked

"Main stabiliser of C1-C2?"transverse ligament (TO)

"ADI > 7 mm implies?"TAL + alar ruptured + tectorial membrane ruptured

✗ Wrong options: ADI 2 · PADI 16 · pars shift · Power's ratio

"Diving injury, LMD 8.3 mm — true?"TAL disrupted, unstable → rigid orthosis / halo / surgery

"Comminuted C1, which suggests TAL rupture?"LMD 9 mm
✗ Wrong options: SAC 15 · ADI 2 · BDI 11 · PVS 5 mm

"Occiput-C2 fusion — motion loss?"50% rotation + 50% flexion-extension (TO)

✗ Wrong options: collar · halo

✗ Wrong options: halo · flex-ex films · fusion

Cauda Equina Syndrome

Spine · 9 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidencerare: 1.5–3.4 per million/yr; prevalence ~1/65,000 · in ~3% of lumbar disc herniations (TO: 1–10%)

Demographicsmales, 4th decade (30s)

LevelL4-5 commonest

Causes

Cause Note
Disc herniation Commonest (large central HNP) (✗ Wrong options: burst fracture commonest)
Spinal epidural haematoma Spontaneous on high-dose DVT prophylaxis, epidural anaesthesia, early post-op prophylaxis
Tumours Myxopapillary ependymoma, schwannoma, meningioma
Synovial facet cyst · epidural abscess Ask about IVDU
Trauma Lumbar burst retropulsion, dislocation
High-grade developmental spondylolisthesis Intact lamina

Pathobiology

Compression → ↓ nutrient delivery (↓ blood flow, ↓ CSF diffusion) → venous congestion + intraneural oedema → "intraneural compartment syndrome" → root ischaemia

Vulnerabilityroots covered only by endoneurium (no Schwann cell covering per TO) + hypovascular central zone

Anatomy

Conus medullaristerminal cord, ends T12–L1 · filum terminale → coccyx

Cauda equinaL1–S5 roots in lumbar canal → lower motor neuron lesion

Bladder innervation Effect
Parasympathetic (pelvic splanchnic, S2–4) Voiding — detrusor contracts, internal sphincter relaxes
Sympathetic (hypogastric plexus) Retention — detrusor relaxes, internal sphincter contracts
Pudendal nerve External sphincter (voluntary)

CES bladderLMN interrupts reflex arc → can't sense filling → retention → overflow incontinence

3Presentation

Historyheavy lifting with flexed spine, coughing spell · ask anticoagulants, recent procedures (haematoma), IVDU (infection)

Symptom Frequency
Back pain Commonest (Orthobullets, OB) — may be the only initial symptom · 83%
Leg pain (radicular) 2nd commonest (Orthobullets) · 90% — "commonest presenting symptom" in some OB stems — read the options
Urinary retention 60%
Urinary incontinence 55%
Leg weakness / sensory deficit / absent ankle jerk 55% / 62% / 62%
Perianal sensory deficit 76%
↓ rectal tone 50% (relatively late)
Sexual / bowel dysfunction Rare

Order of frequencyleg pain > urinary retention > urinary incontinence

Saddle anaesthesialess common but most specific → trigger emergency surgery protocol

Saddle anaesthesia alone, no other LL deficit (TO)lesion below S2 (S3–S5)

Exampalpate bladder · ↓ voluntary rectal tone · pinprick loss S2–S4 perianal/perineum/posterior thigh · ↓ / absent LL reflexes · ↓/absent bulbocavernosus + anal wink
✗ Wrong options: Babinski (UMN sign — EXCEPT answer)

Classic triad (TO)asymmetric areflexic paralysis + bladder/bowel (S2–4) + saddle anaesthesia (S3–4, perianal S5 spared initially)

32-yr, unilateral radiculopathy + saddle anaesthesia (TO)CES + urinary retention + absent ankle jerk
✗ Wrong options: perianal anaesthesia + areflexia · conus

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnosis is clinical (history + exam); MRI confirms cause + plans surgery

Key radiology findings

Study Use
MRI Study of choice, emergent (within 1–2 h of ED arrival); large central disc obliterating canal, haematoma, abscess
CT myelography Can't have MRI (pacemaker) (TO: myelo-CT; ✗ Wrong options: plain CT · angiography · no imaging)
X-ray Usually normal; don't delay MRI; burst fracture / high-grade slip

Genes & lab markers

CBC, ESR, CRPif abscess suspected (fever, IVDU) — not routine
✗ Wrong options: labs before MRI without infection signs

Post-void residual (PVR)pre-op if it doesn't delay surgery · normal < 50–100 mL · PVR < 200 mL → 97% NPV · > 200 mL highly suggestive
✗ Wrong options: "> 200 mL excludes"

Classification – bladder function

Grade Definition
Incomplete (CES-I) ↓ urgency / sensation, no retention or incontinence
Complete (CES-R) Clear retention and/or incontinence

5Management

Suspected CES (saddle anaesthesia / bladder change + back-leg pain)
  • EMERGENT MRI (CT myelogram if MRI contraindicated)
  • Compression confirmed→DECOMPRESSION ASAP
    (< 24 h preferable · < 48 h standard of care)

Timing of decompression

< 48 h vs > 48 hsignificant improvement in sensory, motor, urinary + rectal function when < 48 h · no added benefit < 24 h vs 24–48 h (Ahn meta-analysis)
✗ Wrong options: < 24 h better than < 48 h · < 60 h · < 1 week

Not indicatedepidural steroid injection · methylprednisolone · outpatient MRI · reassurance/bed rest

Surgical technique

Procedure Indication
Microdiscectomy (unilateral laminotomy + medial facetectomy + discectomy) Massive soft disc, young, minimal degeneration
Wide laminectomy (bilateral laminectomy, LF + medial facetectomy, discectomy) Older, hypertrophic LF + lateral recess stenosis; ↓ dural retraction, better visualisation
Laminectomy + fusion High-grade spondylolisthesis; insidious CES with degenerative spondylolisthesis

Microdiscectomy vs wide laminectomysimilar outcomes; goal is adequate thecal sac decompression
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy → greater recovery

6Complications

Prognosis – bladder dysfunction recovery

Worse prognosissaddle anaesthesia + bladder dysfunction at presentation · decompression > 48 h · pre-op chronic back pain (urinary/rectal) · pre-op rectal dysfunction · older age (sexual function, neuro recovery)
✗ Wrong options: age per se in some OB stems

Least likely to recoverbladder dysfunction · long-term urinary dysfunction common (~50%) · bladder improves up to 16 months; motor up to 1 yr; sexual over years
✗ Wrong options: EHL weakness · perianal sensation · foot numbness · leg pain

Expected coursepain + sensory improve; residual bladder dysfunction possible
✗ Wrong options: complete recovery · no change

Complication Fact
Dural tear 1–4%; primary repair ± fat graft, bed rest 4–7 d, ± lumbar drain; outcomes unaffected if treated
Iatrogenic instability Aggressive medial facetectomy
Epidural fibrosis Back/leg pain at ~3 months; gad MRI separates from recurrent HNP; poor response to re-exploration
Wound infection ~1%; diabetics
Vascular injury Curette through ALL; mortality up to 50%
DVT Prophylaxis held for haematoma fear; antiplatelets safe at 48–72 h post-op

Reflexes Areflexia (ankle; knee lost if L3–4 involved) Knee reflex preserved

How it's asked

"Young man, unilateral radiculopathy + saddle anaesthesia — diagnosis + signs?"CES, urinary retention, absent ankle jerk (TO)

"Order of findings in CES, commonest → least?"leg pain > urinary retention > urinary incontinence

"Decompression window for bladder + motor recovery?"< 48 hours

Cerebral Palsy - Spinal Disorders

Spine · 10 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Scoliosis in CPoverall 20%; ↑ with severity · spastic quadriplegia highest (esp. non-sitters) · bedridden → ~100% · rare in ambulators

Spondylolisthesis4–21% in spastic diplegia → check lateral film

Pathophysiology

Causemuscle weakness + truncal imbalance (little evidence) · pelvic obliquity drives deformity

CP scoliosis vs idiopathic
Progression More likely; 1–2° per month from age 8–10
Onset Earlier
Shape Long, stiff C-shaped; left curves common
Sagittal Greater kyphosis / lordosis
Pelvis Pelvic obliquity
Maturity Delayed
Bracing Less effective
Fusion Longer, often to pelvis
Host Medically fragile → multidisciplinary

4Diagnosis & Classification

Weinstein classification

Group Curve Pelvic obliquity
I Double thoracic + lumbar Minimal
II Large lumbar / thoracolumbar Marked

Key radiology findings

X-rayssame technique over time; erect films when possible · AP + lateral (rib deformity, wedging, rotation, spondylolisthesis) · bending films: push-pull / fulcrum if uncooperative

MRInot routine pre-op; only for rapid progression or neuro change

Examhamstring contracture → ↓ lumbar lordosis · hip contracture → ↑ lordosis · curve flexibility, balance, pelvic obliquity

5Management

Non-opnon-progressive curves < 50° or age < 10 (delay surgery) · custom seat (sitting only) · TLSO improves sitting balance, doesn't change natural history (may slow progression only if immature) · Botox short-term (2–3 months)

Operative criteria

Procedure Indication
PSF ± pelvis Group I, 50–90°, ambulators, progressive / interfering with sitting; age > 10; adequate hip ROM; stable nutrition
PSF ± ASF ± pelvis Group I > 90°, non-ambulators, Group II; immature (crankshaft); anterior release for > 100–120° rigid curves (don't bend < 60°)
Growing rods Young child
Pre-op traction Severe rigid curves

Surgery threshold (OB)progressive > 40–50° interfering with sitting in > 10 yrs

Fusion levels

ProximalT1 or T2 (avoid proximal junctional kyphosis)

DistalL4/L5 for long curves · pelvis if obliquity > 15° (else loss of correction + pseudarthrosis)

CP scoliosis needing fusion
  • Pelvic obliquity > 15° / non-sitter → POSTERIOR T2→PELVIS
    (do not fuse to L4 — late loss of correction)
  • Immature or > 100–120° rigid→ADD ANTERIOR RELEASE (same day)

Fixation techniques

Technique Note
Luque rod + sublaminar wires Classic
Unit rod + sublaminar wires Pelvic limb may penetrate pelvis
Pedicle screws Better correction, may avoid anterior surgery
Galveston Rod bent from S1 lamina → PSIS → between iliac tables just above sciatic notch
Others Bilateral sacral screws, iliosacral screws, spinopelvic transiliac, Dunn-McCarthy (S-rod over sacral ala)

A/P same day↓ complications vs staged (nutrition, blood loss, stay) · post-op brace usually unnecessary

Pre-operative assessment

Domain Action
Nutrition Weight < 5th centile → ↑ infection, intubation, stay; albumin > 3.5 g/dL; consider G-tube
Respiratory History, exam, CXR (formal PFTs hard)
GI Treat GERD (aspiration)
Seizures Control; valproic acid → check bleeding time

Goalpainless solid fusion, balanced spine, level pelvis; family goals + risk-benefit

Implant failure Often asymptomatic
Wound infection 3–5%, > idiopathic; local debridement usually enough
GI GERD, malnutrition
Neuro Seizures
Death 0–7%
