Cervical Adjacent Segment Disease

Spine · 11 / 69

1Basics

Definitions

Adjacent segment degenerationradiographic degeneration next to a fusion, ± symptoms

Adjacent segment disease (ASD)clinical radiculopathy / myelopathy from the adjacent motion segment

Adjacent level ossification development (ALOD)large anterior marginal osteophyte / ALL heterotopic bone next to a plated level; disc height preserved, no herniation; clinical significance unclear

Epidemiology

Incidenceclinically relevant ASD 1.6–2.4% / yr (reoperation ~2.4%/yr) · 25.6% at 10 yrs after ACDF

LevelsC4-5, C5-6 (highest), C6-7 · C2-3 lowest

Risk factor for ASD
Smoking Strongest
Female Yes (✗ Wrong options: male)
Age < 60 Yes
< 3 levels fused Yes (more remaining motion segments)
Fusion ending next to C5-6 / C6-7 Most mobile levels
Pre-op dural indentation / disc protrusion at C5-7 Yes
Fusion vs non-fusion (foraminotomy, discectomy) No difference

Pathophysiology

ASD↑ compensatory motion → ↑ intradiscal pressure, stress, shear → herniation, height loss, osteophytes → stenosis · also ALL / longus colli damage · needle marker in wrong disc

Counter-evidenceCDA (motion-preserving) does not reduce ASD (Orthobullets ASD note) — partly natural history of spondylosis

ALODanterior plate within 5 mm of adjacent disc; variable-angle screws allow subsidence toward the disc
✗ Wrong options: diabetes · static screws (protective) · male · age > 60

Park classification of adjacent level ossification

Grade Ossification
0 None
1 < 50% of disc space
2 > 50% of disc space
3 Complete bridging

3Presentation

Radiculopathydermatomal pain / weakness at adjacent root

Myelopathygait instability, hand clumsiness, urinary change

Examinspect wound · painful ROM arc · rule out cubital / carpal tunnel for sensory change · Hoffmann (myelopathy) · Spurling (radiculopathy) · shoulder abduction relief (radiculopathy) · Lhermitte (myelopathy)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP, lateral, flexion-extension Disc narrowing, foraminal stenosis, osteophytes, facet arthropathy; poor correlation with symptoms
CT Pseudarthrosis, OPLL
MRI Foraminal / central stenosis at adjacent level

Differential (top 5)pseudarthrosis · progressive DDD · indolent infection · myelopathy / radiculopathy at a non-adjacent level

Prevalence contextradiographic degeneration in > 80% of asymptomatic unfused people > 60 · in 25–91% of fused patients

5Management

Symptomatic ASD
  • Radiculopathy / mild myelopathy→NSAIDs, gabapentin, PT, activity change ± brief collar
    (avoid narcotics; NSAID caution in elderly)
  • Failed non-op radiculopathy / any significant myelopathy→EXTEND FUSION
    • Single adjacent level→CERVICAL DISC REPLACEMENT (≈ ACDF)

Extension of fusionapproach by pathology location · re-operate from the prior side (assume ipsilateral RLN palsy unless ENT scope is normal → protects contralateral nerve) · include C5-6/C6-7 · fusing > 3 levels ↓ further ASD · plate > 5 mm from adjacent disc

CDR for ASDsingle level; equivalent to ACDF; ↓ adjacent ossification vs plating; align centre of rotation · as index procedure no clear ASD protection (meta-analyses: possibly ↓ reoperation)

6Complications

Progressionnew adjacent segment degenerates after extension → further revision

Reoperationoverall 6.1–25.6%; 0.7–3.7%/yr

X vs Y

Adjacent segment degeneration Adjacent segment disease ALOD
Definition Radiographic Clinical symptoms Anterior ossification
Cause Motion / natural history Same Plate < 5 mm from disc
Rx Observe Non-op → extend fusion / CDR Observe

How it's asked

"Asymptomatic anterior ossification 2 yrs after ACDF — associated factor?"plate placed within 5 mm of adjacent disc
✗ Wrong options: diabetes · static screws · male · age > 60

"Strongest risk factor for cervical ASD?"smoking

"Level with highest ASD risk?"C5-6

"Revision anterior approach — which side?"same side as prior surgery (unless ENT shows normal cords)

Benefits & risks

Benefit Risk
Preserves motion Hardware failure → retropulsion into canal, paralysis
Pseudarthrosis not a concern Persistent facet-origin neck pain
Quicker return to activities —

1- and 2-level radiculopathy Significant facet degeneration (advanced facet arthrosis) → persistent axial neck pain → fuse instead
1- and 2-level myelopathy (thorough decompression) —
Revision CDA within 2 weeks = primary results —

Not contraindicationstwo-level disease · loss of disc height · neutral lordosis/kyphosis · bilateral Hoffmann (myelopathy)

vs ACDF
Neurologic improvement Equivalent (1 + 2 level)

Cervical Facet Dislocations & Fractures

Spine · 13 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Younghigh-energy (MVC, motorcycle, contact sport) · elderly — low-energy

C7 / C7-T117% of injuries → always visualise cervicothoracic junction

Associatedhead injury, non-contiguous spine injuries (TL, CT, OC junctions), limb injuries

Pathoanatomy

Injury Key facts
Facet fracture Superior facet more often; ↓ threshold for dislocation (loses interlock); may be a spontaneously reduced dislocation
Unilateral facet dislocation Most frequently missed C-spine injury on X-ray · ~25% subluxation · monoradiculopathy that improves with traction (inferior facet of cephalad vertebra in foramen) · rotation component
Bilateral facet dislocation ~50% subluxation · SCI in ~80%

Mechanismflexion-distraction ± rotation → posterior structures torn (ISL, capsule, LF, posterior annulus), ALL usually preserved
✗ Wrong options: flexion-compression · vertical compression · extension

PLL / posterior annulus disruption40% unilateral · 80% bilateral

2Mechanism

Allen & Ferguson classification (research)

Group
1 Flexion-compression —
2 Vertical compression —
3 Flexion-distraction Stage 1 facet sprain, < 10° kyphosis · Stage 2 unilateral dislocation · Stage 3 bilateral, 50% (perched) · Stage 4 complete (100%)
4 Extension-compression —
5 Extension-distraction —
6 Lateral flexion —

Clinical (descriptive)compression, burst, flexion-distraction, unilateral/bilateral dislocation, facet fracture

3Presentation

Unilateralsingle-arm paraesthesia: C5-6 → C6 (wrist extension weakness, thumb numbness) · C6-7 → C7 (triceps + wrist flexion weakness, index + middle finger numbness) · angular deformity

Bilateralbilateral UL + LL weakness, sensory change → ASIA documentation

HistoryAS / DISH, prior fusion · scalp lacerations → head injury

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Findings / indication
X-ray (AP, lateral, oblique, open mouth) Subluxation (25% unilateral, 50% bilateral), disc height loss (retropulsed disc), widened interspinous distance, focal hypolordosis, soft-tissue swelling
Flexion-extension Whenever a facet fracture is seen (spontaneous reduction / occult instability)
CT Most cases; subtle facet subluxation, facet/pedicle/lamina fractures
MRI Before any open reduction / surgery; obtunded patient (emergent); failed closed reduction; neuro worsening during reduction — looks for disc herniation, PLC injury, cord compression; cord haematoma = poor motor prognosis

MRI timing (TO)best sensitivity for haemorrhage/oedema within 48 h

Differentiallateral mass fracture-separation → needs 2-level fusion (dislocation = 1 level)

5Management

Operative criteria – facet fractures

Stable (non-op 6–12 wks, collar / halo) Unstable (single-level instrumented fusion)
Reduced unilateral fracture, no instability on flex-ex Bilateral facet fractures
< 40% of lateral mass or < 1 cm height > 40% of lateral mass or > 1 cm height

Non-op risk> 30% re-subluxation / redislocation, persistent pain

Halosuboptimal in lower C-spine; most likely to fail with facet fracture-dislocation · hard orthosis often satisfactory
✗ Wrong options: C1 lateral mass · C2 pars · burst

Closed reduction vs MRI first

Facet dislocation
  • Awake, alert, cooperative→EMERGENT CLOSED REDUCTION (Gardner-Wells)
    → MRI after reduction → URGENT STABILISATION (< 24 h)
  • Obtunded / intoxicated / intubated / GCS ↓→EMERGENT MRI FIRST
    → OPEN REDUCTION + STABILISATION
  • Failed closed reduction or neuro worsening → STOP → MRI→OPEN REDUCTION

Awake patient — source conflictimmediate closed traction (Orthobullets, OB — especially with deteriorating neuro, ASIA E → D) · hard collar + MRI first (TO answer for awake, intact patient 30 min post-injury) — read the options

Pre-reduction MRInot routine in awake patients (delays reduction, early reduction ↑ recovery)

MRI finding that changes planherniated disc at dislocated level → anterior discectomy before/with reduction
✗ Wrong options: cord oedema · PLC disruption · lamina fracture · ALL signal

Reduction fact Value
Unilateral Harder to reduce, more stable after; closed success ~25%
Bilateral Easier (PLL torn), less stable after
Fail closed reduction 26%
Never Closed reduction with altered mental status

Closed reduction technique

Gardner-Wells tongs1 cm above pinna, in line with EAM, below skull equator · posterior pin placement → flexion moment

Weights5–10 lb increments · up to 140 lb or 70% body weight · ~9.4–9.8 lb per level above injury · carbon-fibre tongs for MRI (~80 lb limit)

After reductiondrop to 10–15 lb, add extension (pulley / thoracic pad)

Abortneuro worsening (→ immediate MRI) · overdistraction > 1.5× adjacent disc space

Requirementssedation, respiratory supervision, serial cross-table laterals + neuro exams

Surgical options

Procedure Indication / technique
ACDF (single level, plate) Disc herniation compressing cord after reduction; anterior open reduction of unilateral dislocation — Caspar pin distraction + rotate cephalad pin toward dislocated side
Anterior reduction of bilateral Converging Caspar pins (10–20°) compressed to unlock; curette pushes rostral body back · lamina spreaders not effective for bilateral
Posterior reduction + lateral mass screws No disc herniation; irreducible from front/closed; Penfield 4 lever, remove tip of caudal superior facet; often 2 levels (poor purchase)
Anterior → posterior Disc needs removal but can't reduce anteriorly: discectomy, plate fixed to superior body only (stops graft kick-out, allows rotation)

Biomechanicsposterior fixation > anterior plating for flexion-distraction (Sutterlin, Coe) · anterior overdistraction risks cord + hardware

6Complications

Complication Fact
SSI ↑ posterior approach
Redislocation Unilateral treated in orthosis → ACDF
Respiratory ARDS, pneumonia, tracheostomy
Vertebral artery Up to 11%; ↑ with lateral mass / transverse process involvement; often unrecognised
Oesophageal Anterior reduction → primary repair
Pin-tract Halo; meningitis if inner table breached

Prognosisworse with severe deficit, older age, cord haematoma · bilateral dislocation = worst outcome among subaxial patterns

X vs Y

Unilateral Bilateral
Subluxation 25% 50%
Neuro Radiculopathy SCI ~80%
PLL torn 40% 80%
Reduce Harder, stable after Easier, unstable after
X-ray Most missed Obvious

How it's asked

"Characteristic mechanism of facet dislocation?"flexion-distraction

"Right C6-7 facet dislocation — which CT image?"unilateral right perched/jumped facet

"Awake, alert, 30 min post-injury, C4-5 jumped facets — next (TO)?"hard collar + MRI (TO) · OB awake-patient stems → immediate closed reduction — read the options

"Awake, cooperative, ASIA E → D after CT?"immediate closed reduction with traction
✗ Wrong options: MRI first · steroids

"GCS 3, intubated, bilateral C5-6 dislocation?"MRI

"Intubated, moving limbs, bilateral C6-7?"cervical MRI then reduction

"Intoxicated, obtunded, bilateral C4-5?"immediate MRI then surgery

"MRI finding mandating surgery instead of closed reduction?"herniated disc at dislocated level

"GCS 10, C7-T1 fracture-dislocation?"immediate MRI

"Altered mental status — MRI finding that changes approach?"herniated disc (→ anterior)

"Nondisplaced C6 facet fracture, stable on flex-ex?"hard collar

"Halo most likely to fail?"C6-7 facet fracture-dislocation

"Worst outcome with incomplete SCI?"bilateral facet dislocation

Cervical Lateral Mass Fracture Separation

Spine · 14 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

DemographicsM:F 2:1, mean age 35 (20–70)

LevelC6 > C5 > C7 > C4 > C3

Pathoanatomy

Lesionfracture lines through lamina + pedicle → lateral mass (with its superior + inferior facets) floats free

Two-level instabilityboth the facet joint above and below are compromised → fuse 2 levels (vs facet dislocation = 1 level)

Associated finding Rate
Anterior listhesis of fractured vertebra 77%
Listhesis of vertebra above / below 24% / 10%
Coronal translation 33%
Body collapse 33% (less in type A)

2Mechanism

Mechanismtraffic accident, fall, heavy object on head → hyperextension + lateral compression + rotation

Allen-Ferguson groupsextension-compression · lateral flexion → type B comminution · flexion-distraction

3Presentation

Neuro symptomscommon (up to 66%): radiculopathy, SCI / myelopathy (Frankel / ASIA)

Examtorticollis, paravertebral spasm

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
X-ray (AP, lateral, oblique) Low sensitivity — 38%; disc narrowing; instability = > 3.5 mm displacement, > 10° kyphosis, > 10° rotation vs adjacent level
CT Diagnosis; fracture extends into adjacent facets, foramen transversarium, transverse process, pedicle, lamina; translation, uncovertebral subluxation
MRI ALL rupture 50–75% · PLL 30–35% · ISL/SSL 10–75% · disc disruption, bone bruise

Kotani classification

Type Description Same-level / adjacent translation
A – Separation 2 fracture lines: unilateral lamina + pedicle 91% / 20%
B – Comminution Multiple lines, lateral wedging (lateral flexion) — / 50%
C – Split Vertical coronal line; superior articular process of caudal vertebra invaginates 80% / 0%
D – Traumatic spondylolysis Bilateral horizontal pars fractures → anterior/posterior elements separate 100% / 50%

5Management

Non-op (Miami J / halo)stable, no deficit; halo poorly controls hyperextension/rotation; spontaneous fusion only 20%; late instability + pain → surveillance

Procedure Indication
Posterior decompression + 2-level instrumented fusion Most cases (injured structures posterior); lateral mass / pedicle screws or lateral mass plating; late instability after non-op
Two-level ACDF Minimally displaced, no posterior reduction needed; controls anterior collapse + rotation
Single pedicle screw Type A separation without instability
A + P Extra anterior support; failed anterior reduction / late presentation

Posterior caveatsanterior disc collapse + late kyphosis; midline (wiring) fusion doesn't control rotation

C5 lateral mass fracture-separation + ASIA C
  • Posterior→INSTRUMENTED FUSION C4-6 (both levels)
    (not C5-6 only or C5 corpectomy — body intact)
  • Anterior alternative→2-LEVEL ACDF C4-6

Lateral mass screw safety

Technique (TO)start 1 mm medial to pillar midpoint, aim 30° lateral + 15° cephalad

Structure most at risk (TO)nerve root (exits anterolateral to facet; long screw)
✗ Wrong options: vertebral artery (too medial) · cord (safe) · thoracic duct

6Complications

Vertebral artery injury (pedicle screws) · late kyphosis · late instability · chronic neck pain · radiculopathy

X vs Y

Lateral mass fracture-separation Facet dislocation
Mechanism Hyperextension + lateral compression + rotation Flexion-distraction
Unstable levels 2 1
Fusion 2 levels (above + below) 1 level
X-ray detection 38% Unilateral often missed

How it's asked

"ASIA C, C5 lateral mass fracture-separation — management?"posterior instrumented fusion C4-6
✗ Wrong options: C5-6 · corpectomy · 1-level ACDF

"Structure most at risk with lateral mass screw?"nerve root (TO)

"Why fuse two levels?"superior + inferior facets of the floating mass both incompetent

Cervical Myelopathy

Spine · 15 / 69

1Basics

Aetiology

Commonest causedegenerative cervical spondylotic myelopathy (CSM): osteophytes, disc-osteophyte complex, degenerative spondylolisthesis, LF hypertrophy

Otherscongenital stenosis (symptoms when spondylosis adds on) · OPLL · tumour · epidural abscess · trauma · kyphosis

Cord injury mechanismdirect compression + ischaemia (anterior spinal artery compression)

Tandem stenosis (cervical + lumbar)~20% (Orthobullets) · 5–25% (OB) → myelopathy takes precedence

Epidemiology & canal diameter

Commonest level of spondylosis (TO)C5-6, then C6-7 (most subaxial motion)
✗ Wrong options: C3-4 · C1-2

Canal (AP) Value (TO)
Normal ~17 mm (C1 23, C2 20, C3–6 17, C7 15)
Relative stenosis 10–13 mm
Absolute stenosis < 10 mm (area < 100 mm²)
Cord compression Canal < 13 mm (Orthobullets)
Pavlov (Torg) ratio < 0.8 → congenitally narrow canal

X-ray degeneration70% by age 70 · MRI false positives: 28% of > 40 yrs have HNP / foraminal stenosis

Natural history

Patternslow, stepwise deterioration with plateaus · rarely improves non-op
✗ Wrong options: stable · rapid continuous decline · improves with steroids/rest

Worse non-op outcomesegmental kyphosis, circumferential compression

3Presentation

Symptomsneck pain/stiffness (often absent; ~15% of severe cases painless), occipital headache · diffuse non-dermatomal bilateral paraesthesia · hand clumsiness (buttons, handwriting, dropping things) · gait instability (most important clinical predictor; stairs) · urinary retention (rare, late)

Severe CSMproximal LL muscles weaker than distal (opposite in lumbar stenosis)

Clinical diagnosis – UMN signs

Sign Fact
Gait ataxia Commonest physical finding (91%) (OB)
Hoffmann Flick middle finger DIP → thumb/index flexion; "most common exam finding" (Orthobullets) · 80% (OB) / 59% in another series; ↑ with severity
Hyperreflexia LL 81%, UL 67%; absent if concomitant peripheral neuropathy / root compression / diabetes
Inverted radial reflex Tap distal brachioradialis → finger flexion
Sustained clonus (> 3 beats) Sensitivity ~13%, specificity ~100%
Babinski Least common (≤ 1/3)
Finger escape (Wartenberg) Small finger abducts/flexes (intrinsic weakness)
Grip-and-release Normal 20 in 10 s
Lhermitte Neck flexion → electric shock down spine/limbs; specific, insensitive; also MS
Romberg / tandem gait Posterior column dysfunction
Proprioception loss Advanced, poor prognosis
Pinprick ↓ Lateral spinothalamic tract

UMN vs LMNfasciculations, atrophy, tongue fasciculations, areflexia = LMN (EXCEPT answers) · weakness = both

Myelopathy handintrinsic/webspace atrophy, finger escape, dysdiadochokinesia, Hoffmann
✗ Wrong options: intrinsic plus posture

Other testsSpurling (radiculopathy) · Jackson (hyperextension) · Lasègue (lumbar SLR)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Findings
X-ray (AP, lateral, oblique, flex-ext) Uncovertebral + facet degeneration, osteophytes, disc narrowing; Pavlov < 0.8; C2-C7 alignment + local kyphosis angle; obliques → foraminal stenosis; flex-ext → instability / compensatory subluxation
MRI Study of choice; CSF effacement = functional stenosis; T2 bright = myelomalacia; T1 signal change → poorer prognosis; compression ratio < 0.4 → poor prognosis
CT OPLL, osteophytes
CT myelogram Pacemaker / hardware artefact (C1-2 or lumbar puncture)
NCS High false negatives; separates peripheral vs central (ALS)

Compression ratiosmallest AP cord diameter ÷ largest transverse diameter

Prognostic MRIfocal T2 signal better than multisegmental T2 signal · isolated low T1 = gliosis/cavitation, little improvement · cord shape boomerang → teardrop → triangular (worst)

Non-op success predictortransverse cord area > 70 mm²
✗ Wrong options: ↑ CMCT · low T1 signal · canal < 13 mm · younger age

Differentialnormal ageing, stroke, movement disorders, B12 deficiency, ALS, MS

Tandem stenosis

Lumbar claudication + UMN signs (hyperreflexia, clonus, broad gait, clumsy hands)MRI cervical (± thoracic) spine before lumbar surgery · pacemaker → CT myelogram of C-spine · gait problem with 5/5 strength + mild lumbar stenosis → MRI thoracic + cervical (brain MRI second-line)
✗ Wrong options: lumbar decompression ± fusion · ESI · PT · discogram · ABI

Classifications

Nurick
0 Root symptoms only
1 Cord signs, normal gait
2 Gait difficulty, fully employed
3 Gait prevents employment, walks unassisted
4 Walks with assistance
5 Chair / bedbound
Ranawat
I Pain, no deficit
II Subjective weakness, hyperreflexia, dysaesthesia
IIIA Objective weakness + long-tract signs, ambulatory
IIIB Same, non-ambulatory

JOA / mJOA (17 points)UL motor, LL motor, sensory, bladder · severe = mJOA < 12 · excellent outcome ≥ 16

5Management

Non-opmild disease without functional impairment or poor surgical candidate (function > exam findings) · NSAIDs, gabapentin, collar in slight flexion, PT · no traction/chiropractic · cord compression without myelopathy signs → PT
✗ Wrong options: surgery

Surgerysignificant functional impairment / progression; mild myelopathy by mJOA also indicated; operate as soon as medically stable
✗ Wrong options: wait for Nurick IV, multisegmental T2 signal, intolerable pain, incontinence

Goals & outcomes of surgery

Goalhalt stepwise neurologic decline
✗ Wrong options: complete recovery · mJOA > 16 · resolve myelomalacia · restore normal lordosis (aim neutral)

Expectedsignificant mJOA improvement at 1 yr even in severe (mJOA 10) CSM; gait / sensory improvement variable

Approach selection

Cervical myelopathy surgery
  • 1–2 levels, anterior pathology→ACDF (± corpectomy)
  • ≥ 3 levels, lordotic / neutral
    • No axial neck pain, want motion→LAMINOPLASTY
    • Axial neck pain / instability→LAMINECTOMY + FUSION
  • Fixed kyphosis > 10–13°
    • ≤ 2 levels→ANTERIOR (ACDF / corpectomy)
    • ≥ 3 levels→ANTERIOR + POSTERIOR

Operative criteria – alignment thresholds

Threshold Meaning
Fixed kyphosis > 10° Anterior procedure (corrects kyphosis); posterior alone not enough
Local kyphosis > 13° Contraindication to posterior decompression / strongest predictor of poor laminoplasty outcome (✗ Wrong options: multilevel stenosis · osteoporosis · CSF effacement)
Kyphosis > 12° + > 2 levels Combined A/P (OB)
≥ 3-level corpectomy Anterior alone → > 70% catastrophic failure → add posterior

Bowstringingin kyphosis cord drapes over anterior osteophytes; posterior decompression can't let it drift back

Anterior decompression – ACDF/corpectomy

ACDFmainstay for 1–2 levels, anterior pathology (soft disc, disc-osteophyte, focal OPLL), fixed kyphosis > 10° · single-level CSM gold standard (TO) · Smith-Robinson approach · avoid with poor swallowing

Anterior vs posterioranterior: ↓ infection, ↓ blood loss, ↓ post-op pain · no difference in C5 palsy, C7 radiculopathy, overall complications

Corpectomyretrovertebral disease / kyphosis · 2-level corpectomies vulnerable (prefer 1 corpectomy + adjacent discectomy) · graft dislodgement 7–20% (can be fatal) · 2-level → add posterior fixation; 3-level → A + P mandatory

3-level corpectomy, anterior plate failureprevented by combined anterior + posterior fixation
✗ Wrong options: discectomies (can't decompress OPLL) · mesh cage · longer collar/halo · screw mix

Cervical disc arthroplasty

Ideal (TO)skeletally mature, 20–70 yrs, 1–2 levels C3–C7, soft disc, minimal spondylosis, preserved facets, disc height ≥ 3 mm, ≥ 6 wks failed non-op · single-level myelopathy without kyphosis → CDA gives best function (OB)

Contraindications (TO)≥ 3 levels · instability (> 3 mm translation / > 11° rotation) · facet degeneration · axial neck pain alone · severe spondylosis (bridging, > 50% height loss, < 2° motion) · osteoporosis / metabolic bone disease · adjacent fusion / prior surgery at level · morbid obesity (BMI > 40) · RA / spondyloarthropathy · infection, malignancy, steroids, pregnancy · kyphotic deformity
✗ Wrong options: 2-level disease (NOT a contraindication)

Complicationhardware failure → retropulsion

Posterior decompression – laminectomy/fusion vs laminoplasty

Laminectomy + fusion Laminoplasty
Indication Multilevel, kyphosis < 10°, axial neck pain (facet) Multilevel, preserved lordosis, congenital stenosis, OPLL, motion desired, pseudarthrosis risk (diabetes, steroids)
Contraindication Fixed kyphosis > 10–13° Kyphosis > 13° (e.g. 15°, 20°); severe axial neck pain (relative)
Motion Lost Preserved (some loss)
Wound complications Higher Lower
Cost (2 yr) Higher Lower
Revision Comparable Comparable
ASD Higher Lower
C5 palsy Higher (some series) Similar / lower

Laminoplasty mechanism↑ AP canal dimension + cord drifts dorsally · open door = unilateral hinge, opening held by bone/anchors/plate · French door = bilateral hinges, midline opening · C3–7 · facets not violated · bilateral exposure still needed
✗ Wrong options: medial-lateral

Not contraindications to laminoplastyOPLL · multilevel stenosis · 30° flex-ext motion · age > 55 · SAC < 8 mm · prior posterior surgery · radiculopathy (add keyhole foraminotomy) · C7 SVA +5 cm · compression ratio 0.3

Laminoplasty + fusion after ACDFpreserves paraspinal muscle attachments / ↓ atrophy
✗ Wrong options: infection · neuro · stay benefit

Laminectomy alonerarely; post-laminectomy kyphosis 11–47%

Posterior-alone ideal imagemultilevel stenosis with preserved lordosis
✗ Wrong options: teardrop fracture · kyphotic multilevel → A/P · skip-level lordotic → skip ACDF · anterior abscess

Central cord syndrome on background CSM + axial neck pain, multilevelposterior decompression + fusion
✗ Wrong options: 2-level corpectomy without posterior · lumbar MRI (proximal weaker)

Combined anterior-posterior

Indicationsmultilevel stenosis in rigid kyphotic spine · multilevel corpectomies · post-laminectomy kyphosis

Occipitocervical fusionperiodontoid pannus (posterior-only resolves pannus; transoral ↑ morbidity > 4 h)

6Complications

C5 palsy

Definitionpost-op deltoid ± biceps weakness ≥ 1 grade without myelopathy worsening

Incidence~4.6% (Orthobullets) · 5–12% after laminoplasty · 0–30% range

Timingimmediate to weeks (most within 1 week; up to 20 days); 92% unilateral; half with C5 pain/sensory change

Risk factorsmale (strongest) · laminectomy + fusion (higher in some studies)
✗ Wrong options: age · myelopathy severity · OPLL · level

Anterior vs posteriorno significant difference (Orthobullets, OB, TO); laminoplasty lower in some studies

Mechanismdorsal cord drift tethers C5 root (C5 at apex of lordosis) vs segmental cord ischaemia/reperfusion · prevention: prophylactic C4-5 keyhole foraminotomy, avoid excessive cord shift/lordosis, deltoid/biceps MEP monitoring

Prognosisgood; observe · ~80% recover by 6–12 months · prolonged recovery: multilevel paresis, motor grade ≤ 2, sensory involvement + intractable pain
✗ Wrong options: revision · extension · steroids

Anterior approach complications

Complication Fact
RLN palsy (hoarseness) Commonest nerve injury (~1%); prolonged tracheo-oesophageal retraction / ET cuff; right = left in studies; ↑ in revision (up to 9.5%); observe → ENT at 6 wks (Teflon); revision on same side as prior palsy · left RLN loops aortic arch, right subclavian
Dysphagia 30–50%; local retropharyngeal steroid before closure ↓ it (✗ Wrong options: TXA · incision type)
Airway compromise > 5 h surgery, > 300 mL blood loss, multilevel at/above C3-4 (C2–C4 exposure) → keep intubated 24–48 h
Retropharyngeal haematoma (TO) 6–24 h, tense incision, dyspnoea/dysphagia/dysphonia → immediate evacuation
Angioedema (TO) 6–12 h, face/tongue swelling → IV steroids, antihistamine, adrenaline
Pharyngolaryngeal oedema (TO) 12–72 h, voice change, worse supine → may need cricothyrotomy
Vertebral artery Burr through lateral wall of body in corpectomy
Horner Lateral retraction on longus colli (sympathetic chain)
Superior laryngeal nerve Rare; C4 and above
Hypoglossal Rare; tongue deviation
Oesophageal Rare; trauma, revision, diverticula

Posterior approach & general complications

Complication Fact
Infection Higher posterior than anterior
Pseudarthrosis 12% single, 30% multilevel (3-level ACDF + plate fusion ~80%)
Post-laminectomy kyphosis Loss of posterior tension band + muscle denervation; ↑ with pre-op kyphosis, > 30–50% facet resection, children (~100%) → A/P revision
Epidural haematoma 1/1000; new weakness → emergent MRI + evacuation
Hardware failure 7–20% after 2-level corpectomy → stabilise posteriorly
Epidural haematoma age T1 T2
Hyperacute (< 24 h) Hypo Hyper (STIR bright)
Acute (1–3 d) Iso Hypo
Subacute (3 d–2 wk) Hyper Hypo
Chronic (> 2 wk) Hypo Hypo

Root-level localisation

Disc → root Motor Sensory Reflex
C4-5 → C5 Deltoid (± biceps) Lateral arm Biceps
C5-6 → C6 Biceps (elbow flexion) + wrist extensors Radial forearm, thumb Supinator (brachioradialis)
C6-7 → C7 Triceps + wrist flexors Index, middle (ring) Triceps
C7-T1 → C8 Finger flexors (FDP) Little finger, ulnar hand/forearm —
T1 Finger abduction Medial forearm —

Cervical ruledisc compresses the exiting lower root (C5-6 → C6)

Shoulder abduction relief signradiculopathy (C7 here; elbow flexion weakness = C6)

X vs Y

Myelopathy Radiculopathy
Lesion Cord (UMN) Root (LMN)
Signs Hoffmann, clonus, broad gait Dermatomal pain, weak reflex
Tests Lhermitte Spurling, shoulder abduction relief

Cervical Radiculopathy

Spine · 16 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence107.3 / 100,000 men, 63.5 / 100,000 women per year · peak 50–54 yrs

Risk factorswhite race, smoking, prior lumbar radiculopathy

Degeneration prevalenceX-ray changes in 70% by 70 · MRI: HNP/foraminal stenosis in 28% of asymptomatic > 40 · ≥ 1 degenerated disc in ~57% (50–60%) of asymptomatic > 40 (Boden) · up to 19% asymptomatic root compression → treat only if clinical findings match MRI

Aetiology

Cause Note
Cervical spondylosis Disc-osteophyte, height loss, uncovertebral + facet spurs
Soft disc Posterolateral = commonest (between uncinate and lateral PLL) → mostly motor · intraforaminal → radicular pain · midline → myelopathy
Double crush Root + distal entrapment (↓ axoplasmic flow)
Rare Tumour, root avulsion, synovial / meningeal cyst, dural AVF, tortuous vertebral artery

Chemical mediatorsIL-1, IL-6, substance P, neuropeptide Y, CGRP, bradykinin, TNF-α, prostaglandins

Anatomy

Pedicle-root mismatchcervical root exits above its pedicle (C6 above C6 pedicle) · lumbar root exits below (L5 under L5) · extra C8 root makes the switch

Disc → rootcervical disc compresses the lower (exiting) root: C6-7 → C7

Horizontal cervical rootcentral/paracentral and foraminal discs hit the SAME root (vs lumbar: paracentral → traversing, foraminal → exiting) · far-lateral discs not typically described in C-spine

Compression sourcesfacet hypertrophy (posterior) · uncovertebral osteophyte (anterior) · disc height loss (↓ foraminal volume) · ventrolateral root course → ventral compression

Discannulus type I collagen (thicker ventrally) · nucleus type II collagen + GAGs; water 90% (< 30 yrs) → 70% (8th decade)

3Presentation

Symptomsoccipital headache, trapezial / interscapular pain, neck pain, unilateral arm pain (often non-dermatomal), dermatomal paraesthesia, weakness (overhead = C7, grip = C8)

Root-level localisation

Root (disc) Motor Reflex Sensory
C4 (C3-4) Scapular winging — Base of neck; Tinel at levator scapulae
C5 (C4-5) Deltoid + biceps (shoulder abduction, ER, elbow flexion) Biceps Superior shoulder, lateral arm
C6 (C5-6) Brachioradialis (elbow flexion in mid-pronation) + wrist extension (ECRL), biceps Brachioradialis (supinator) Thumb, (index), dorsoradial forearm
C7 (C6-7) Triceps, wrist flexion, finger extension, pronation Triceps Middle finger (index/ring), dorsal forearm, posterior arm
C8 (C7-T1) Finger flexion — DIP of middle + index (FDP), grip, thumb extension — Ring + little finger, ulnar hand/forearm
T1 (T1-2) Hand intrinsics (finger abduction) — Axilla / medial arm, ipsilateral Horner

Commonest rootC7 (several studies)

C8 vs ulnar neuropathyC8 weakens index/middle FDP (AIN-innervated) — ulnar entrapment does not

C6 vs tennis elbow vs PINC6: thumb paraesthesia + relief with shoulder abduction · lateral epicondylitis: pain with resisted wrist/long-finger extension · PIN: painless wrist extension weakness with radial deviation

Facet fracture localisationC6 superior facet fracture → C5-6 instability → C6 root (radial forearm + thumb numbness)

Provocative tests

Test Fact
Shoulder abduction relief (SAR) Hand on head relieves pain (↓ root tension) — virtually pathognomonic, best separates cervical vs shoulder/peripheral causes; analogue of SLR
Spurling Extension + rotation + lateral bend to affected side + axial load → ipsilateral arm pain; specific, not sensitive (✗ Wrong options: contralateral rotation)
Upper limb tension, Valsalva, neck distraction Supportive
Hoffmann, inverted radial reflex, clonus, Babinski Myelopathy (✗ Wrong options: radiculopathy)

Peripheral mimicsFroment (ulnar), "OK" sign failure (AIN), Guyon (no dorsal wrist sensory change), cubital tunnel (worse with elbow flexion), diabetic neuropathy (glove-stocking)

Differentialcarpal / cubital tunnel, Parsonage-Turner, thoracic outlet, myelopathy, brachial plexus injury, rotator cuff (history must match MRI)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP, lateral, oblique Oblique = best for foraminal stenosis from osteophytes; flex-ext for instability
MRI (T2 axial) Red flags (fever, weight loss, night pain), > 6 weeks of failed care, motor weakness; loss of perineural fat
CT Bony anatomy, OPLL, instrumentation planning; post-op pseudarthrosis study of choice
CT myelogram Pacemaker, hardware artefact
Discography Rarely; oesophageal puncture, discitis

Genes & lab markers – electrodiagnostics

EMG / NCS finding Fact
Fibrillations + positive sharp waves In affected root muscles (✓) — appear ~3 weeks; paraspinals first
SNAPs normal Lesion proximal to DRG
CMAP ↓ in proportion to atrophy
F-wave ↓ / absent in severe cases (✗ Wrong options: ↑)
H-reflex S1 only (✗ Wrong options: cervical)
Prolonged distal latency Peripheral compression (✗ Wrong options: radiculopathy)
Sensitivity 40–70% — not a screening test; not needed with concordant exam + MRI

Selective nerve root blocklocalises level in multilevel disease

5Management

Natural history & non-op care

Natural historyfavourable: 75% (75–90%) improve non-op
✗ Wrong options: stepwise decline = myelopathy · needs PT/surgery to resolve

MedicationsNSAIDs = most appropriate for chronic radicular pain (TO) · brief oral steroids · gabapentin · nortriptyline if depressed (↓ narcotic need)
✗ Wrong options: narcotics · muscle relaxants (habituation, depression) · anticonvulsants

Othershort immobilisation < 1–2 weeks (avoid prolonged → atrophy) · isometrics, heat, traction (not in myelopathy)

Selective nerve root steroid injectionlong-term relief 40–70% (50–70%); steroid + LA = LA alone long term; ↑ risks vs lumbar (dural puncture, meningitis, abscess, root injury)

Axial neck pain only (no root/cord signs, solid old fusion)education + PT
✗ Wrong options: hardware removal · revision · PSF · ESI

Return to playafter symptom resolution + repeat MRI without cord compression; oral methylprednisolone speeds return; no ↑ catastrophic SCI risk after C4-5 HNP · single-level ACDF not a contraindication · collegiate C5 radiculopathy → NSAIDs + PT, RTP after resolution + repeat imaging
✗ Wrong options: immediate RTP on steroids · EMG · surgery

Operative criteria

Indication
Disabling pain after 3 months of non-op care
Progressive / significant neurologic deficit
Static deficit with significant radicular pain

Surgical options

Procedure Indication / outcome
ACDF Gold standard; ventral compression; e.g. 6 months C7 radiculopathy + ventral cord compression (✗ Wrong options: posterior foraminotomy (cord risk) · anterior foraminotomy (vertebral artery) · corpectomy · laminoplasty)
Posterior foraminotomy Foraminal soft disc / osteophyte, single level, prior anterior surgery; > 91% success, ~3% complications, = ACDF outcomes, faster return to work, lower cost · not for central disc, myelopathy, instability, OPLL, kyphosis
Anterior foraminotomy Unilateral root, avoid fusion (smokers, diabetics); uncovertebral resection; vertebral artery + sympathetic chain risk; not widely accepted
CDA Single level, minimal facet arthrosis; = ACDF (arm pain, NDI, SF-36, neuro; PROMs equivalent at 2–5 yrs); ASD + reoperation + dysphagia similar; less neck pain; heterotopic bone 60% (no motion effect)

Best posterior foraminotomy candidateyoung, foraminal soft disc at C6-7, C7 radiculopathy
✗ Wrong options: myelopathy · SCI · midline disc-osteophyte · MS

ACDF technique

ApproachSmith-Robinson · transverse incision 1–2 levels, longitudinal for multilevel / short thick neck

Landmarksmandible C1-2 · hyoid C3-4 · thyroid cartilage C4-6 · cricoid C5-6

Sideleft-sided lower RLN risk theoretically; C7-T1 left → thoracic duct risk · deflate ET cuff after retractor placement (RLN compressed against tube)

Plates↑ fusion in multilevel (↓ graft complications, reoperation, earlier return to work); single-level: no difference · dynamic (controlled settling) vs static (fixed angle) · old Caspar bicortical → neuro risk · screw length > 80% AP → ↓ pseudarthrosis

GraftICBG autograft gold standard (donor pain) · allograft ↑ pseudarthrosis (41% vs 27%) + subsidence (28% vs 16%) (Orthobullets) · no difference single level (OB) — read the options

Post-opwalk day 0, soft collar briefly (hard collar if no plate), ROM at 6 wks, full activity 3 months

6Complications

Pseudarthrosis

Ratessingle level 5–10% (Orthobullets complications) · 20% (Orthobullets outcomes) · 2.6–4.8% (OB) · multilevel 30% / > 50% · doesn't always correlate with outcome

Risk factorssmoking + diabetes (strongest, modifiable) · multilevel · revision (✗ Wrong options: graft type · plate · level · alignment single level) · smoking cessation ≥ 4 weeks pre-op = greatest fusion impact (✗ Wrong options: plate · autograft · collar)

Treatmentasymptomatic → observe · symptomatic → posterior cervical fusion (↑ fusion, ↓ revision; but ↑ blood loss, stay, complications) or anterior revision if radiculopathy (EXCEPT: posterior does NOT ↓ fusion rate)

ACDF complications

Complication Fact
RLN palsy Commonest nerve injury (1–11%; 2.7%); vagus branch; left hooks aortic arch, right hooks subclavian (shorter, crosses lateral → medial higher, can be aberrant) → right theoretically riskier (TO: left approach preferred) · no proven clinical difference (Orthobullets, OB); posterior cricoarytenoid paralysis; observe → ENT at 6 wks (Teflon)
Revision after RLN palsy Same side as prior approach; pre-op laryngoscopy for revision / prior thyroidectomy (bilateral palsy → stridor) · not contralateral approach
Superior laryngeal nerve Runs with superior thyroid artery (upper C-spine); cricothyroid → pitch / voice fatigue
Hypoglossal Upper C-spine (C2-3); tongue deviates toward injured side
Horner Sympathetic chain on lateral longus colli at C6; retractors not under longus colli → ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis, enophthalmos
Vertebral artery Lateral discectomy / uncovertebral bleeding; aberrant medial course (C6)
Dysphagia ↑ at C3-4; zero-profile cages ↓ prominence; local steroid helps
Oesophageal perforation Early: sharp instruments → repair now · late: plate loosening → NG + TPN
Airway > 5 h surgery, exposure above C4, > 4 levels / ≥ 4 bodies (✗ Wrong options: smoking · lung disease · myelopathy · drains)
CSF leak (TO) ~1% (↑ OPLL 12.5%, revision, corpectomy, men); intra-op → watertight repair; post-op → bed rest → lumbar subarachnoid drain → open repair
Adjacent-level ossification Plate < 5 mm from supra-adjacent disc (✗ Wrong options: needle · stripping · longus colli); start screws near inferior endplate of top vertebra ↓ it
ASD ~3%/yr, 30% at 10 yrs

Example C5-6 → C6 L4-5 paracentral → L5, foraminal → L4
Radiculopathy Myelopathy

Cervical Spine Anatomy

Spine · 17 / 69

1Basics

Embryology

Hox (homeobox) genesdirect craniocaudal differentiation + segmentation

Atlas (C1)3 ossification centres: 2 lateral masses (join posteriorly → posterior arch) + anterior arch (appears ~1 yr); lateral masses fuse to body at 7 yrs

Axis (C2)5 centres: 2 neural arches/lateral masses, 2 halves of dens (usually fused at birth; persistence = dens bicornis), body · subdental (basilar / dentocentral) synchondrosis open in all children to 3 yrs, half at 4–5, fused ~6 yrs · secondary tip centre (ossiculum terminale) appears ~3 yrs, fuses ~12 yrs (persistence = ossiculum terminale persistens, insignificant)

3-yr-old with synchondrosis between dens and bodynormal physiologic finding
✗ Wrong options: developmental anomaly · type 1/3 odontoid fracture

Osteology

Feature Levels
Transverse foramen C1–C7 (all subaxial vertebrae in most people)
Vertebral artery passes through C6 → C1; C7 foramen usually empty
Bifid spinous process C2–C6 (C7 not bifid)
Carotid (Chassaignac) tubercle C6 — anterior approach landmark
C8 No vertebra; C8 root exists
Atlas No body, no spinous process

Canalnormal 17 mm; < 13 mm → possible cord compression

Alignmentlordosis measured C2–C7

Facets & kinematics

Subaxial superior facetsface posteromedial at C3 → posterolateral at C7
✗ Wrong options: reverse

Axial viewsuperior articular process is anterior to the inferior articular process

Motiongreatest at cephalad subaxial levels (facet orientation)
✗ Wrong options: caudal

Joint Contribution
Occiput-C1 ~50% of flexion-extension
C1-C2 (diarthrodial) 50° of 100° rotation, 10° of 110° flex-ext, 0° lateral bend
C2–C7 50° flex-ext, 50° rotation, 60° lateral bend

Odontoid blood supply

Watershed between apex (internal carotid branches) and base (vertebral artery branches) → poor healing of type II odontoid fractures

Ligaments

Transverselimits anterior translation of atlas (strongest ligament of spine) · apical + alar — limit rotation

Connects Ligaments (TO)
Atlas → occiput Anterior atlanto-occipital membrane (ALL extension) · posterior atlanto-occipital membrane · ascending band of cruciate
Axis (dens) → occiput Tectorial membrane (PLL extension; C2–3 bodies → FM) · alar (dens → condyles) · apical (dens tip → FM, weak)
Axis → atlas Transverse atlantal ligament · atlanto-alar part of alar · descending band of cruciate

Vertebral artery segments (TO)

Segment Course
V1 Pre-foraminal Subclavian → front of C7 transverse process (between longus colli + scalenus anterior)
V2 Foraminal Transverse foramina C6 → C2
V3 Extradural (atlantic) Above C2 → through C1 transverse foramen → horizontal in occipital triangle → canal
V4 Intradural Pierces dura → anterior medulla → joins opposite → basilar at lower pons

C1 transverse foramen containsextradural segment of vertebral artery + venous plexus + sympathetic plexus (TO)

Medial / enlarged transverse foramen on CT before ACDF↑ vertebral artery injury risk
✗ Wrong options: IJV · ICA · vagus

Pedicle & body dimensions (TO)

Measure Level
Smallest transverse pedicle diameter C3 (5.1 mm); cervical pedicles C2 largest, C3 smallest, ↑ C3 → C7
Narrowest pedicle overall (average) T4
Largest pedicle diameter / longest pedicle L5
Shortest pedicle T10
Largest canal cross-section C2 (374 mm²)
Smallest canal T3–T8
Smallest body C3 + C5 · narrowest body width C4
Tallest / shortest disc L2-3 / T1-2
Lumbar pedicles Angle more medially caudally; L1 smallest lumbar

C1 lateral mass screw

Trajectory10° medial + 22° cephalad · safe even with anomalous vertebral artery that precludes transarticular screws · medial angle protects internal carotid

Muscles

Sternocleidomastoid (TO)one side: rotates head to OPPOSITE side + tilts to same side · both: flex neck + extend head, lift head off pillow, accessory inspiration
✗ Wrong options: rotate to same side (EXCEPT answer)

X vs Y

C1 (atlas) C2 (axis) C7
Body No ✓ + dens Yes
Spinous process No Bifid Non-bifid (prominens)
Ossification centres 3 5 —
Vertebral artery in foramen Yes Yes Usually no

How it's asked

"Structure in C1 transverse foramen?"extradural segment of vertebral artery (TO)

"3-yr-old, synchondrosis between dens and body?"normal

"Subaxial anatomy — true?"all subaxial vertebrae have a foramen transversarium
✗ Wrong options: artery through all · rotation greatest caudally · facets posterolateral → posteromedial caudally · inferior process anterior

"Medially placed, enlarged transverse foramen before ACDF — risk?"vertebral artery injury

"C1 lateral mass screw trajectory?"10° medial, 22° cephalad

"SCM functions EXCEPT?"rotate neck to same side (TO)

"Smallest pedicle diameter (C2–C5)?"C3 (TO)

"Ligament connecting atlas to occiput?"ascending band of cruciate (TO)
✗ Wrong options: tectorial membrane · alar · TAL

Cervical Spine Trauma Evaluation

Spine · 18 / 69

1Basics

Ruleevery trauma patient has a C-spine injury until proven otherwise

Clearanceconfirming absence of injury by exam ± imaging; do it efficiently (delay ↑ complications)

Missed injuriespermanent disability; causes: inadequate imaging of the injured level, LOC, multisystem trauma

Noncontiguous spinal injuries10–15% (Orthobullets) · up to 16% (OB) → image the whole spine once one fracture is found

High-energy markersMVA > 35 mph, fall > 10 ft, closed head injury, neuro deficit, pelvis/limb fractures

Mechanism clueselderly fall onto forehead / high-speed rear-end → hyperextension

Comorbidities to identifyAS, DISH, prior fusion, ligamentous laxity disorders

3Presentation

Primary surveyABC; manual in-line traction whenever collar off for airway; inspect whole spine; seat-belt sign → TL flexion-distraction

Secondary surveyremove collar, inspect face/scalp, head posture (rotation → unilateral facet dislocation), palpate posterior midline (no tenderness in alert patient → low probability of injury), log roll, full neuro exam

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria – clearance

Clinical clearance (no imaging) — ALL of Radiographic clearance — ANY of
Awake, alert, not intoxicated Intoxicated
No neck pain, tenderness or neuro deficit Altered mental status / obtunded
No distracting injury Neck pain or tenderness
— Distracting injury

PaediatricNEXUS-based clinical guidelines → fewer CTs = most effective radiation reduction · < 3 yrs can't cooperate; X-ray screen first, CT if abnormal
✗ Wrong options: fluoroscopy · low-dose CT · avoiding radiographs · MRI for all

Key radiology findings

Study Requirement
Lateral X-ray Must show inferior endplate of C7 (Orthobullets) · top of T1 (OB)
AP + open-mouth odontoid Standard trio
Swimmer's view (TO) Cervicothoracic (C7-T1) junction (✗ Wrong options: Hill-Sachs · Bankart · AO junction)
CT Higher sensitivity, faster; must include superior endplate of T1 (Orthobullets) · inferior endplate of T1 (OB answer) — read the options
MRI Ligament / cord / disc
Flexion-extension Not in acute / known instability or deficit; only to exclude occult instability after clearance

Lateral X-ray linesanterior vertebral line · posterior vertebral body line · spinolaminar line

Prevertebral / retropharyngeal soft tissue (TO) Upper limit
C2 6–7 mm ("above cricoid" → < 7 mm)
C3 7 mm
C6 (cricoid) 18–22 mm (retropharyngeal < 22 mm)
Child at C6 14 mm

Obtunded / intubated after 3 days, X-rays negative, occipital skin erythemadefinitive clearance with CT ± MRI
✗ Wrong options: keep collar until surgeries done · halo · wait to examine

GCS 9, multitraumaCT C-spine
✗ Wrong options: remove collar · wait without imaging · X-rays to C7 only · flex-ext

Burst fracture found (C5 / C7)CT of entire spine before surgery
✗ Wrong options: steroids · flex-ext · MRA · EMG · lumbar MRI

5Management

Collarfrom scene until directed exam

Whiplashrear-end low-speed extension injury, normal neuro + X-rays → soft collar + early ROM / PT (✗ Wrong options: Philadelphia collar · halo · C2 screws) · persistent pain at 4 wks → NSAIDs + activity modification + PT (✗ Wrong options: observation · ESI · facet blocks · MRI)

Injury → procedure (OB)flexion teardrop → anterior corpectomy + strut + plate · AAI with cord compression → posterior C1-2 fusion · bilateral facet dislocation → anterior or posterior reduction + fusion · central cord + spondylosis → ACDF or posterior decompression-fusion · C7 Chance (bony) → halo or posterior fusion

Transient quadriparesis – return to play

Recurrent transient quadriparesis + cervical stenosis on CT/MRIno further contact sport · stenosis < 13 mm, absolute < 10 mm · Torg ratio has low predictive value (Cantu)
✗ Wrong options: neck roll · EMG · ESI · steroid pack

6Complications

Delayed clearanceaspiration, ↓ respiratory function, occipital / submandibular pressure ulcers, ↑ ICP

Prognosis

Elderly (75 vs 60 yrs) incomplete cervical SCI↑ mortality · elderly: lower-energy falls with head strike, fewer deficits
✗ Wrong options: more severe deficits · more paralysis · epidural haematoma · chronic pain

Preadolescent SCI (e.g. 3-yr-old T2/3 paraplegia)≥ 90% develop paralytic scoliosis; bracing doesn't prevent it · uncommon post-adolescence

X vs Y

Clinical clearance Imaging clearance
Mental state Alert, sober Obtunded / intoxicated
Symptoms No pain, tenderness, deficit Pain, tenderness, deficit
Distracting injury None Present

How it's asked

"C5 burst on X-ray, MRI shows retropulsion — before surgery?"CT of entire spine

"Intubated 3 days, X-rays negative, occipital erythema?"definitive CT and/or MRI clearance

"C7 burst on MRI with deficits — next study?"CT cervical, thoracic + lumbar

"Most effective radiation reduction in paediatric trauma?"clinical guidelines → fewer CTs

"GCS 9, collar on, multiple fractures?"CT C-spine to T1

"Injury suited to anterior corpectomy + plate?"flexion teardrop fracture

"< 5 mph rear-end, neck stiffness, normal X-rays?"soft orthosis + early PT

"4 weeks of whiplash pain?"NSAIDs, activity modification, PT

"75- vs 60-yr incomplete SCI — 75-yr has ↑?"mortality

"3-yr-old with complete paraplegia — later deformity?"≥ 90%

"Recurrent transient quadriparesis with stenosis?"stop football

"Normal prevertebral space above cricoid?"< 7 mm (TO)

"Normal retropharyngeal space at cricoid?"< 22 mm (TO)

"Swimmer's view shows?"cervicothoracic junction (TO)

Cervical Spondylosis

Spine · 19 / 69

1Basics

Prevalence & high-risk sports

Onset40–50 yrs · 85% show spondylosis regardless of symptoms: > 65 yrs (Orthobullets) · > 60 yrs (OB) · men > women

LevelsC5-6 > C6-7 (most subaxial flexion-extension) · risk: excessive driving, smoking, lifting, professional athletes

Highest spinal degeneration in sportjockeys (horse racing) · also wrestling, rugby, ski-jumping, gymnastics
✗ Wrong options: badminton · baseball · volleyball · golf

Pathoanatomy

Motion segmentdisc + 2 facet joints + 2 uncovertebral joints (Luschka)

Degenerative cascade
  • Disc→desiccation, height loss, bulge / herniation
  • Joints→uncinate spurs + facet arthrosis
  • Ligaments→LF thickening + infolding (height loss)
  • Deformity → kyphosis → load shifts to facets/uncovertebral→more spurs

Clinical syndromesaxial / discogenic neck pain · radiculopathy · myelopathy

2Mechanism

Root compression (radiculopathy)foraminal spurs (facet + uncovertebral) · posterolateral disc / disc-osteophyte between uncinate and lateral PLL · foraminal soft disc → exiting root (C6-7 → C7)

Central stenosis (myelopathy)canal < 13 mm (normal 17) · worse in extension: cord pinched between disc anteriorly and facets + infolded LF posteriorly

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Findings
X-ray Uncovertebral + facet degeneration, osteophytes, disc narrowing, endplate sclerosis, ↓ sagittal diameter; poor symptom correlation · lateral: alignment, canal size
Oblique Foraminal stenosis (uncovertebral arthrosis)
Flexion-extension Angular / translational instability; compensatory subluxation next to stiff segment
MRI (axial) Modality of choice; disc degeneration, myelomalacia; 28% false positives > 40 yrs
CT myelogram Bony detail + neural status; pacemaker / hardware
Discography Rarely; oesophageal puncture, discitis

5Management

Treatmentby pain severity/chronicity, instability, neuro deficit: observation → medical → surgery

Axial neck pain only, normal neuro (70-yr, 2 months)counsel that 85% of > 60s show these X-ray findings
✗ Wrong options: surgery to prevent decline · MRI for radiculopathy (clinical diagnosis) · "< 10% improve non-op"

How it's asked

"Sport with highest cervical + lumbar degeneration?"jockeys

"70-yr axial neck pain, spondylotic X-ray, no deficits — counsel?"85% of > 60-yr-olds have these findings

"Commonest level?"C5-6

Chance Fracture (Flexion-Distraction Injury)

Spine · 20 / 69

1Basics

Definitionthoracolumbar flexion-distraction (seat-belt / jack-knife) injury; described by GQ Chance 1948 (purely osseous)

LevelT12–L2 / T12–L3 (transitional zone; TO: D12–L2)

Typesbony (fracture through spinous process → lamina → pedicles → body; heals well) · ligamentous / discoligamentous (through disc + ligaments; heals poorly) · mixed

Neuro deficitrelatively low incidence (TO)

Associated intra-abdominal injuries (lap belt)

GI injury ~50%commonest associated injury (✗ Wrong options: head injury · pulmonary contusion · pelvis · GU) · colon rupture, small bowel / duodenal tear (✗ Wrong options: tamponade · bladder rupture · cholecystitis)

Seat-belt sign (abdominal ecchymosis) → suspect flexion-distraction injury + bowel injury · lap-only belts (old cars, rear seat, children)
✗ Wrong options: L5 TP fracture (pelvic ring) · burst · pars · HNP

2Mechanism

Axis of rotationanterior to the spine (abdominal wall / abdominal viscera) — lap belt acts as fulcrum
✗ Wrong options: ISL · PLL · ALL

Column failureposterior + middle columns fail in tension (distraction) · anterior column: fails in compression (Orthobullets, OB) · "no crushing of the body" (TO) — TO answer: middle + posterior columns fail in distraction

Posterior column failure modetension
✗ Wrong options: torsion · shear · compression

Pure ligamentous variant (X-ray)splayed spinous processes + ↑ posterior disc height, no body fracture

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP + lateral Interspinous widening, horizontal fracture line, empty pedicle sign on AP
Flexion-extension Instability
CT Bony extent; retropulsion
MRI Posterior ligamentous complex (PLC) — ↑ T2 in ISL / PLL = ligamentous injury

AO thoracolumbar classification (TO)

Type Pattern
A0 Spinous / transverse process fracture
A1 Wedge, one endplate, posterior wall intact
A2 Split, both endplates, posterior wall intact
A3 Incomplete burst — one endplate + posterior wall
A4 Complete burst — both endplates + posterior wall
B1 Monosegmental osseous posterior tension band failure = bony Chance
B2 Osseoligamentous posterior tension band disruption (± bone; ISL T2 signal; heals slower)
B3 Hyperextension — anterior tension band failure (ankylosed spines)
C Translation / dislocation (no subtypes; all operative)

Neuro modifierN0 intact · N1 transient · N2 radicular · N3 incomplete SCI / cauda equina · N4 complete · NX unknown · "+" ongoing compression

Case modifiersM1 indeterminate tension band injury · M2 comorbidity (AS, skin, rheumatologic)

B1 vs B2B1: CT line through spinous process, MRI posterior elements may look normal · B2: ISL hyperintense, body comminution favours B2

Operative criteria (TO – TLICS)

TLICS component Points
Distraction mechanism 4
PLC injury suspected / confirmed 2 / 3
Neuro intact 0
Total ≥ 5 Surgery (≤ 3 non-op)

5Management

Chance / flexion-distraction injury
  • Purely bony, PLC intact, neuro intact, kyphosis < 15–20°→HYPEREXTENSION CAST / TLSO (12–16 wks)
    (serial X-rays for nonunion + kyphosis)
  • Ligamentous / mixed (PLC torn)→POSTERIOR COMPRESSION INSTRUMENTATION + FUSION
    (1 level above + 1 below with pedicle screws)
  • Neuro deficit→DECOMPRESSION + STABILISATION

Non-op union> 90% in selected bony injuries (cancellous bone heals)

Surgical constructcompression construct for Chance · historically 3 above + 2 below · modern pedicle screws 1 above + 1 below (short segment ± index screws)
✗ Wrong options: distraction (that's for burst)

Anteriorvertebrectomy + strut + instrumentation (decompression)

Laminectomyonly for compressive deficit; further destabilises posterior column

Bowel perforationlaparotomy first; spine staged or same sitting

Paediatric soft-tissue Chance (8-yr, L3-4, CT negative for bone)L3-4 posterior instrumented fusion
✗ Wrong options: percutaneous screws (bony only) · TLSO · L1-5 fusion + laminectomy · PLIF

Bony-ligamentous L2 (PLC + PLL signal, through superior endplate), ASIA E (TO)posterior short-segment instrumentation
✗ Wrong options: cast (not purely osseous) · surgery justified by seat-belt sign · TLSO because neuro intact

6Complications

Complication Note
Pain Commonest
Progressive kyphosis Unrecognised PLL/PLC injury
Scoliosis, flat back Forward-flexed posture, fatigue
Post-traumatic syringomyelia —
Nonunion Ligamentous / non-op

How it's asked

"Lap-belt bruising + Chance fracture — commonest associated injury?"colonic / GI injury

"Seat-belt sign, GCS 8 — suspect?"flexion-distraction injury

"Lap belt + small bowel tear — spinal injury?"flexion-distraction

"Lap belt, posterior column involved — likely associated injury?"gastrointestinal

"Mechanism of Chance fracture?"flexion-distraction (TO)

"Columns failing in Chance fracture?"middle + posterior in distraction (TO)

"Posterior column failure mode?"tension

"Centre of rotation, ligamentous Chance?"abdominal viscera (anterior to spine)

"8-yr soft-tissue Chance L3-4, no deficit?"L3-4 posterior instrumented fusion

"Bony Chance — AO type?"B1 (TO)

"L2 bony-ligamentous Chance with PLC signal, ASIA E?"posterior short-segment instrumentation (TO)

Congenital Muscular Torticollis

Spine · 21 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest cause of infantile torticollis0.3–2.0%
✗ Wrong options: Klippel-Feil · Chiari · AARD · paroxysmal torticollis

SexM:F 3:2 · mostly right side (TO)

Risk factorsfirst pregnancy, breech, traumatic delivery, oligohydramnios (packaging)

Pathophysiology

Lesionsternocleidomastoid (SCM) contracture / fibromatosis
✗ Wrong options: platysma · omohyoid · longus colli · trapezius

Theoriescompression/stretch injury · venous outflow obstruction → compartment syndrome of SCM

Masspalpable at birth or within 4 weeks, middle–lower third of sternal head; regresses over months → fibrous cord

Associated packaging disorders

Condition Note
DDH 5–15% → examine hips
Metatarsus adductus ✓
Calcaneovalgus feet ✓
Plagiocephaly Contralateral side
Congenital atlanto-occipital anomalies ✓

Anatomy – SCM

Originsternal head (manubrium) + clavicular head (medial third of clavicle)

Insertionmastoid process + lateral occipital bone

Nervespinal accessory (CN XI) — at risk in release

Actionipsilateral tilt/flexion + contralateral rotation of the chin

3Presentation

Posturehead tilt TOWARD affected SCM + chin rotated AWAY ("cock-robin")

Painless passive motion · older child: tight band, ↓ rotation + lateral flexion

Examvision, hearing, neuro, hips, feet, spine

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Indication / finding
Ultrasound Modality of choice with palpable mass + ↓ ROM; enlarged hyperechoic (fibrotic) SCM; separates CMT from serious causes; follows fibrosis over time
X-ray C-spine Tilt/rotation without a mass; exclude bony causes
Dynamic CT C1-2 Exclude AARD
MRI brain + C-spine Non-muscular / central causes

5Management

Congenital muscular torticollis
  • < 1 yr, < 30° ROM loss→PASSIVE STRETCHING (90–95% respond in year 1)
    (tilt away + rotate chin toward affected side)
  • Failed ≥ 1 yr stretching→BIPOLAR RELEASE / Z-LENGTHENING of SCM

Surgical timingafter ≥ 1 yr failed stretching; 92% success even in older children; facial asymmetry improves if done before 10 yrs (Orthobullets) · after 3–4 yrs — release before 3 yrs → deep painful scarring (TO)

Techniqueshort proximal incision behind ear (mastoid) ± distal sternal/clavicular incision · unipolar (clavicular) or bipolar (preferred); Z-plasty clavicular head + mastoid release · CN XI at risk · botox for refractory (OB)

6Complications

Untreatedpermanent rotational deformity, positional plagiocephaly, craniofacial asymmetry / hemihypoplasia, compensatory scoliosis

X vs Y

CMT AARD / Grisel Klippel-Feil Paroxysmal torticollis of infancy
Pain Painless Painful Painless Episodic
Mass SCM mass No No No
SCM spasm vs chin Opposite side (SCM primary) Same side (secondary) — —
Key Breech, first-born, DDH URTI / trauma Short webbed neck, low hairline, ↓ ROM Vestibular; pallor, ataxia; resolves by 2–3 yrs
Rx Stretching Collar → traction → fusion — Self-limiting

How it's asked

"True about SCM contracture?"surgery before 3 yrs → deep painful scarring (TO)
✗ Wrong options: disappears after infancy · best age 1–2 · unipolar sternal release suffices

"Treatment of choice in infancy?"passive stretching (TO)

"Muscle implicated?"sternocleidomastoid

"6-week infant, head tilt since birth + neck mass — commonest cause?"congenital muscular torticollis

"Imaging with palpable mass?"ultrasound

"Associated condition to screen?"DDH

Congenital Scoliosis

Spine · 22 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology & aetiology

Definitionfailure of normal vertebral development at 4–6 weeks of gestation (mesenchymal anlage) — same window as heart + kidneys

Prevalence1–4%

Causesmostly sporadic; maternal diabetes, alcohol, valproic acid, hyperthermia · environmental / vascular disruption

Genes & inheritance

Familial inheritancenot associated → "deformity NOT familial" = congenital scoliosis (e.g. child with myelomeningocele) · genetics uncertain
✗ Wrong options: unlike AIS

Jarcho-Levin (spondylocostal dysostosis)most commonly autosomal recessive

Associated anomalies

System Rate
Any systemic anomaly Up to 60–61%
Cardiac 10% (Orthobullets) · 25–26% (OB) — VSD commonest, then ASD, PDA, ToF, TGA
Genitourinary 25% (Orthobullets) · 20% / up to one-third (OB) — unilateral absent kidney, horseshoe, obstructive uropathy, duplicate ureters, hypospadias
Intraspinal (neural axis) 20–40% (Orthobullets) · 37% (OB) · 18% pre-MRI era — diastematomyelia, tethered cord, syrinx, Chiari, intradural lipoma, dural bands; ↑ with segmentation + mixed defects
Orthopaedic Clubfoot, DDH, limb hypoplasia, Sprengel, Klippel-Feil, vertical talus, cavus, thumb hypoplasia / radial club hand
Syndrome Features
VACTERL (38–55%) Vertebral, anal atresia, cardiac, TE fistula, renal, radial / limb
Goldenhar (oculo-auriculo-vertebral) Hemifacial microsomia + epibulbar dermoids
Jarcho-Levin Short-trunk dwarfism, multiple vertebral + rib fusions → thoracic insufficiency syndrome
Klippel-Feil Short neck, low hairline, cervical fusion
Alagille Peripheral pulmonic stenosis, cholestasis, facial dysmorphism

Not associatedSever's disease (calcaneal apophysitis)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Classification

Category Type Note
Failure of formation Fully segmented hemivertebra Disc + growth plate above and below → progresses; commonest anomaly (38%) (TO)
Semisegmented Fused one side
Unsegmented Fused both sides
Incarcerated Within margins of adjacent vertebrae → little progression
Unincarcerated Laterally placed
Wedge / butterfly —
Failure of segmentation Block vertebra Least progression (< 2°/yr)
Unilateral unsegmented bar Common, likely to progress
Mixed Unilateral bar + contralateral hemivertebra Most rapid progression

Progression risk

Anomaly (fastest → slowest) Rate
1 Unilateral bar + contralateral (convex) hemivertebra 5–10°/yr (Orthobullets) · 6–7°/yr, most > 50° by age 2 (TO)
2 Unilateral unsegmented bar ~5°/yr; > 50° by 10, > 70° at maturity
(Double convex hemivertebra) 3–4°/yr (TO)
3 Fully segmented hemivertebra 1–2°/yr
4 Unincarcerated hemivertebra —
5 Incarcerated hemivertebra —
6 Unsegmented hemivertebra —
7 Block vertebra < 2°/yr

Timingfastest in first 3 years · fused ribs ↑ progression

Anterior failure of formation (kyphosis)progressive sagittal deformity + paralysis · anterior failure of segmentation → progressive but rarely paralysis

Key radiology findings & work-up

Study Purpose
AP + lateral X-ray Usually diagnostic
3D CT Posterior anatomy, surgical planning (judicious radiation)
MRI whole spine All before surgery; immediately if hairy patch / neuro signs (sedation in infants → may defer if no surgery planned)
Renal US (or MRI) All patients — "renal US indicated in?" → congenital scoliosis
Echocardiogram Cardiac evaluation

New congenital scoliosis in infantecho + renal US (+ spinal MRI) first (✗ Wrong options: HLA-B27 · bone scan · skeletal survey · EMG · brace · surgery yet) · MRI already normal → renal US next (✗ Wrong options: blood cultures · flex-ext C-spine · platelets)

5Management

Observationnon-progressive: incarcerated, non-segmented, some partially segmented hemivertebrae; serial X-rays every 4–6 months; document progression before surgery

BracingNOT effective for congenital curves; may control supple compensatory curves · derotational casting may delay growing constructs
✗ Wrong options: primary treatment

Congenital scoliosis
  • Unilateral bar + contralateral hemivertebra→SURGERY AT ANY AGE
    (hemivertebra excision + short-segment posterior fusion)
  • Progressive fully segmented hemivertebra, young→HEMIVERTEBRECTOMY + SHORT FUSION
  • Unilateral bar, minimal deformity→IN-SITU FUSION
  • Multiple fused ribs (> 4) / thoracic insufficiency→VEPTR / rib osteotomies / growing rods

Operative criteria & procedures

Procedure Indication
Hemivertebra excision + short posterior fusion Progressive hemivertebra (esp. lumbosacral → oblique takeoff, truncal imbalance); best < 5–6 yrs, flexible < 40° (Orthobullets) · ages 3–8 (posterior; younger = poor anchors) · indication = documented progression (✗ Wrong options: failed bracing · Cobb > 45° · Risser 4 · maturity)
In-situ arthrodesis (A/P or posterior) Unilateral bars with minimal deformity
Posterior fusion ± osteotomy Hemivertebra opposite bar not needing vertebrectomy; older patients with progression/deficit/respiratory decline; many pedicle screws ↓ crankshaft
A/P fusion ± vertebrectomy Young (girls < 10, boys < 12) with progression, deficit, respiratory decline
Convex hemiepiphysiodesis Concave growth intact, < 5 yrs, < 40–50°; mixed results
Distraction growing rods Control deformity during growth, delay fusion; lengthen ~6-monthly
VEPTR / rib osteotomies > 4 fused ribs, thoracic insufficiency syndrome (may stiffen chest)
Spinal column shortening resection Late, severe, rigid deformity; fixed pelvic obliquity

22-month / 14-month with bar + contralateral hemivertebra (~40°), normal MRIhemivertebrectomy + short-segment posterior instrumented fusion
✗ Wrong options: observe · brace · Risser cast · VEPTR · growing rods

7-yr, hemivertebra curve ↑ 10°hemivertebra excision + limited fusion

18-month, lumbar hemivertebraecho + renal US first; no long TL fusion (crankshaft, limits growth)

Congenital kyphosis

Level (TO)T7–T9 (gibbus) · associations: MPS, SED congenita, Morquio, pseudoachondroplasia

Type Prognosis
I – failure of formation (anterior) Worst; relentless progression → paraplegia
II – failure of segmentation Rounded, slower, paraplegia uncommon

Treatment (TO)no bracing · < 5 yrs + < 50–55° → posterior in-situ fusion (anterior growth continues → some correction) · > 5 yrs, > 55°, or neuro deficit → anterior release/decompression + strut + posterior instrumented fusion (neuromonitoring)

Supports early in-situ posterior fusion (8-month)50° curve, failure of formation, bar, neuro weakness
✗ Wrong options: block vertebra (EXCEPT — least progression)

6Complications

Complication Note
Crankshaft Posterior fusion alone in young
Short stature Fusion limits trunk growth (younger worse)
Neurologic injury Overdistraction / shortening, overcorrection, segmental vessel ligation → SSEP + MEP
Soft tissue Optimise nutrition pre-op

X vs Y

Congenital Idiopathic (AIS)
Cause Vertebral anomaly (4–6 wks) Unknown, familial
Inheritance No Yes
Brace Ineffective Effective 25–45°
Work-up Echo + renal US + MRI MRI only if atypical
Anterior failure of formation Anterior failure of segmentation
Deformity Sharp kyphosis Rounded kyphosis
Paralysis Often Rare

How it's asked

"LEAST likely association?"Sever's disease

"Infant with congenital scoliosis + hairy patch, one kidney?"MRI of entire spine

"Screening tests with congenital vertebral anomalies?"renal US + echo + spinal MRI

"2-yr, progressed 15°, MRI normal — additional study?"renal ultrasound

"18-month, new congenital scoliosis — next step?"echocardiogram + renal US

"Most progressive anomaly?"unilateral bar with convex hemivertebra (TO)

"Worst prognosis?"hemivertebra with contralateral bar (TO)

"Pattern with worst prognosis, surgical indication?"hemivertebra + contralateral bar

"Anterior failure of formation leads to?"progressive sagittal deformity + paralysis

"8-month congenital kyphosis — EXCEPT for early fusion?"block vertebra

Degenerative Spondylolisthesis

Spine · 23 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitionanterior slip with an intact pars (Wiltse type III) (TO)

Prevalence~5% men, ~9% women (5–9%)

Demographicswomen > 40 (~8×; 5–10× in OB), African Americans, diabetics; hormonal ligamentous laxity

LevelL4-5 (5× other levels)
✗ Wrong options: L5-S1 = isthmic

Risk factorssacralisation of L5 (transitional L5) · sagittally oriented facets

Pathoanatomy

Cascadedisc degeneration → facet capsule degeneration → microinstability → macroinstability → anterolisthesis (worse with congenitally sagittal facets)

Graderarely > Meyerding II (posterior arch intact → canal narrows as body slides)

Wiltse classification (TO)

Type Name
I Dysplastic (no pars defect)
II Isthmic (lytic, elongated, acute pars)
III Degenerative
IV Traumatic (non-pars arch fracture)
V Pathologic (e.g. OI)
VI Iatrogenic (added later)

2Mechanism

Stenosis L4-5 slip compresses Cause
Central + lateral recess Traversing L5 Slip, LF hypertrophy, facet osteophytes
Foraminal Exiting L4 Vertical: disc height loss + posterolateral body osteophyte pushes root against pedicle · AP: superior facet + posterior body changes

3Presentation

Back paincommonest; mechanical, relieved by rest/sitting · start-up pain (sit → stand) suggests instability

Neurogenic claudication2nd commonest: buttock/leg pain with walking upright, relieved by sitting / flexing (shopping cart), not by standing still · stationary bike tolerated

L4quadriceps (sit-to-stand), ↓ patellar reflex, dorsiflexion (heel walk)

L5dorsiflexion (heel walk), EHL, gluteus medius (hip abduction) · EHL weakness = commonest documented deficit (L5)

Hamstring tightnesscommon; separate from neurogenic pain · cauda equina very rare

Neurogenic vs vascular claudication

Neurogenic Vascular
Relief Sitting / flexion Standing still
Stationary bike Tolerated Painful
Pulses / ABI Normal (ABI > 0.9) ↓
Walking test Stop + stand → no relief; sit → relief Stop + stand → relief

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
Standing lateral + flexion-extension Slip; instability = 4 mm translation or 10° angulation vs adjacent segment
MRI (T2 sagittal + axial) Persistent leg pain after failed non-op; neural compression
CT Bony pathology; most reliable for pseudarthrosis after fusion
CT myelogram MRI contraindicated (pacemaker)

5Management

Non-op (most)activity restriction, NSAIDs, PT · ESI second-line

Operative criteria

Indication for surgery
Persistent incapacitating pain after 6 months non-op incl. ESIs (commonest)
Progressive motor deficit
Cauda equina syndrome
Active smoker → smoking cessation first (continue PT)
Symptomatic L4-5 DS failing 6 months non-op
  • Standard→DECOMPRESSION + INSTRUMENTED POSTEROLATERAL FUSION (± TLIF/PLIF)
  • Medically frail, can't tolerate fusion→DECOMPRESSION ALONE
    (~31% progressive instability)
  • Revision pseudarthrosis→ALIF

SPORTsurgery → greater pain relief + better function at 4 yrs vs non-op

Herkowitz & Kurzdecompression + fusion 90% good/excellent vs 44% decompression alone · satisfaction ~79% with fusion vs ~69% decompression alone

Not appropriatedecompression alone (iatrogenic instability, ~30% progress) · microdiscectomy · TDR (doesn't address facet pain generator) · stand-alone ALIF (suboptimal fusion, cage extrusion; doesn't decompress) · fusion alone (must decompress lateral recess + foramen) · uninstrumented fusion (↑ nonunion)

Fusion technique & outcomes

Pedicle screws↓ pseudarthrosis (↑ fusion rate); outcome benefit inferred, not directly proven · slip reduction: limited role in adults, not correlated with outcome

Solid fusionmost important for long-term relief of back + leg pain (short-term relief similar with pseudarthrosis)
✗ Wrong options: interbody device · rhBMP-2 · preserving facet capsules

Interbody cage (TLIF/PLIF)↑ hospital cost without ↑ fusion, ODI, SF-36 or LOS (OB) · "may ↑ fusion + ↓ slip progression" (Orthobullets) — read the options

Cortical bone trajectory (CBT) screwsstart more medial + caudal, aim cephalad + lateral, smaller screws · ↓ blood loss, smaller incision, ↓ 1-wk pain; fusion + function = pedicle screws; usually with PLIF/TLIF · e.g. single-level PLIF with local autograft + CBT screws

Fusion after laminectomy (no DS/scoliosis)if destabilised: pars fractured or whole facet removed
✗ Wrong options: dural tear repair · 2 levels · smoker · < half facets removed

Smoking

Smoking↑ pseudarthrosis, infection, reoperation; ↓ functional outcome scores
✗ Wrong options: LOS · airway after corpectomy · haematoma

Post-op cessation ≥ 6 months → ↑ fusion rate vs continued smoking, but risk stays above non-smokers whether quitting pre- or post-op · pre-op cessation ≥ 4 wks ↓ wound complications · multilevel fusion ↑ nonunion risk in smokers

6Complications

Complication Fact
Pseudarthrosis 5–30%; CT best (✗ Wrong options: MRI · flex-ext (miss 60–80%) · bone scan · ESR/CRP)
Adjacent segment disease 30–40% radiographic (Orthobullets) · 20–29% need surgery at 10 yrs (18% at 8 yrs SPORT) · ↑ with laminectomy at cranial adjacent level (✗ Wrong options: 1-level fusion · DS itself · obesity)
SSI 0.1–2%; I&D + retain hardware (stability aids eradication) (✗ Wrong options: aspiration · antibiotics alone · remove hardware)
SSI risk factors Age > 60–65, diabetes, smoking, ↑ BMI, alcohol, blood loss, operative time, levels, staged A/P, prior SSI (✗ Wrong options: previous spine surgery (EXCEPT))
LFCN palsy Prone iliac bolster → lateral thigh numbness (meralgia paraesthetica)
Ulnar nerve / brachial plexopathy Arm positioning
Dural tear —
In-hospital complications Age (strongest), ≥ 3 comorbidities, blood loss, operative time, levels fused (✗ Wrong options: gender, race, hospital size)

Adjacent-level symptoms not matching MRI (vague radicular pain, L5/S1 weakness, L3-4 degeneration)EMG before surgery

X vs Y

Degenerative Isthmic
Level L4-5 L5-S1
Pars Intact Defect
Sex Female Male
Root Traversing L5 (+ exiting L4 foraminal) Exiting L5
Canal Narrowed Widened
Grade ≤ II Can be high

How it's asked

"L4/5 DS, failed 6 months incl. ESIs — next?"decompression + fusion
✗ Wrong options: TDR · microdiscectomy · decompression alone · fusion alone

"Failed non-op, flexion-extension + MRI DS?"laminectomy + partial facetectomy + foraminotomy + instrumented PSF

"DS with EHL weakness, failed ESIs?"decompressive laminectomy + posterior instrumented fusion

"Pacemaker, CT myelogram DS, ABI 0.95, bikes painlessly?"decompression + instrumented fusion

"70-yr, trouble heel-walking, failed non-op?"laminectomy + instrumented fusion

"SPORT — surgery vs non-op at 4 yrs?"greater pain relief + improved function

"Greatest effect on pain, function, ODI (4 mm translation)?"decompression + instrumented fusion

"Laminectomy for stenosis — fuse if?"pars fractured

"Best long-term improvement?"open laminectomy + instrumented fusion

"Pedicle screws in posterolateral fusion?"↓ pseudarthrosis

"Most important for long-term relief?"solid L4/5 fusion

"Fusion vs pseudarthrosis outcome?"better long-term back + leg pain relief

"Interbody spacer adds?"increased hospital cost

"↓ blood loss + incision, high fusion?"PLIF with local autograft + cortical trajectory screws

"Smoker quits post-op — nonunion risk?"still higher than non-smokers

"Smoking associated with?"lower functional scores

"Post-op cessation needed to ↑ fusion?"≥ 6 months

"Heavy smoker, failed 8 months non-op, no deficit?"continue PT + smoking cessation

"Six-week wound erythema, ↑ ESR, dorsal collection?"debridement, delayed closure, retain hardware

"SSI risk factors EXCEPT?"previous spine surgery

"Recurrent pain 1 yr after fusion, normal labs — confirm diagnosis?"CT lumbar spine

"Complication risk factors?"age + blood loss + levels fused (all)

"Lateral thigh numbness after prone fusion?"positioning (LFCN)

"Highest risk of ASD needing surgery?"laminectomy at cranial adjacent level

"Need for adjacent-level surgery at 10 yrs?"20–29%

"Expected weakness (L4-5 DS)?"EHL
