Disc Space Infection - Pediatric

Spine · 24 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Whochildren > adults · males · usually < 5 yrs

Wherelumbar spine (50–60%)

Pathophysiology

Why childrenvessels run from cartilaginous endplate into nucleus pulposus → direct haematogenous seeding of disc → spreads to endplate/body · adults: vessels reach annulus only → isolated discitis rare

Organism Note
Staph aureus > 80%
TB Always consider — esp. not improving on first-line antibiotics
Salmonella Sickle cell

3Presentation

Toddlerlimp / refuses to sit or walk, loss of appetite, abdominal pain, fever only 25%

Older childback pain with point tenderness, ↓ ROM

4Diagnosis & Classification

Genes & lab markers

Test Fact
ESR, CRP, WBC High-normal or mildly ↑; WBC normal in ~45%
CRP Most sensitive for monitoring treatment (half-life 19 h) (✗ Wrong options: ESR (3 wks to normalise) · WBC · blood cultures · procalcitonin)
Blood cultures Identify organism (often negative)

Key radiology findings

X-rayunreliable; earliest changes at ~1 week

Order Finding Timing
1 (earliest) Loss of lumbar lordosis Before bony change
2 Disc space narrowing 10–21 days (Orthobullets) · from 1 wk (OB)
3 Endplate erosion 10–21 days / 1–3 wks
Later "Sawtooth" erosion 3–4 wks · endplate scalloping Long-standing
Healed Vertebra magna, permanent height loss, block vertebra (spontaneous fusion) Resolved

MRIdiagnostic test of choice

5Management

Paediatric discitis
  • Early, no abscess / thecal sac displacement→ADMIT: BED REST + IV ANTI-STAPH ABX
    (7–10 d IV → total 4–6 wks; ± immobilisation; follow CRP)
  • No response at 7–10 d→CT-GUIDED BIOPSY (rule out TB)
  • Late / abscess + deficit / failed non-op→SURGICAL DEBRIDEMENT + ABX + BRACE

Bacteraemic 5-yr, no collectionadmit, bed rest + IV antibiotics
✗ Wrong options: oral antibiotics at home · surgery · cast alone (slow, recurrent)

IV antibioticsspeed resolution + ↓ recurrence

6Complications

Long-termdisc space narrowing, interbody fusion, back pain

X vs Y

Paediatric discitis Spondylolysis
Labs ↑ ESR/CRP Normal
Exam Pain flexion + extension, refuses to walk Pain with extension (gymnast)
Rx IV antibiotics Activity restriction ± brace

How it's asked

"Disc space infection in children — all true EXCEPT?"loss of disc height is the earliest sign (TO) — earliest = loss of lordosis

"Order of radiographic findings?"loss of lordosis → disc narrowing → endplate erosion

"6-yr gymnast, ↑ ESR/CRP, discitis on MRI — true?"IV antibiotics usually resolve it
✗ Wrong options: urgent debridement · bracing first line (spondylolysis)

"Best marker to monitor antibiotic response?"CRP

"5-yr, gram-positive bacteraemia, no collection?"admit for bed rest + IV antibiotics

Discogenic Back Pain

Spine · 25 / 69

1Basics

Definitionaxial low back pain from disc degeneration, no radicular symptoms

Biologydisc characteristics modified by bone morphogenetic proteins

3Presentation

Pain ↑ withbending, sitting, axial loading · flexion aggravates, extension relieves

ExamSLR / nerve tension negative, neuro normal

Acute lumbago (3 wks), no red flagsPT + NSAIDs, no imaging · red flags: diabetes / IVDU (infection), cancer, smoking, night pain
✗ Wrong options: X-ray · MRI · discography · steroid taper

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray Best initial study — degeneration, vacuum phenomenon; excludes other causes
MRI Degenerated discs without significant stenosis / herniation
Provocative discography Primary confirmatory test but accelerates degeneration: ↑ herniation, ↓ disc height, reactive endplate changes (✗ Wrong options: myelogram · TFESI · spinal anaesthesia)

Diagnostic criteria – positive discogram

Criterion
Concordant pain on injection
Abnormal disc morphology on fluoroscopy / post-discography CT
Negative (painless) control level(s)

5Management

First line (majority)NSAIDs, PT, cognitive therapy, lifestyle change

Fusion vs cognitive-exercise therapyequivalent ODI improvement at 1 + 10 yrs
✗ Wrong options: fusion superior · therapy superior

Fusioncontroversial; poor results when based on positive discography

Lumbar total disc replacement (TDR)

Indicationsingle-level DDD with disease-free facets (controversial)

TDR vs fusion / ALIF Finding
Safety + efficacy at 2 yrs Comparable / better
Hospital stay Shorter
Reoperation Lower
Disc height restoration Greater
Subsidence Less
Adjacent segment disease Lower

Contraindicationspars defect, fracture, facet arthrosis, spondylolisthesis, posterior element insufficiency, prior laminectomy, scoliosis, instability

Metal-on-metal TDR↑ serum cobalt + chromium, like MoM THA; persist; renally excreted
✗ Wrong options: transient · only cobalt · hepatic neutralisation · negligible

Failed lumbar TDR
  • Asymptomatic subsidence, no extrusion→OBSERVE
  • Facet pain / micro-instability, implant well placed→POSTERIOR STABILISATION
  • Poly dislocation / malposition→REVISION ARTHROPLASTY or ARTHRODESIS VIA RETROPERITONEAL APPROACH
    (not far-lateral — iliac crest blocks L5-S1)

X vs Y

Discogenic pain Radiculopathy
Pain Axial Leg > back
SLR Negative Positive
Worse with Sitting, bending, loading Sitting/cough (HNP)

How it's asked

"3 weeks of lumbago, no red flags?"PT + NSAIDs

"Best initial study for discogenic pain?"radiography

"MoM vs poly TDR — labs?"↑ cobalt + chromium like MoM THA

"TDR vs fusion for single-level DDD?"comparable safety + efficacy at 2 yrs

"Anterior poly dislocation 6 wks after TDR?"revision to arthrodesis via retroperitoneal approach

"Fusion vs cognitive/exercise therapy?"equivalent improvement

"Procedure accelerating disc degeneration?"provocative discography

DISH (Diffuse Idiopathic Skeletal Hyperostosis)

Spine · 26 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

AlsoForestier disease; enthesopathy of spine + extremities; ossification of the anterior longitudinal ligament (TO: non-inflammatory, "degenerative")

Incidence6–12% overall · uncommon < 50 · > 50: 25% M / 15% F · > 80: 28% M / 26% F · non-diabetics 1.6–13% vs diabetics 13–49%

Racecommonest in Caucasians; less in Black, Native American, Asian
✗ Wrong options: "most common in African Americans" (EXCEPT answer)

Locationthoracic (right side) > cervical > lumbar · thoracic T7–T11, often isolated · right-sided thoracic because the pulsatile aorta protects the left · cervical + lumbar symmetric

Genes & lab markers

Marker Fact
HLA-B8 Associated (esp. DISH + diabetes)
HLA-B27 Not associated (✗ Wrong options: "90% B27 positive")
↑ HbA1c / diabetes Image showing DISH → elevated HbA1c
↑ Growth hormone + IGF-1 ✓
ESR Normal

Risk factorsgout, hyperlipidaemia, diabetes, coronary artery disease

Associated conditions

Lumbarspinal stenosis

Cervical (large anterior osteophytes)dysphagia, stridor, hoarseness, sleep apnoea, difficult intubation, myelopathy

Fractureankylosed segments → long lever arms; hyperextension injuries; low-energy falls → unstable fractures

Extra-spinalelbow and other entheses · heterotopic ossification after THA 30–50% (vs < 20% without DISH)

3Presentation

Often asymptomatic (incidental)

Thoracolumbarmild chronic back pain (ankylosis stabilises) + stiffness worse in morning + cold weather

Cervicalpain, stiffness, dysphagia (food "stuck"), stridor, hoarseness, sleep apnoea

Exam↓ spinal ROM; myelopathy / stenosis signs

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria

Criterion (Resnick)
Flowing ossification along anterolateral aspect of ≥ 4 contiguous vertebrae (= non-marginal syndesmophytes at 3 successive levels)
Preserved disc height; little degenerative change (no marginal sclerosis, no vacuum)
No facet ankylosis; no SI joint erosion / sclerosis / fusion

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
X-ray "Flowing candle wax" non-marginal ossification; thoracic right-sided; lateral C-spine: anterior bone with preserved disc space (best view to separate from AS)
Bone scan ↑ uptake — may mimic metastases
CT Any neck pain after trauma → rule out occult fracture
MRI Ligament / cord injury

Dysphagia + neck stiffness in a diabeticX-rays of cervical, thoracic, lumbar spine first
✗ Wrong options: HLA-B27 · MRI first · ESI · inflammatory labs

Osteophyte vs syndesmophyte vs DISH (TO)

Structure Origin / orientation
Traction osteophyte Small, horizontal, parallel to endplate, few mm from margin (Sharpey fibres)
Claw osteophyte Larger, curved, may bridge — progression of traction osteophyte in degenerated disc (TO image answer)
Syndesmophyte (AS) Ossified annulus, vertical, at the endplate margin (marginal)
DISH Ossified ALL, undulating, away from endplate margin (non-marginal)

Osteophytes80% men / 60% women by 50, 95% by 70; most at L3-4 + T9-10

5Management

Non-op (most)activity modification, PT, brace, NSAIDs, bisphosphonates

Cervical traction (fracture)caution: over-distraction (compromised ligaments)

Surgeryfor sequelae: lumbar stenosis, cervical myelopathy, adult spinal deformity

Fractures – CT & long posterior fusion

DISH + trauma (even standing-height fall)
  • Neck pain→CT C-SPINE (not soft/hard collar + discharge or flex-ext)
  • Fracture into posterior elements→LONG POSTERIOR FUSION
    (≥ 3 levels above + 3 below, e.g. C6 → C3-T2)
    (not C5-7 short, anterior alone — failure, or halo / collar — ↑ mortality)

6Complications

Cervical trauma mortality15% operative vs 67% non-operative · ↑ morbidity + mortality vs non-ankylosed spines · vs AS: "higher" / "similar" (Orthobullets) · equivalent (OB) — read the options

Neurologic injury with traumaAS > DISH (67% vs 40%); AS also more surgery + epidural haematoma

HO after THA30–50%

X vs Y

DISH Ankylosing spondylitis
Syndesmophytes Non-marginal, flowing candle wax Marginal, bamboo spine, Romanus shiny corners
Disc space Preserved Ossified (cervical)
Bone density Normal / ↑ Osteopenia
HLA B8 B27
Age > 50 Young (15–40)
SI joints Spared (abnormality excludes DISH) Bilateral sacroiliitis
Facets Spared Ankylosed
Diabetes Yes No
ESR Normal ↑
Thoracic Right-sided Whole spine
Trauma neuro injury Lower Higher

"50-yr, neck stiffness, vertigo — lateral C-spine flowing ossification?"DISH (TO)

"Marked structures on X-ray/CT (bridging curved spurs)?"claw osteophytes (TO)

"67-yr neck + back pain, anterior flowing ossification?"DISH
✗ Wrong options: OPLL · RA · AS · OA

Halo Orthosis Immobilization

Spine · 27 / 69

1Basics

Biomechanics

Principlefixes skull to torso → most rigid external cervical immobilisation (TO)

Best controlsupper cervical motion: rotation at C1-2 / occipitocervical junction
✗ Wrong options: subaxial flex-ext, rotation, lateral bend, CT junction

Weaknessintercalated paradoxical motion in subaxial spine; lateral bending least controlled → "snaking": recumbent lateral shows mid-cervical kyphosis, upright shows lordosis → not ideal for lower cervical injuries (incl. facet fracture-dislocation — fails)

5Management

Indications

Adult Paediatric
Occipital condyle fracture Atlanto-occipital dissociation
Occipitocervical dislocation Jefferson / atlas fractures
Stable Jefferson (type II atlas) Unstable odontoid fractures
Type II odontoid in young Persistent AARS
Type II / IIA hangman C1-2 dissociation; subaxial trauma
Post-op adjunct Pre-op traction for deformity (halo-gravity)

Contraindications

Absolute Relative
Cranial fracture Polytrauma, severe chest trauma, barrel chest, obesity
Infection Advanced age — mortality 21% if > 79 yrs (elderly → avoid halo)
Severe soft-tissue injury at pin sites —

Pre-application CTsuspected cranial fracture · children < 10 yrs (bone thickness, avoid sutures)
✗ Wrong options: skull X-ray

Pin technique

Adult Child
Pins 4 (2 anterior, 2 posterior diagonally opposite) 6–8 (Orthobullets) · 8–12 (OB) — more pins
Torque 8 in-lb (Orthobullets, OB); 6–8 in-lb range 2–4 in-lb ("finger-tight") · 1–5 in-lb (OB) — lower torque (✗ Wrong options: ft-lb · maximal hand tightening)
Vest Standard Custom vest > 2 yrs · Minerva cast < 2 yrs
Retighten Day 2 —

Anterior safe zone1 cm above the orbital rim, over the lateral third of the orbit (Orthobullets) · "lateral two-thirds", ≥ 4.5 cm from midline (OB) — at/below skull equator · lateral to supraorbital nerve, anterior + medial to temporalis

Ringbelow widest part of skull, ~1 cm above ear helix · pins perpendicular to cortex

Anterior pin position
  • Too MEDIAL→SUPRAORBITAL + SUPRATROCHLEAR NERVE injury, frontal sinus
    (forehead / upper eyelid numbness)
  • Correct (1 cm above lateral third, below equator)→NO NEURO COMPLICATION
  • Too LATERAL (temporal fossa) → THIN BONE + TEMPORALIS→jaw pain, chewing pain, infection

6Complications

Overallchildren ~70% (68%) vs adults ~35% (36%)

Complication Rate / management
Pin loosening 36% — commonest in adults; retighten; persistent → exchange
Pin-site infection 12–20%; esp. posterior pin in temporalis fossa; not loose → keep pin + oral antibiotics + local care (✗ Wrong options: remove · tighten) · loose / deep → remove + new site, IV abx, drain abscess
Discomfort 18%; release skin around pin
Dural puncture 1%; meningitis if inner table breached
Abducens (CN VI) palsy Commonest cranial nerve (✗ Wrong options: oculomotor · trochlear) — traction injury, not pin; diplopia + loss of lateral gaze; ↓ traction + observe (resolves)
Supraorbital / supratrochlear palsy Medial anterior pins
Medical Pneumonia, ARDS, arrhythmia; ↑ mortality elderly

X vs Y

Nerve Cause Deficit
Supraorbital Medial anterior pin Upper eyelid / forehead / scalp numbness
Supratrochlear More medial pin Medial forehead + medial eyelid numbness
Abducens (VI) Traction Diplopia, ↓ lateral gaze

How it's asked

"Anterior pins too medial — nerve at risk?"supraorbital nerve

"Region for anterior pin?"1 cm above lateral orbit, below equator

"Red (medial) zone — structures at risk?"supraorbital + supratrochlear nerves

"Anterior pin position?"1 cm above, within lateral third of superior orbital rim
✗ Wrong options: temporalis · medial third

"7-yr, 10 pins at 6 in-lb, pin complication — change?"lower insertional torque

"Preadolescent halo?"8–12 pins at 1–5 in-lb

"2-yr Down syndrome, O-C2 fusion + halo — recommended?"pre-op skull CT

"Halo — all true EXCEPT?"oculomotor is commonest injured CN (TO) — it's abducens

"Commonest neurologic complication of halo traction?"deficit in lateral eye movement (CN VI)

"Lateral gaze loss after 2 wks halo traction?"CN VI injury
✗ Wrong options: surgery needed

"Erythematous, non-loose pin, afebrile?"leave pin + oral antibiotics

"Halo best controls?"atlantoaxial rotation

"Occipital condyle fracture in halo — true?"pins tightened to 8 in-lb
✗ Wrong options: lower C-spine · lateral bend best controlled · middle third · trochlear nerve

Incomplete Spinal Cord Injuries

Spine · 28 / 69

1Basics

Definition

Incomplete SCIsome motor or sensory function preserved below the level: sacral sparing (voluntary anal contraction, perianal sensation) or palpable/visible muscle contraction below level · sacral sparing separates complete from incomplete

Epidemiology

US~11,000 new SCI/yr · 34% incomplete tetraplegia (central cord commonest) · 17% incomplete paraplegia · 47% complete

Anatomy – tracts

Tract Function Crosses
Lateral corticospinal (LCT) Motor (voluntary) — upper limb fibres central/medial, lower limb lateral Brainstem (medulla) → ipsilateral in cord
Ventral corticospinal Motor (smaller) —
Dorsal columns (gracilis = legs/lower trunk, cuneatus = arms) Fine touch, vibration, proprioception Brainstem
Lateral spinothalamic (LST) Pain, temperature, crude sensation In cord, 1–2 levels above entry
Ventral spinothalamic Light (crude) touch In cord

Blood supplyanterior spinal artery → anterior 2/3 of cord (fed by segmental arteries incl. artery of Adamkiewicz: largest, usually left, T8–L1)

4Diagnosis & Classification

ASIA impairment scale

Grade Definition
A Complete — no motor or sensory in S4-S5
B Sensory incomplete — sensation preserved below level incl. S4-S5, no motor
C Motor incomplete — > half key muscles below level grade < 3
D Motor incomplete — ≥ half key muscles grade ≥ 3
E Normal

Incomplete cord syndromes

Syndrome Mechanism Lost Preserved Prognosis
Central cord Elderly, hyperextension, spondylotic stenosis (anterior osteophytes + infolded LF) Motor UE > LE, hands > arms; burning dysaesthesia (hyperpathia) in arms Sacral sparing; LE motor Good (ambulation > 80%); clumsy hands
Anterior cord Flexion-compression (bone) or anterior spinal artery injury (aortic surgery, T12 corpectomy) Motor (LCT) + pain/temperature (LST) ± light touch; LE > UE Dorsal columns (proprioception, vibration) Worst; 10–20% motor recovery; mimics complete
Brown-Séquard Hemitransection — penetrating (stab) Ipsilateral motor + proprioception/vibration; contralateral pain + temperature — Best — 99% (98%) ambulatory
Posterior cord Very rare; posterior spinal artery / tumour / trauma Proprioception Motor, pain, temperature, light touch —

5Management

Central cord syndrome

Pathophysiologytransient compression → central cord oedema → selective lateral corticospinal white matter injury
✗ Wrong options: central grey haemorrhage · anterior/posterior spinal artery · chronic compression = myelopathy · disc herniation · OPLL

Late pictureUE LMN signs (clumsy) + LE UMN signs (spastic)

Treatmentcontroversial · no active compression → non-op · active compression / instability → decompression · ASIA C may benefit from early (< 24 h) decompression (OB)
✗ Wrong options: high-dose steroids · routine non-op for all

Recovery orderLE first → bowel/bladder → proximal UE → hands last · age < 50 → best recovery · usually walk + regain bladder; hands rarely normal
✗ Wrong options: stepwise decline = myelopathy · rapid decline = abscess/tumour

Better neurologic outcomeASIA C/D (mild) · T2 cord signal → less early decline (OB)

Prognosis & surgical indications

Most important prognostic variablecompleteness / severity of neurologic deficit
✗ Wrong options: mechanism · steroids < 8 h · gender · early surgery

Gunshot woundssurgery only for retained canal fragment + incomplete SCI with progressive deficit (or persistent CSF leak) · trans-colonic GSW → broad antibiotics, no surgery · stable burst without deficit → non-op (Wood RCT) · central cord without compression → non-op · type III odontoid neuro intact → non-op

X vs Y

Finding Syndrome
Arms + hands weak > legs, elderly fall on forehead Central cord
Legs paralysed, pain/temp lost, proprioception kept Anterior cord
Ipsilateral motor + proprioception, contralateral pain/temp Brown-Séquard
Proprioception lost only Posterior cord
After thoracoabdominal aneurysm repair / left-sided T12 corpectomy Anterior cord (Adamkiewicz)
ASIA C ASIA D
Key muscles below level > half grade < 3 ≥ half grade ≥ 3

How it's asked

"78-yr fall, hands weakest, urinary dysfunction — prognosis non-op?"likely to walk independently
✗ Wrong options: full hand recovery · stepwise · < 10% bladder recovery

"79-yr forehead strike, UE weak, legs normal — pathophysiology?"selective injury to central lateral corticospinal tract

"Tract causing motor deficit (UE 3/5, LE 4/5)?"lateral corticospinal

"Wrist/finger flexion 3/5, legs 4+/5?"lateral corticospinal tract

"NOT associated with central cord?"LE dysaesthesias

"Who gets central cord syndrome?"67-yr after hyperextension
✗ Wrong options: penetrating → BSS · aortic repair → anterior cord

"73-yr, arms 3/5, legs 4+/5 after forehead strike — pathogenesis?"transient compression + central cord oedema

"75-yr falls on head, stenotic MRI — symptoms?"UE weakness primarily

"Central cord — true?"age < 50 → better ambulation + motor recovery
✗ Wrong options: steroids · routine non-op

"88-yr, arms > legs weakness — pattern?"central cord diagram

"C5-6 dislocation with Brown-Séquard — findings?"ipsilateral motor loss, contralateral pain/temp loss

"Stab with hemitransection?"ipsilateral motor + proprioception, contralateral pain/temp

"Right neck stab, left pain loss, right proprioception loss — motor + temp?"right motor loss, left temperature loss

"Most consistent with anterior cord?"paraplegia after thoracoabdominal aneurysm repair

"T12 corpectomy (left approach), new leg weakness, sensation intact, canal patent?"occlusion of arterial supply (Adamkiewicz)

"MVC, legs weak, ↓ sensation below T9, position sense kept — spared region?"dorsal columns

"Posterior cord syndrome loses?"proprioception

"Most important prognostic variable?"severity of neurologic deficit

Infantile Idiopathic Scoliosis

Spine · 29 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitionidiopathic scoliosis presenting at ≤ 3 years

Incidence4% of idiopathic scoliosis

Sexmales > females (3:1, OB)

Curveusually left thoracic

Risk factorfamily history
✗ Wrong options: predicts progression

Genes & associations

Inheritanceautosomal dominant, variable penetrance

Associationsplagiocephaly, congenital defects (DDH, congenital heart disease), neural axis anomalies (22% of curves > 20°; 80% of these need neurosurgery; syrinx ~20%), thoracic insufficiency syndrome

Early-onset scoliosis (EOS)

Definition (TO)curve > 10° with onset before age 10, any aetiology (GSSG / CSSG) · categories (Vitale): idiopathic, neuromuscular, congenital, syndromic (Marfan, Down)
✗ Wrong options: "< 5 yrs only" · better prognosis than older children

GrowthT1-L5 grows fastest in first 5 yrs; thoracic height doubles birth → maturity · thoracic spine height: 12 cm at birth, 18 cm at 5 yrs, 27 cm at maturity · thoracic volume 6% at birth, 30% at 5, 50% at 10 · target ≥ 22 cm T1–T12 before fusion

Lungs (TO)alveolar number ↑ until ~age 8, THEN volumetric growth · earlier onset + larger curve → pulmonary failure · infantile/juvenile or severely lordotic curves cause pulmonary problems; typical AIS does not
✗ Wrong options: reverse (EXCEPT answer)

Thoracic insufficiency syndrome↓ thoracic growth + lung volume → pulmonary hypertension, cor pulmonale · PFT impairment > 60°, cardiopulmonary issues > 90°

3Presentation

Historyage noticed, progression, perinatal, milestones; drooling → neuro cause

Inspectioncafé-au-lait (NF), hair patch, dimple above gluteal fold (below = benign), nevi, plagiocephaly

Neuromilestones, cavovarus, asymmetric abdominal reflexes (syrinx), clonus, Hoffmann, Babinski, gait

4Diagnosis & Classification

Typesresolving (most) vs progressive

Key radiology findings

X-raystanding PA + lateral; supine if can't stand (underestimates curve); exclude congenital anomalies

Measure Technique Progression risk
Cobb Standard > 20° → progression
Rib phase Convex apical rib head vs apical body on AP Phase 1 = no overlap · Phase 2 = overlap → high risk (✗ Wrong options: "phase 1 worse" (TO))
RVAD (Mehta) Perpendicular to apical endplate; rib-vertebral angles via rib head–neck line; concave − convex > 20° → progression (83%); < 20° → resolves (83%) (✗ Wrong options: > 10°)

Mehta predictorsCobb > 20° · RVAD > 20° · phase 2 rib
✗ Wrong options: age · sex · family history

MRI (whole spine)first, before treatment: tether, cyst, tumour, syrinx · abnormal abdominal reflexes or < 10 yrs with significant curve → MRI spinal axis
✗ Wrong options: PT · brace · observe · fusion

5Management

Infantile idiopathic scoliosis (MRI normal)
  • Cobb < 30° (< 20–25°) + RVAD < 20° + phase 1→OBSERVE (~90% resolve)
  • Flexible, Cobb > 30° (> 20–25°), RVAD > 20°, phase 2→SERIAL MEHTA (EDF) DEROTATIONAL CASTING
    (or TLSO); brace after casting / late flexible
  • Cobb > 50–60° or failed casting→GROWING RODS (dual / magnetic) or VEPTR
    (fusion delayed to ~10 yrs)

Mehta casting

Elongation-derotation-flexion (EDF) castingcorrects in 3D while allowing growth · start early: before 18 months–4 yrs → better chance of curve < 15° (worse if started > 4 yrs) · smaller initial Cobb + good first in-cast correction predict success · may cure idiopathic infantile curves; in syndromic EOS → delays surgery, curve may still progress but spine keeps growing · complications: skin necrosis, infection

1-yr-old, 35°, RVAD > 20°, phase 2serial derotational casting
✗ Wrong options: TLSO · observe · fusion · VBT

22-month, RVAD 26° → casting indication
✗ Wrong options: Cobb 18°/28° · RVAD 16°/5°

Growing constructs

Growing rodsanchors proximal + distal; lengthen every 6–8 months; permit up to 5 cm growth; ↑ FVC · traditional or magnetic · 5-yr failing casting with ↓ FEV1/FVC → growing rods
✗ Wrong options: more casts · TLSO · fusion

Fusion before age 10pulmonary compromise → delay

VEPTR (TO)rib-based distraction (rib hooks proximally; spine/pelvic anchors distally) → EOS with rib anomalies / TIS, also neuromuscular (SMA) · facilitates infant use · pelvic anchors in ambulators → crouched gait (limit to non-ambulators)
✗ Wrong options: "pelvic anchors make infant use difficult" (EXCEPT answer)

Growth-friendly classes (Skaggs)distraction: TGR, VEPTR, MCGR · compression: vertebral body stapling, VBT · guided growth: Luque trolley, Shilla

6Complications

Surgeryhigh complication rate · progressive untreated curves can be fatal · progressive by age 5 → > 50% exceed 70°

X vs Y

Infantile Juvenile Adolescent
Age 0–3 4–10 10–18
Sex Male F (older) Female
Curve Left thoracic Right thoracic Right thoracic
Natural history Most resolve Most progress Variable
Key Rx Mehta cast Brace / growing rods Brace / PSF

How it's asked

"Suggests progression in infantile scoliosis?"rib overlap of apical vertebra (phase 2)
✗ Wrong options: RVAD > 10° · male · family history

"Indication for Mehta casting / TLSO?"RVAD 26°

"RVAD calculation?"concave angle − convex angle (X − Y)

"VEPTR — incorrect statement?""pelvic anchors make use in infants with TIS difficult" (TO)

"5-yr failing Mehta casts, ↓ FEV1/FVC?"traditional or magnetic growing rods

"Mehta casting for syndromic EOS — expected?"continued progression but further spine growth

"1-yr-old, 35°, RVAD > 20°, phase 2 rib?"serial derotational casting

"22-month, 48°, RVAD 24°, MRI normal — true?"earlier EDF casting → better chance of curve < 15°

"6-yr painless curve, asymmetric abdominal reflexes?"MRI spinal axis

"Lungs and spinal deformity — EXCEPT?""volumetric growth until 8 then alveolar increase" (TO — reverse)

"EOS definition?"scoliosis developing before age 10 (TO)

Intervertebral Disc

Spine · 30 / 69

1Basics

Composition & structure

Functionmotion + stability; links bodies; 25% of spinal column height

Endplateshyaline cartilage attaches disc to bodies

Annulus fibrosus Nucleus pulposus
Collagen Type I (obliquely oriented, 30° alternating "criss-cross" lamellae); inner annulus also type II; minor type V Type II (minor type XI)
Collagen : proteoglycan High collagen / low PG Low collagen / high PG
Water Lower (~65% outer annulus) ~88% (80–90%) (✗ Wrong options: 60–70%)
Cells Fibroblast-like (annulus only) Chondrocyte-like (survive hypoxia); notochordal cells gone by ~10 yrs
Origin Sclerotome Notochord
Property Highest tensile stress / tensile modulus — resists torsion, axial + tensile loads; prevents distraction Highest compressive stress; compressibility; distributes load

Aggrecanmost abundant proteoglycan; hydrophilic → maintains water content (compressive strength) · GAGs: chondroitin + keratan sulphate · other PGs: decorin (fibrillogenesis), versican (adhesion), biglycan, lumican, fibromodulin (more in annulus)
✗ Wrong options: tensile strength (collagen) · sensory transmission · cross-linking

Aggrecan : collagennucleus > 1 · annulus < 1 · endplate < 1

Nutrition & metabolism

Largest avascular structurecapillaries end at endplates; nutrition by diffusion through pores in the (central) endplates · outer third of annulus from epidural vessels (TO) · loading "pumps" diffusion
✗ Wrong options: CSF · annular vessels · diffusion across annulus (not porous enough)

Nucleus cellsenergy by anaerobic glycolysis → need glucose; die with ↓ glucose or acidic pH, not hypoxia
✗ Wrong options: Krebs

Endplate damage (smoking, calcification, sclerosis) → degeneration

Innervation

Sinuvertebral nervefrom dorsal root ganglion (Orthobullets, OB) · "branch of ventral ramus" (TO) → superficial annulus only (nucleus + inner annulus aneural) · painful degenerate discs: nerves grow to inner third (~50%) with granulation tissue (neo-innervation) · central endplate also innervated

Pain neuropeptidessubstance P, calcitonin (CGRP), VIP, CPON (neuropeptide Y flank)

Biomechanics

Viscoelasticcreep (deformation over time) + hysteresis (energy absorption with repetitive loading; ↓ with age)

Intradiscal pressurelowest supine < standing < highest sitting flexed forward holding weights; load held closer to body → lower pressure
✗ Wrong options: highest standing (EXCEPT answer)

Diurnal~25% of water lost by day, regained at night
✗ Wrong options: "water ↓ at night"

After subtotal discectomyextension = most stable loading mode

2Mechanism

Degeneration biology

↓ Decreases with age / degeneration ↑ Increases
Water Keratan : chondroitin sulphate ratio
Proteoglycans / aggrecan (large aggregating PG degradation = pathognomonic) Lactate
pH Degradative enzymes — MMPs
Viable cell number, matrix synthesis, disc height Fibroblast-like cell density (annulus only — not in nucleus)
Nutritional transport Type X collagen
Elasticity, GAG : collagen ratio, sulphation Inner annulus thickness
No change: absolute collagen quantity Nucleus stays type II

Conversion to fibrocartilageannulus–nucleus distinction lost, endplate hyalinisation, fissures, Schmorl nodes, vascularisation of posterior annulus

Annular tearscommonest posterolateral
✗ Wrong options: central

Herniated discspontaneous ↑ IL-1β, OPG, RANKL, PTH; IL-1β stimulates disc cells to ↑ MMPs, nitric oxide, IL-6, PGE2 · MMPs = most abundant product of herniated disc cells → ECM proteolysis
✗ Wrong options: caspases · decorin

Kirkaldy-Willis stages (TO)

Stage Age Features
Dysfunction 15–45 Circumferential + radial annular tears, facet synovitis
Instability 35–70 Internal disruption, disc resorption, facet laxity/subluxation → HNP
Stabilisation > 60 Hypertrophic bone, segmental stiffening → stenosis

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Vacuum phenomenon (TO)nitrogen gas in disc/vertebra: DDD (commonest), osteoporotic collapse, myeloma, Schmorl node, osteonecrosis (Kümmell), rarely infection
✗ Wrong options: endplate avulsion (EXCEPT)

Pfirrmann classification (TO – T2 MRI)

Grade Signal Nucleus vs annulus Height
I Homogeneous, bright white Clear Normal
II Inhomogeneous, white ± grey band Clear Normal
III Intermittent grey Unclear Normal / slightly ↓
IV Dark grey Lost Slightly–moderately ↓
V Black Lost Collapsed

5Management

Biological therapyBMP-2, BMP-7, TGF-β = morphogens: ↑ chondrogenic phenotype + matrix synthesis without proliferation · IGF-1, FGF = mitogens (↑ proliferation)

HNP natural history (TO)mostly favourable; surgical benefit mainly first year; non-progressive deficits (except CES) treated non-op; surgery can wait 6–12 wks

X vs Y

Morphogen Mitogen
Examples BMP-2, BMP-7, TGF-β IGF-1, FGF
Action ↑ Chondrogenic phenotype, matrix ↑ Cell number

Definitionidiopathic scoliosis presenting at 4–10 yrs (3–10 in OB)

Anomaly Clue

3Presentation

X-rayupright PA + lateral · Cobb > 10° = scoliosis (error 3–5°) · bending films → which curves to fuse

Diagnostic criteria – MRI (whole spine)

MRI indicated Not an indication
Age < 10–11 with curve > 20° (even neuro-normal; e.g. 25° right thoracic in 7-yr) Right thoracic curve per se (in older child)
Left thoracic curve Thoracic hypokyphosis
Asymmetric / absent abdominal reflexes Lenke C lumbar modifier
Male No family history

Absent pedicle, congenital bar —

5Management

Juvenile idiopathic scoliosis (MRI normal)
  • < 20°→OBSERVE (frequent X-rays)
  • 20–50°→BRACE 16–23 h/day (prevent progression)
    (TO: 25–40° → casting)
  • > 50°
    • Young, much growth left→GROWING RODS / VEPTR (or A + P fusion)
    • Near maturity→POSTERIOR SPINAL FUSION

Bracingrelative CI = thoracic hypokyphosis · goal is prevention, not correction

45° curve in 7-yr (TO)options: growing rods, stapling, casting · (✗ Wrong options: posterior instrumented fusion (crankshaft + ↓ thoracic growth)) · TO thresholds: 25–40° cast, > 40° growth-friendly surgery

FusionA + P in young (prevent crankshaft) · PSF near maturity (gold standard thoracic + double major) · ASF for TL / lumbar with normal sagittal profile

Growing rods

Lengtheningevery ~6 months → > 2 procedures
✗ Wrong options: VBT + PSF = 2 stages · single-stage PSF · A/P 1–2 stages · halo + PSF

Law of diminishing returnseach lengthening needs ↑ force and gains ↓ length (force doubles by 5th; < 8 mm per lengthening)

Comparison Finding
Traditional rods vs VEPTR TGR: ↑ curve correction + truncal height, fewer complications (VEPTR subcutaneous → ↑ wound problems)
Magnetic (MCGR) vs traditional MCGR → fewer surgeries
Final fusion after growth-friendly vs primary fusion ↑ reoperation + complications
Younger age / longer duration ↑ infection + wound complications
Pulmonary Growing rods ↑ PFTs + diaphragm motion

6Complications

Crankshaftposterior fusion alone in young child

X vs Y

Juvenile Infantile Adolescent
Age 4–10 0–3 10–18
Sex F > M M > F F
Curve Right thoracic Left thoracic Right thoracic
Neural axis anomaly 18–25% ~22% (> 20°) Low
Natural history Progresses (70% treated) Mostly resolves Variable

How it's asked

"MRI rules out all EXCEPT?"atlantoaxial rotatory instability

"Screening MRI indications EXCEPT?"right thoracic curve
✗ Wrong options: male, age < 11, absent apical lordosis, neuro signs

"8-yr, left curve, normal neuro — next?"MRI cervical-thoracic-lumbar

"Progressive curve + absent abdominal reflex?"spinal cord abnormality → whole-spine MRI

"7-yr, MRI recommended because?"25° right thoracic curve

"7-yr, 45° JIS — EXCEPT?"posterior instrumented fusion (TO)

"Growing rods with repeated lengthenings?"force ↑, length gained ↓

"TGR vs VEPTR?"TGR more correction + truncal height

"7-yr, 75° + 45° curves — option needing > 2 surgeries?"traditional growing rods

Klippel-Feil Syndrome

Spine · 32 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitioncongenital fusion of ≥ 2 cervical vertebrae from failure of segmentation (or formation) of cervical somites at 3–8 weeks

Incidenceclassically 1 in 40,000 · recent: 1 in 172 to 1 in 83 (asymptomatic) · 60–70% female

Commonest fusion levelsC2-C3 (71%) > C5-C6 (68%) > C6-C7 (68%) > C3-C4 (29%) (TO)
✗ Wrong options: C3-4

Genes & inheritance

Pattern Gene
Sporadic Commonest; isolated fusion
Autosomal dominant GDF6 (chr 8), GDF3 (chr 12); SGM1 (chr 8)
Autosomal recessive MEOX1 (chr 17) (encodes MOX1, regulates vertebral separation)
Other Notch, Pax genes; fetal alcohol (50% have cervical fusions)

Associated conditions

Association Rate
Congenital scoliosis 53.5–60% (commonest orthopaedic association)
Sprengel deformity 30% (Orthobullets) · 33% / 50% / 20% (TO) · 6.4–16.7% (Orthobullets exam) — read the options
Renal (aplasia) 33% → renal US
Deafness 30%
Congenital heart (VSD) 15–30% (5–30%) → echo
Atlantoaxial instability ~50%
Adjacent-level degeneration 100%
CNS anomalies 19% (brainstem, Chiari, tethered cord, diastematomyelia)
Others Basilar invagination, cervical stenosis, synkinesis (mirror movements), cleft palate, absent ribs

Wildervanck (cervico-oculo-acoustic)KFS + ossicular deafness + Duane syndrome (abducens palsy with retraction bulbi); X-linked, female (Clarke KF4)

3Presentation

Classic triad (< 50%)low posterior hairline, short (webbed) neck, limited cervical ROM

Most consistent finding (TO)limitation of neck motion
✗ Wrong options: short neck · low hairline · high scapula

Not a feature / not in triad (TO)bilateral SCM contractures · head tilt (torticollis) may coexist but not part of triad

Symptomsneck stiffness, neck/back pain (onset ~16 yrs, worse ~28 yrs), radiculopathy/myelopathy from hypermobile adjacent segments · ↑ cervical myelopathy rate

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP, lateral, open-mouth; whole spine Fusions (craniocervical / subaxial); basilar invagination (dens above McRae's line); AAI = ADI > 5 mm; stenosis < 13 mm; disc calcifications (resolve in 6 months)
CT Bony anatomy pre-op
MRI Cord anomalies; smaller cords in larger canals
Renal US + echo Renal aplasia, VSD

Classifications

KFS (original) Fusion Inheritance
I Most / all cervical Sporadic
II 1–2 vertebrae AD
III + thoracic / lumbar AR
Samartzis (prognostic) Pattern Symptoms
I (25%) Single-level fusion Axial
II (50%) Multiple non-contiguous Myelopathy / radiculopathy
III (25%) Multiple contiguous Myelopathy / radiculopathy

ClarkeKF1 (AR, only class with C1 fusion) · KF2 (AD, C2-3 dominant, SGM1) · KF3 (isolated, e.g. C5-6) · KF4 (X-linked, Wildervanck)

High-risk fusion patterns (TO)

Pattern Problem
1 — C1-2 fusion + occipitalised atlas Motion concentrated at atlantoaxial → odontoid hypermobile, posterior dislocation
2 — Long fusion + abnormal occipitocervical junction Forces through abnormal dens / C1 ring → instability
3 — Single open interspace between two fused blocks Hypermobile segment → instability + DJD
Least symptomatic Multiple short fusions separated by > 1 mobile interspace (stress shared)

Above vs below C3fusions above C3 → more symptoms, avoid contact sport · below C3 → usually asymptomatic, normal life span

5Management

Contact sports participation

Clearance Indication
Can play contact sport Asymptomatic, 1–2 level fusion below C3 (e.g. C4-5), Samartzis II at/below C3 with full ROM, no instability/spondylosis
No contact sport (absolute) Fusion involving C2 (commonest presentation), long / multilevel fusions, any ↓ cervical motion, instability, spondylosis, occipitocervical anomaly, after KFS surgery
Relative Samartzis II with prior transient quadriplegia

Surgery (decompression + fusion)basilar invagination, chronic pain, myelopathy, atlantoaxial instability, symptomatic adjacent disease, progressive scoliosis (82% of KFS with congenital scoliosis need surgery)

6Complications

Cervical SCIwith contact sports (small cords); central cord after minor trauma

X vs Y

Klippel-Feil Congenital muscular torticollis
Lesion Bony fusion SCM fibrosis
Neck Short, low hairline, ↓ ROM Tilt + rotation, SCM mass
Associations Renal, deafness, VSD, Sprengel, scoliosis DDH, metatarsus adductus

How it's asked

"Adolescent, short neck, renal aplasia, C4-5 fusion — football?"can play (TO)

"10-yr, cervical fusion above C3, renal aplasia?"Klippel-Feil, no contact sports

"14-yr, C2-4 fusion, lacrosse?"refrain from contact sports
✗ Wrong options: after MRI · after PT · after fusion

"NOT a feature of KFS?"bilateral SCM contractures (TO)

"Fusion pattern least likely symptomatic?"multiple short fusions separated by mobile joints (TO)

"Most consistent finding?"limitation of neck motion (TO)

"Most commonly fused level?"C2-C3 (TO)

"Not part of triad?"head tilt (TO)

Low Back Pain

Spine · 33 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology & natural history

Lifetime50–80% (70–85%) · ~$100 billion/yr · 2nd only to respiratory infection for office visits · leading cause of disability worldwide

Workback injury ~2% of workforce/yr · of those off work: 60–70% back by 6 wks, 80–90% by 12 wks; slow after 12 wks

Course90% resolve within 1 yr · chronic in 5–10% · recurrence 20–72%

Commonest causemuscle strain (musculogenic); commonest degenerative: stenosis, disc herniation, discogenic pain

Risk factorsobesity, smoking, sex, lifting, vibration, prolonged sitting, job dissatisfaction

Red vs yellow flags (TO)

Red flags (serious pathology) Yellow flags (chronic disability risk)
Age < 20 or > 50 Belief pain is severely disabling
Significant trauma Fear-avoidance behaviour
Constant, night pain, worsening Low mood
Cancer, steroids, IVDU Expects passive treatment
Unexplained fever / weight loss Poor job satisfaction
Neurologic deficit · bowel/bladder change (CES) Lack of family support (✗ Wrong options: red flag)

3Presentation

Pain type Features
Musculogenic Commonest; activity-related stiffness, difficulty bending
Discogenic Controversial; discogram
Mechanical Facet degeneration / instability; worse lifting, standing
Sacroiliac SI joint
Radicular Unilateral dermatomal leg pain
Referred Buttocks, posterior thighs, groin (L5-S1)
Neurogenic claudication Buttock/leg pain with standing — fairly specific for stenosis
Myelopathy Cord lesion (~L1 or above)
CES / conus Bilateral leg pain, weakness, saddle anaesthesia, bowel/bladder

Non-organic (Waddell) signs

Significant if ≥ 3 of 5

Waddell sign
Superficial / non-anatomic tenderness
Simulation — pain with axial compression or simulated rotation
Distraction — negative SLR when distracted (seated)
Regional non-dermatomal sensory / motor disturbance
Overreaction

Organic signpositive SLR with distraction · neuroanatomic deficit is NOT Waddell (TO)
✗ Wrong options: axial-load pain · diffuse tenderness · non-dermatomal numbness · simulated rotation

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

X-rayonly if > 1 month failed treatment or red flags
✗ Wrong options: at first visit for 2-day / 2-week atraumatic pain

MRIsensitive + specific but many asymptomatic abnormalities

Asymptomatic age HNP Bulge Degeneration
20–39 21% 56% 34%
40–59 22% 50% 59%
60–79 36% 79% 93%

High-intensity zone (annular tear)59% symptomatic vs 25% asymptomatic (Carragee); discography provokes pain ~70% regardless

Compression fracture acuityMRI (oedema ∝ recency)
✗ Wrong options: CT · flex-ext · indium scan

Differentialstrain, HNP, DS, stenosis, radiculopathy, AAA, SI infection, AS, coccydynia, sacral insufficiency fracture

5Management

Acute atraumatic LBP, no red flags
  • REASSURANCE + LIMITED ANALGESIA + EARLY ROM / activity, follow-up ~4 wks
    (no X-ray, MRI or ESI at first visit)
  • > 1 month failed / red flags→IMAGING

Chronic work-related pain — strongest negative predictorhigh pre-treatment VAS
✗ Wrong options: race · smoking · occupation · gender

Chronic non-specific pain, non-surgical (smoker, depression, narcotics)cognitive intervention + exercise + smoking cessation
✗ Wrong options: discography · EMG · myelogram

Coccydynia (TO)

Causecommonest direct trauma; childbirth, surgery, epidural

Acute (< 2 months)rest + NSAIDs 8 wks, stool softener, U-shaped cushion

Chronic (> 2 months)steroid + LA injection ± manipulation under GA → after 2 failed monthly injections → coccygectomy (60–80% success) (TO answer 80%)

Baastrup disease ("kissing spines") (TO)

Pathologycrowding / contact of adjacent spinous processes with excessive lordosis → interspinous ligament degeneration, adventitious bursa, sclerosis, flattening · commonest L4-L5
✗ Wrong options: spina bifida · cervical instability/myelopathy

Clinicalmidline pain radiating up/down, worse in extension, better in flexion, point tenderness · rarely claudication (epidural cyst)

Imaginglateral X-ray contact + sclerosis; MRI interspinous bursitis (< 10%)

TreatmentNSAIDs, steroid + LA injection, bursa excision / osteotomy

X vs Y

Organic Non-organic (Waddell)
SLR distracted Positive Negative
Deficit Dermatomal Regional
Tenderness Localised Superficial / diffuse

How it's asked

"2-day atraumatic LBP — all appropriate EXCEPT?"lumbar radiographs

"Missed work for acute LBP — return within 6 wks?"60–70%

"Severe atraumatic LBP, no red flags — management?"reassurance, limited analgesics, early ROM

"Return to work within 12 wks?"80–90%

"Radiographs NOT indicated initially?"28-yr with 2 wks isolated LBP

"Waddell signs EXCEPT?"neuroanatomic motor/sensory compromise (TO)

"Finding indicating organic cause?"positive SLR with distraction

"Most likely non-organic LBP?"superficial tenderness, non-dermatomal numbness/weakness, negative SLR, unrelated disc degeneration

"Strongest negative predictor of non-op success?"high pre-treatment VAS

"4-yr LBP, smoker, depression, narcotics, annular tear on MRI?"cognitive intervention, exercise, smoking cessation

"Acuity of compression fracture?"MRI

"Red flags EXCEPT?"lack of family support (TO — yellow flag)

"Coccygectomy success after failed non-op?"80% (TO)

"Baastrup disease?"crowding of spinous processes (TO)

Lower Extremity Spine Exam

Spine · 34 / 69

1Basics

Root map

Overlapmost muscles have > 1 root (e.g. ankle dorsiflexion = L4 + L5)

Root Motion Muscle (nerve) Sensory Reflex
L1 — — Iliac crest, groin Cremasteric (L1-2)
L2 Hip flexion + adduction Iliopsoas (femoral/plexus), adductors (obturator) Anterior + inner thigh Cremasteric
L3 Knee extension (+ L4) Quadriceps (femoral) Anterior/medial thigh, medial knee —
L4 Ankle dorsiflexion (+ L5) Tibialis anterior (DPN) Lateral thigh, anterior knee, medial leg Patellar
L5 Foot inversion, toe dorsiflexion, hip extension, hip abduction Tib posterior (tibial), EHL/EDL (DPN), hamstrings, glut max (inferior gluteal), glut medius (superior gluteal) Lateral leg + dorsum of foot —
S1 Plantar flexion, eversion Gastrocsoleus (tibial), peroneals (SPN) Posterior leg (lateral foot) Achilles
S2 Toe plantar flexion FHL, FDL (tibial) Plantar foot —
S3–4 Bowel + bladder Bladder Perianal —

3Presentation

Symptom clues

Symptom Think
Weight loss, axial pain, night pain Tumour / infection
Mechanical pain Instability, discogenic
Radicular pain HNP
Referred pain PUD, cholecystitis, renal stone, PID, pancreatitis
Psychogenic Secondary gain

Inspectionscars, café-au-lait, lumbar hairy patch

Grading (ASIA)

Motor
0 Paralysis
1 Palpable / visible contraction
2 Full ROM, gravity eliminated
3 Full ROM against gravity
4 Against some resistance
5 Normal resistance
NT Not testable

Sensory0 absent · 1 impaired · 2 normal · NT

4Diagnosis & Classification

Special tests

Test Fact
Straight leg raise Supine passive elevation 30–70° (positive by ~40°) → pain below the knee = L4–S1 (L5-S1) root tension; separate from hamstring tightness
Crossed SLR Raising the well leg reproduces pain in the affected leg (more specific)
Femoral nerve stretch Prone, knee flexed + hip extended → anterior thigh pain = L2-3, L3-4 protrusion
Babinski, ankle clonus UMN lesion
Bulbocavernosus reflex Squeeze glans/clitoris or tug Foley → anal contraction; tests for spinal shock
Lhermitte / Hoffmann / Spurling Cervical tests (✗ Wrong options: lumbar)

Clinical findings → lesion

Finding Cause
Medial knee paraesthesia Saphenous (femoral) irritation — psoas abscess, surfer's compression
Trendelenburg gait L5 root → gluteus medius (superior gluteal) — L5/S1 far-lateral HNP (OB) / paracentral L4/5 HNP (Orthobullets)
Lateral thigh numbness Meralgia paraesthetica (LFCN) — positioning
Foot drop Common peroneal palsy / sciatic compression (lateral knee pressure, hip dislocation)
Quadriceps weakness Femoral nerve palsy (hyperflexed Pavlik) / L3-L4 roots

HNP → root (Trendelenburg logic)

Disc Root Weak
L3/4 far lateral L3 Hip flexion
L3/4 central / L4/5 far lateral L4 Knee extension, dorsiflexion
L4/5 paracentral or L5/S1 far lateral L5 Hip abduction (Trendelenburg), EHL
L5/S1 paracentral S1 Plantar flexion

UMN + LMN together (TO)

Absent ankle jerks + upgoing plantarsB12 deficiency (subacute combined degeneration), motor neuron disease, Friedreich ataxia (also conus lesions, cervical myelopathy + lumbar stenosis)
✗ Wrong options: multiple sclerosis (UMN only, never LMN)

Waddell signs (≥ 3 = significant)

Sign Description
Tenderness Superficial (light touch) or deep non-anatomic
Simulation Pain with light axial skull compression or pelvic twist
Distraction No pain with distracted SLR
Regional Non-anatomic motor / sensory findings
Overreaction Any time

How it's asked

"Passive leg elevation 30–70° → pain below knee?"straight leg raise test

"Trendelenburg gait from lumbar disc?"L5/S1 far-lateral herniation (L5 root)

"NOT a cause of absent ankle jerks + upgoing plantars?"multiple sclerosis (TO)

"Bulbocavernosus reflex tests for?"spinal shock

"Femoral stretch test positive in?"L2-3 / L3-4 disc

Lumbar Disc Herniation

Spine · 35 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidencepeak 4th–5th decades · lifetime prevalence 10% · only ~5% symptomatic · M:F 3:1

Commonest levelL5-S1 (Orthobullets) · L4-L5 (TO) — read the options · 95% at L4-5 or L5-S1

Foraminal herniationscommonest at L3-L4 (OB)

Pathoanatomy

Mechanismrecurrent torsional strain → outer annulus tears → nucleus herniates · PLL weakest posterolaterally → paracentral site

Pain fibressinuvertebral nerve → posterior annulus (vertebrogenic back pain precedes herniation)

Who herniatesyoung, well-hydrated discs (child: ring apophysis Salter-Harris II) · desiccated old discs less likely

Sciaticacompression + inflammation: TNF-α, MMP, NO, PGE₂, IL-6

Disc part Collagen Property
Annulus fibrosus Type I, high collagen / low proteoglycan Tensile strength, 15–25 lamellae
Nucleus pulposus Type II, low collagen / high proteoglycan, 88% water Compressibility; avascular (endplate diffusion)

Root anatomy

Lumbarvertical root: L5 runs under L5 pedicle (match) → paracentral and foraminal disc at same level hit different roots

Cervicalhorizontal root: C6 under C5 pedicle (mismatch) → central + foraminal hit same root

3Presentation

Historysudden pain after lifting · vibration + sitting with bending/rotation · relief supine with hips + knees flexed

Radicular painworse sitting, cough, Valsalva, sneeze · better standing · walking doesn't worsen

Postureleans away from the radiculopathy side (opens foramen) · sciatic scoliosis (list)

Cauda equina1–10% (central disc): bilateral leg pain, weakness, saddle anaesthesia, bowel/bladder → emergent discectomy within 48 h

Root localisation

Root Motor Sensory Reflex
L3 Hip adduction, knee extension Anteromedial thigh, near medial femoral epicondyle, groin —
L4 Ankle DF (L4 > L5), quadriceps (single-leg sit-to-stand) · can't heel walk (TO) Lateral thigh → across knee → medial leg/malleolus ↓ Patellar
L5 EHL (great toe extension), foot inversion (tib post), hip abduction (glut medius → Trendelenburg) Anterolateral leg, dorsum of foot, first web space —
S1 Plantar flexion (10 single-leg calf raises) Posterior calf, lateral foot ↓ Achilles

Tibialis anteriorL4 or L5 dominant (~50:50); L3-4 paracentral → traversing L4 → weak tib anterior ± quads (OB)

Paracentral vs far lateral

Location Share Root hit Example
Central — Back pain; CES Surgical emergency
Paracentral (posterolateral) 90–95% Traversing (lower) L4-5 → L5 · L5-S1 → S1 · L3-4 → L4
Foraminal / far lateral 5–10% Exiting (upper) + DRG → more severe pain L4-5 → L4 (TO) · L5-S1 → L5 · L3-4 → L3
Axillary — Exiting + traversing —

Trendelenburg gaitL5 → gluteus medius → far lateral L5-S1 (TO) or posterolateral L4-5 (TO answer "L4L5 herniated disc with positive Trendelenburg test")

L4-5 extraforaminal discL4 → heel walk not possible (TO) · pain worse standing + extension, eased by flexion

First dorsal web-space numbnessL5 → L4-5 disc prolapse (TO)

Weak great-toe dorsiflexion — causesL5 root, peroneal palsy, sciatic lesion
✗ Wrong options: Morton's neuroma (TO)

L5 radiculopathy vs peroneal palsy

L5 radiculopathy Common peroneal palsy
Ankle inversion (tib post, L5 + tibial n.) Weak Intact
DF, toe extension, eversion Weak Weak
Hip abduction Weak Intact
Cough / Valsalva / SLR Worse No effect

4Diagnosis & Classification

Provocative tests

Test Fact
Straight leg raise Reproduces leg pain at 30–70° hip flexion · L4, L5, S1 tension · most predictive finding for a good surgical candidate · false positive in hip disease (TO)
Laségue Orthobullets: = SLR · TO: two-step — SLR, then flex the knee → sciatic pain disappears (hip pain doesn't) · "Laségue = SLR" is the incorrect statement (TO) — read the options
Bragard (reinforcement) SLR to pain, lower slightly, dorsiflex ankle → pain returns · makes SLR specific for nerve compression (TO answer over crossed SLR)
Crossed (well-leg) SLR Less sensitive, more specific · large central disc indenting thecal sac (TO)
Bowstring (McNab) SLR to pain, flex knee, press tibial nerve in popliteal fossa → pain
Femoral stretch (Wassermann) Prone, knee flexed, hip extended → anterior thigh pain · L2, L3
Kernig Neck flexion + hip flexion + leg extension
Naffziger Jugular compression → cough → pain
Milgram Supine straight-leg elevation held 30 s
✗ Wrong options: apprehension · Murphy Shoulder/patella instability · gallbladder (TO)

Key radiology findings

Study Use / finding
X-ray AP + lateral Usually normal · loss of lordosis (spasm), disc-height loss, sciatic scoliosis · counts transitional vertebrae (localisation) · poor sensitivity
Flexion-extension Instability → adds fusion
MRI without gadolinium Modality of choice · if pain > 1 month failing non-op or red flags · many asymptomatic findings → correlate · distinguishes synovial facet cyst
MRI with gadolinium Revision: scar enhances, recurrent disc doesn't
CT myelogram Can't have MRI (pacemaker) · 93% accurate · misses foraminal/extraforaminal discs

Red flags → MRI (TO)

Red flagsunexplained fever, IVDU, age < 20, significant trauma, cancer history, CES
✗ Wrong options: lack of family support (yellow flag) (TO)

Classifications

Morphology Definition
Protrusion Eccentric bulge, annulus intact
Extrusion Through annulus, still continuous with disc space
Sequestered (free) Through annulus, no continuity; migrates up/down
Containment Definition
Contained Beneath PLL
Uncontained Dorsal to PLL
Timing Duration
Acute < 3–6 months (surgical outcome depends on chronicity)
Chronic > 6 months

5Management

Lumbar disc herniation
  • CES→EMERGENT DISCECTOMY (within 48 h)
  • No CES, no significant weakness→NSAIDs + ACTIVITY MODIFICATION + PT
    (mild non-progressive weakness still non-op)
  • Failed ~6 wks, severe pain→TRANSFORAMINAL SELECTIVE ROOT INJECTION
  • Persistent disabling pain > 6 wks / progressive weakness→MICRODISCECTOMY
    • Paracentral→MIDLINE LAMINOTOMY + LIMITED DISCECTOMY
    • Far lateral→PARASPINAL (WILTSE) APPROACH

Natural history & non-operative care

Natural history90% improve within 3 months without surgery · less likely if still symptomatic at 6 wks · TO stem: 70% resolve with NSAIDs + PT

Resorptionherniation shrinks over time · sequestered fragments resorb most · by macrophage phagocytosis + enzymatic degradation

Non-op predictorsgood: higher education, no radiculopathy · poor: BMI > 30, symptoms > 6 months before treatment

MedicationsNSAIDs, muscle relaxants · oral steroid → modest function gain, no pain gain · narcotics 2–3 days only (worse surgical outcomes)

PTfrom ~3 wks · extension exercises (✓ acute posterior-thigh radicular pain) · traction, careful manipulation

Early (2-day, 2-week) radicular pain, no deficitanti-inflammatories + activity modification
✗ Wrong options: MRI · surgery · injection

Selective nerve root injection2nd line after ~6 wks · transforaminal best for far lateral · durable relief ~50% (vs ~90% surgery) · best with extruded discs · steroid adds nothing over lidocaine

Picking the injection leveltarget the compressed root's foramen: lateral calf → L5 transforaminal · anterior thigh with L3-4 foraminal disc → L3-L4 transforaminal (L3 root) · L5-S1 foramen block → dorsal foot + great toe
✗ Wrong options: lateral foot = S1 foramen · shin = L4

Facet joint injectionrelief on two occasions → indication for facet ablation; medial branch block predicts better

Operative criteria (SPORT)

Indication Detail
Persistent disabling radicular pain > 6 wks, failed non-op ± injection · refractory radicular pain = clearest indication · better if done within 2 months
Progressive / significant weakness —
Cauda equina Emergent
No Back pain alone · no instability → no fusion (✗ Wrong options: ALIF · PLIF)
Outcome Finding
Surgery vs non-op Better pain + function at 4 yrs · early relief sustained to 2 yrs · equal disability at 5 yrs · long term equivalent
As-treated analysis Significant benefit of surgery (pain, function, satisfaction)
Return to work Same
Workers' compensation Less symptom relief, less QoL gain at 5 yrs
Better surgical result Worse surgical result
Leg pain dominant, positive SLR, weakness matching MRI Workers' comp
Married, age > 41, no joint problems Smoking
Progressively worsening symptoms Chronic headaches, depression
Paracentral / foraminal, caudal levels (L5-S1 > L2-3) Central / extraforaminal
Severe leg pain, short duration, younger, active —

Surgical approach

Pathology Operation
Paracentral Midline approach (between spinous process and multifidus) → laminotomy + limited discectomy (e.g. L4 laminotomy, partial flavum resection, medial facetectomy)
Lateral (not far lateral) protrusion, e.g. L1-2 Midline microdiscectomy (OB)
Far lateral (e.g. L4-5 → L4, medial malleolus) Paraspinal muscle-splitting approach of Wiltse (multifidus–longissimus) · outside-in, preserves facet · tubular/crank retractor
Recurrent paracentral Revision via midline

Fragment excision vs subtotal discectomyfragment excision: less long-term back pain, more reherniation at 2 yrs · limited beats complete discectomy

Techniquemicroscope = loupes · endoscopic → lower infection · all MIS techniques similar

Rehabearly return to high-intensity exercise (~4 wks) · Orthobullets 4–6 wks
✗ Wrong options: bed rest · avoid exercise 6–8 wks

Endoscopic anatomy (TO)

Kambin's triangleanswer not keyed by source · options: exiting root / superior endplate / traversing root vs alternatives — the commonly taught boundaries are exiting root (hypotenuse), superior endplate of the lower vertebra (base), traversing root / dural sac (height)

Recurrent herniation & postoperative course

Definitionrecurrent sciatica at the operated level after a 6-month pain-free interval (ipsi- or contralateral)

Rate5–15% · SPORT reoperation 15% at 8 yrs, with or without durotomy · protective: discrete herniation, annular defect < 6 mm

Treatmentnon-op first → revision discectomy · outcomes = primary (equal leg pain + function) · fusion only if instability
✗ Wrong options: PLIF · ALIF · disc replacement · interspinous spacer

Post-op MRI Diagnosis Action
3 wks, recurrent leg pain + radicular signs, no enhancement of disc Recurrent herniation Revision laminotomy + discectomy
8 wks, back pain only, no neuro, Modic endplate change Spondylosis Physical therapy
Disc fluid + enhancement Discitis Biopsy + IV antibiotics

Chronic pain & central sensitisation

Pain diagramscreening tool for pain sensitisation, psychosocial load, pain-resource use · correlates positively with anxiety/depression · not sensitive or specific for surgical benefit

Central sensitisationamplified CNS neural signalling → pain hypersensitivity · multiple surgeries, solid fusion, negative infection work-up, PI-LL 9° (normal)
✗ Wrong options: coronal deformity · PJK · adjacent segment

Far lateral disc + DRGDRG compression → more severe pain than paracentral
✗ Wrong options: CBT most cost-effective · standard laminotomy · stimulator · ALIF

Chronic discogenic back painCBT added to non-op care improves function (= surgery) · disc replacement no clinically significant gain · fusion ~60% satisfaction · discography ↑ herniation at tested level · successful treatment improves depression

Return to play

Athletesmore games played before injury → better return · older age → worse · RTP ~80%, only ~60% active at 2 yrs · football: less likely to return but greatest benefit from surgery

6Complications

Complication Fact
Dural tear 0–4% (Orthobullets) · 1% (OB) · watertight repair · no effect on long-term outcome
Recurrent HNP 5–15%
Wound infection Up to 3% · epidural abscess 0.3%
Discitis 2.3% → IV antibiotics ± I&D
Epidural fibrosis Radicular symptoms; 3.2× recurrent radiculopathy after revision
Chronic LBP Central sensitisation; Modic changes
Vascular catastrophe Through anterior annulus → aorta / IVC
Instability Over-resection of lamina / pars → fusion

Post-op neuro deficit timing (TO)

Timing Cause
Immediate Battered root (retraction), screw / decompression injury
Delayed, day 3 after TLIF Haematoma (✓) or gel foam → immediate re-exploration + evacuation · prevent with haemostasis + drain

Fusion approach complications

ALIF transperitoneal Retrograde ejaculation (4–10×) — superior hypogastric plexus at L5-S1 → internal sphincter fails
ALIF retroperitoneal Ureter
Lateral / XLIF / OLIF Lumbar plexus
Long S1 pedicle screw L5 root on the anterior sacral ala

How it's asked

Lumbar Spinal Stenosis

Spine · 36 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidencecommonest reason for lumbar surgery > 65 yrs · seen in 20–25% · M:F 1.5:1 · average age 65

LevelL4-5 (91%)

Risk factorsCaucasian, ↑ BMI, congenital anomalies (20%: short pedicles + laminae)

Strongest factor for symptomatic degenerative disc diseasegenetic predisposition (TO)
✗ Wrong options: heavy lifting · vibration · tobacco

Pathoanatomy

Cascadenucleus loses cells, water, proteoglycan → disc height ↓, annulus bulges → anterior column absorbs less stress → force transfers to posterior elements → facet hypertrophy, osteophytes, ligamentum flavum buckling / hypertrophy

Compressing structuresbony: facet osteophytes, uncinate (posterior body) spur, spondylolisthesis · soft: bulging disc, ligamentum flavum, synovial facet cyst

Commonest compression sourcesuperior articular process (facet hypertrophy in the lateral recess) (TO)
✗ Wrong options: disc · lamina · inferior articular process

Posture (TO)canal space maximal in flexion (and axial distraction) · ↓ with extension + axial loading → side-lying (flexed) better than flat · uphill easier · shopping-cart sign

Facetsbecome more coronal moving inferiorly

Ligamentum flavum

Originventral surface of the cranial lamina, 60–70% of the way up (closer to the superior edge) · inserts on the superior edge of the caudal lamina (OB) · TO: origin lower half of anterior surface of lamina above, insertion posterior surface + upper margin of lamina below — read the options

Composition80% elastin, 20% type I collagen (✗ Wrong options: type 2) · posterior column (✗ Wrong options: middle column) · not the primary flexion restraint (= supraspinous ligament)

3Presentation

Neurogenic claudicationback, buttock, thigh pain/heaviness · worse with extension (walking, standing, downhill/down stairs, lying supine) · relieved by flexion (sitting, shopping cart, cycling, fetal position)

Neuro examoften normal (examined seated) · SLR usually negative · no UMN signs (clonus, Babinski = cord) · no step-wise progression (= cervical myelopathy) · Valsalva doesn't worsen (unlike HNP)

Kemp signunilateral radicular pain from foraminal stenosis worse with back extension

Bladderrecurrent UTIs up to 10% (autonomic sphincter dysfunction) · CES rare

L2-3 / L3-4 stenosispain in anterior thigh + anteromedial leg
✗ Wrong options: plantar/lateral foot = S1 · anterolateral leg/dorsum = L5 · inguinal = L1

Neurogenic vs vascular claudication

Neurogenic Vascular
Postural change Yes No
Walking upright Symptoms Symptoms
Standing still Symptoms persist Relieves
Sitting Relieves Relieves
Stairs Up easier (flexed); down worse Down easier; up worse
Stationary bike (flexed) Relieves Causes
Leaning backwards Worse (✓ favours neurogenic) No effect
Pulses Normal Abnormal / asymmetric
Other — Night pain relieved by hanging leg, trophic changes, calcified iliacs

Suspected vascular → ankle-brachial index · ABI 0.4–0.8 → claudication
✗ Wrong options: SNRB · EMG/NCS · hip X-ray · CT myelogram

Hip-spine syndromehip OA + stenosis → diagnostic lidocaine + steroid injection (hip or epidural) to find the primary generator · severe stenosis → decompress anyway (risk of motor weakness)
✗ Wrong options: THA · decompression first · EMG

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
Standing AP + lateral Degenerative changes, degenerative spondylolisthesis, scoliosis · poor clinical correlation
Flexion-extension Segmental instability → decides fusion
MRI Modality of choice · thecal sac < 100 mm², lost perineural fat, facet + flavum hypertrophy · positive in asymptomatic people
CT myelogram Can't have MRI; bony detail · superior to MRI for decompression planning (OB) · can't separate vascular from neurogenic
Plain myelogram Dynamic cut-off in extension (invasive)

Diagnostic criteria – critical central stenosis (axial CT)

Measure Threshold
AP canal diameter < 10 mm
Cross-sectional area < 100 mm²
No Transverse < 20 mm, combined < 30 mm, area 130 mm², AP < 20/50 mm

Anatomic classification

Zone Cause Root (at L4-5)
Central Flavum hypertrophy + bulging disc → thecal sac Lower / non-specific (L5)
Lateral recess (subarticular) Facet arthropathy, superior articular facet overgrowth Descending / traversing (L5)
Foraminal (between pedicle borders) Disc-height loss, foraminal disc/osteophyte, scoliosis · superior facet overhang Exiting (L4)
Extraforaminal (lateral to pedicle) Far lateral disc Exiting (L4)

Etiologic classification

Type Examples
Acquired Degenerative (commonest), post-surgical, post-traumatic, AS, Paget, acromegaly, fluorosis
Congenital Short pedicles, medial facets · idiopathic or developmental (achondroplasia)

5Management

Lumbar spinal stenosis
  • First line→NSAIDs, PT, WEIGHT LOSS, BRACE, EPIDURAL STEROID
  • Failed 3–6 months / progressive deficit→WIDE DECOMPRESSION
    • Stable (normal flexion-extension)→DECOMPRESSION ALONE
    • Instability / spondylolisthesis / > 50% facets removed→DECOMPRESSION + INSTRUMENTED FUSION

Natural history & SPORT

Non-op prognosismost get some relief within 3 months · > 50% non-op satisfactory at 10 yrs · delay of surgery doesn't worsen result · complete myelographic block responds poorly · catastrophic neuro progression unlikely

SPORT (as-treated)surgery → better pain + function (+ disability, satisfaction) at 2 and 4 yrs · same 4-yr mortality · benefit persists at 8 yrs (some convergence)

Reoperation~13% at 4 yrs, ~half restenosis at same level
✗ Wrong options: 25%

Commonest surgical complication (SPORT)dural tear
✗ Wrong options: infection

ESImay avoid surgery (Orthobullets) · efficacy vs placebo questioned (OB) · temporary relief only → surgery

Operative criteria

Procedure Indication
Decompression alone Persistent pain 3–6 months failing non-op · progressive deficit (weakness, bowel/bladder) · no instability
Decompression + instrumented fusion Segmental instability (isthmic / degenerative spondylolisthesis, degenerative scoliosis) · surgical instability (complete laminectomy + > 50% facets)
✗ Wrong option: fusion Stable spine (even stable grade I listhesis elsewhere) — fusion adds nothing (Försth: same 2- and 5-yr outcomes, more time, blood, cost)
✗ Wrong option: microdiscectomy Flavum / facet stenosis (any approach)
✗ Wrong option: fusion alone Doesn't decompress
✗ Wrong options: facet injection · manual therapy Don't treat neurologic symptoms

Iatrogenic instability (TO)

Destabilising (✓ needs fusion) Not destabilising
Bilateral inferior facet (inferior articular process) resection Near-total discectomy (> 50% nucleus)
> 50% facet removal Laminotomy + spinous process removal (+ interspinous ligament)
— Medial facetectomy ≤ 1/4 (20%)
— Unilateral hemilaminectomy

Rule of thumb (TO)unilateral decompression: up to half of a facet · bilateral: one third each side

Surgical technique

Wide pedicle-to-pedicle decompression (L4-5)inferior half L4 spinous process · L4 lamina to flavum insertion · flavum · medial facetectomy + lateral recess undercutting · explore L4-5 + L5-S1 foramina, palpate L4 + L5 pedicles

Level matchingsingle-level → single-level laminectomy · multilevel → multilevel laminectomy · stenosis at L3-4 + stable L4-5 listhesis → L3-4 laminectomy alone
✗ Wrong options: L4-5 laminotomy only

Midline-sparingunilateral laminectomy undercutting the spinous process preserves spinous process + inter/supraspinous ligaments

Stenosis + spondylolisthesis / instabilitybilateral laminectomies + single-level (L4-5) instrumented fusion
✗ Wrong options: L4-S1

Fusioninstrumentation controversial · PLIF/TLIF accepted, not superior · adjacent segment degeneration > 30% at 10 yrs

Outcomescommonest cause of failure recurrence above/below · comorbidities = strongest predictor of outcome

Prognostic factors

Strongest predictor (stenosis surgery)comorbid medical conditions
✗ Wrong options: smoking · multilevel · income

Degenerative disc disease surgeryworkers' compensation most predictive
✗ Wrong options: duration · severity · levels · arthroplasty

Chronic preop opioidsprolonged hospital stay, ↑ costs, harder analgesia, ↓ PROs
✗ Wrong options: more SSI

Fusion assessment & bone health

Most reliable fusion assessmentCT (X-ray only 60–80% accurate)

Osteoporosis + fusionteriparatide improves fusion rates
✗ Wrong options: denosumab · alendronate · clodronate · calcitonin

Related concepts

Adult deformity HRQLsagittal balance = best predictor
✗ Wrong options: coronal · curve correction · shoulder

SLRpain below knee at 30–70°

Far lateral disc (L3-4 → L3, quad weakness)lateral paraspinal (Wiltse) approach + partial discectomy
✗ Wrong options: midline · fusion

6Complications

Complication Rate / fact
Wound infection 10% (fusion) · discectomy 0.7% → 1.4% with microscope · deep → I&D + cultures + antibiotics
Pneumonia / renal failure 5% each
Persistent neuro deficit 4%
Transient neuro deficit 36%
UTI / transfusion / confusion 34% / 27% / 27%
Risk ↑ with Age, blood loss, levels fused

Persistent leg pain after decompression

Timing Cause
No improvement from day 1 Residual foraminal stenosis (inadequate decompression)
Recurrence after pain-free interval Recurrent herniation, instability, restenosis
Slow axial pain Infection
Immediate saddle anaesthesia CES (rare)
Decompression alone in degenerative listhesis Instability

Dural tear

Repaired watertightoutcome not affected · drains safe · commoner in revision · rate 2–17%

Persistent CSF leakpositional headache + nausea weeks later, normal WBC/ESR → MRI → reoperation = gold standard (fibrin glue, subarachnoid drain, blood patch less invasive) · lumbar drain only after failed repairs

Surgical site infection

Risk factorsHbA1c > 7.5% (✓), op time > 3 h, posterior approach, BMI > 35 (> 30 obese), age > 70, active smoking, albumin low

Morphine paste on dura↑ SSI (epidemic) · ✓ protective: intrawound vancomycin, clipping, alcohol prep · chlorhexidine wash not proven

Lateral transpsoas fusion

Structure Injury result
Genitofemoral nerve (L1-2) — on psoas, mid-axis L3-4; at risk at L4-5 Groin / medial thigh numbness + burning ("liquid trickling") — commonest lateral-approach nerve injury
Sympathetic chain (anterolateral spine) Ipsilateral leg anhidrosis, warmth (post-sympathectomy)
Femoral / obturator Prolonged retraction at L4-5
Ilioinguinal Base of penis
Iliohypogastric Suprapubic skin
Superior hypogastric plexus Retrograde ejaculation

X vs Y

Stenosis Disc herniation
Age 65 40s
Worse Extension, walking, standing Sitting, flexion, cough
SLR Negative Positive
Treatment Laminectomy Microdiscectomy

Lumbar Spine Anatomy

Spine · 37 / 69

1Basics

Alignment

Lumbar lordosisaverage 60° (range 20–80°) · apex L3 · mostly from the disc spaces

Osteology

Vertebral bodieslargest in the axial spine

Part Fact
Pedicles Project posteriorly from posterolateral body corners
Laminae Posteromedial from pedicles, join midline
Mammillary processes Separate ossification centres; project posteriorly from the superior articular facet · pedicle screw landmark
Pars interarticularis Bone between superior + inferior facets · site of spondylolysis
Facet (zygapophyseal) joint From pedicle–lamina junction · more coronal moving distally

Ligamentum flavum

Attachments (TO)origin lower half of the anterior surface of the lamina above · insertion posterior surface + upper margin of the lamina below · OB: origin ventral cranial lamina 60–70% of the way up (closer to superior edge), insertion superior edge of caudal lamina — read the options

MRIlow signal on T2 · thickened flavum → stenosis
✗ Wrong options: synovial cyst and epidural CSF are high signal

Pedicle anatomy

Landmarkssuperior-inferior midpoint = midpoint of transverse process · medial-lateral midpoint = lateral border of pars

Pedicle screw entry (TO)mammillary process, lateral to pars · lumbar: intersection of line through lateral facet and line bisecting transverse process, ~1 mm below the facet joint · laterality: mammillary > intersection > pars start points

Danger (TO)lumbar root lies in the upper third of the foramen, just inferior to the medial pedicle wall → medial or inferior breach more dangerous than lateral/superior

Measure Fact
Medial angulation Increases distally: L1 12° → L5 30° → S1 39° · largest horizontal angle at L5 (TO)
Smallest lumbar diameter L1 (OB, Orthobullets)
Smallest overall T4 (Orthobullets) · T5 horizontal (TO) — read the options
Widest L5 horizontal; T11 sagittal · S1 ~19 mm
Sagittal angle Caudal at L5, cephalad L3–T1 (TO)
Thoracic pedicle 62–79% cancellous · lateral wall thinner than medial

Cortical : cancellous ratio

Highest → lowest
Thoracic pedicles
Lumbar pedicles
Vertebral bodies
Sacrum (lowest)

Cervical pedicles (TO)

SizeC3 smallest, C2 + C7 largest · C3–C5 need < 4.5 mm screws

Angulationmedial at all levels: most C5, least C2 + C7 · upward at C2-3, parallel C4-5, downward C6-7

Vertebral arteryat risk C3–C6 (lateral cortex thinnest; medial cortex ~2× thicker) · less at risk C2 + C7-T1 (outside foramen at C7)

Neural structures

Root ruleexits under the same-numbered pedicle · central/paracentral → traversing root · far lateral → exiting root

Cervical vs lumbarcervical C6 under C5 pedicle (mismatch, extra C8 root) · lumbar L5 under L5 (match) · vertical lumbar roots → paracentral + foraminal discs hit different roots

Cauda equinabegins ~L1

Pain innervation

Discsinuvertebral nerve → outer annulus only (nociception + proprioception) · discogenic pain: axial, worse sitting/bending, no radicular signs
✗ Wrong options: nucleus · DRG direct

Duraintradural communicating nerve (central herniation pain)

Dorsal ramus branchesmedial → facet capsule + multifidus · intermediate → longissimus, spinalis · lateral → iliocostalis + skin

Facet dual supplyeach facet from the medial branch of the level above + same level · L4-L5 facet block = L3 + L4 medial branches
✗ Wrong options: ventral rami · lateral/intermediate branches

Ventral ramianteromedial trunk

Blood supply

Segmental arteriesdorsal branches → dura + posterior elements · tie off for corpectomy

4Diagnosis & Classification

Lumbopelvic parameters

Parameter Definition Formula
Pelvic incidence (PI) Line S1 endplate centre → femoral head centre vs perpendicular to S1 endplate PI = PT + SS · fixed, independent of pelvic position
Pelvic tilt (PT) Same line vs vertical PT = PI − SS
Sacral slope (SS) S1 endplate vs horizontal SS = PI − PT

PIcorrelates with spondylolisthesis severity (Meyerding grade)

Image-guided interventions

Procedure Use
Selective nerve root injection Unilateral radicular symptoms · diagnostic + therapeutic · transforaminal (outside-in)
Facet injection Confirms facet as pain generator · may be therapeutic
Epidural injection Lumbar spinal stenosis
Discography Controversial · concordant vs discordant pain · shows annular tears

Needle22–25 G, CT or fluoroscopy

5Management

Surgical approaches

Approach Facts
Posterior midline PLIF / TLIF
Wiltse paraspinal Far lateral pathology
Retroperitoneal (anterolateral) Aortic bifurcation L4-5 · superior hypogastric plexus on L5 body → retrograde ejaculation
Lateral transpsoas Left side (aorta tolerates retraction) · ideal L1-2, L2-3, L3-4 · L4-5 = highest plexus risk (hip flexion + knee extension weakness) · T12-L1 needs rib + diaphragm takedown

Transpsoas safe zone

Cranial → caudalsafe zone shrinks because the lumbar plexus moves ventral · triggered EMG helps
✗ Wrong options: vessels move; great vessels roughly constant

Nerve injured Result
Ilioinguinal + iliohypogastric Delayed (~2 wks) abdominal wall paresis — bulge mimics hernia (CT excludes) · groin paraesthesia · resolves with observation
Genitofemoral Genital numbness, lost cremasteric reflex
Femoral Knee extension weakness, anterior thigh numbness
LFCN Lateral thigh (aggressive retroperitoneal fat dissection)
Obturator Medial thigh numbness, adduction weakness

Risk factorsmultilevel, long retraction, excessive bed break, hips not flexed

Retroperitoneal relations (TO)

Anterior to L3 bodyanswer not keyed by source (options aorta, ureter, right iliac artery, IVC) · Orthobullets: aorta bifurcates at L4-5, so the iliac arteries lie below L3

Neuromuscular vs idiopathic curves

Neuromuscular curves
Progression More rapid; may progress after maturity
Pelvis Pelvic obliquity
