Surgery ↑ Pulmonary complications

Paralytic scoliosis after childhood SCI (TO)

Cervical complete SCI before age 10paralytic scoliosis in virtually 100% (TO answer: 6-yr with C4-5 fracture-dislocation → 100%) · risk depends on level + age at injury

5Management

Outcomespinal fusion is the only treatment with proven benefit on deformity · carers report excellent QoL improvement

Classification & treatment

Category Disease Non-op Operative
Upper motor neuron Cerebral palsy Boston underarm brace until puberty (10–12), wheelchair modification Group I → PSF · Group II → PSF ± ASF + fusion to pelvis (Luque-Galveston) · indications: curve > 50°, worsening pelvic obliquity with sitting imbalance
Rett syndrome Brace C-shaped curves PSF — thoracic curve affecting sitting/balance, C-curves failing brace
Muscle weakness Spinal muscular atrophy Boston brace until puberty PSF to pelvis (wheelchair sitting) · release hip contractures first · may transiently lose UE function
Muscular dystrophy Bracing contraindicated PSF ± pelvis (controversial) · curve > 20° or progressive · operate early (20–30°) before pulmonary function declines
Paralytic Spina bifida, SCI Bracing contraindicated ASF + PSF with pelvic fixation (anterior fusion needed — deficient posterior elements)
Polio Boston brace until puberty PSF

6Complications

Wound complication risk factors

Risk factor Not a risk factor
Serum albumin < 3.5 g/dL Hemiplegia
WBC / lymphocytes < 1,500 cells/µL Number of levels fused
VP shunt Blood loss, transfusion
Severe spastic quadriplegia, non-ambulatory, seizures Preoperative curve magnitude
— Age, length of surgery

Infection rate4–14% (> idiopathic)

How it's asked

"Wound complication risk factors in CP — EXCEPT?"hemiplegia

"Known risk factor for wound infection after NM fusion?"albumin < 3.5 g/dL
✗ Wrong options: levels · blood loss · curve size

"Paralytic scoliosis after complete C4-5 SCI at 6 yrs?"100% (TO)

Occipitocervical Instability

Spine · 40 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Traumatic15–30% of cervical injuries at the occipitocervical junction · in 19% of fatal cervical injuries

Acquiredcommonest in Down syndrome · usually asymptomatic, found on screening (surgery, Special Olympics)

Synonymsatlanto-occipital dissociation (AOD), occipitocervical dislocation

Pathophysiology

Traumatichigh-energy translation or distraction · head usually displaces anteriorly

Acquiredbony dysplasia, ligament laxity · RA, Down syndrome

Associationsatlantoaxial instability, neuro deficit, vertebral / carotid artery injury, Down syndrome

Anatomy

Occipital condyleslateral foramen magnum · near vertebral arteries, medulla, CN IX–XII

Joints (6)anterior + posterior median atlanto-odontoid, paired atlanto-occipital, paired atlantoaxial

Ligament Attachment Role
Transverse (TAL) Behind dens → C1 medial tubercles (horizontal limb of cruciate) Primary atlantoaxial stabiliser · "strongest ligament of the spine" (TO)
Alar (paired) Dens → occipital condyles (+ C1 lateral mass) Strong · OC stability · limits C1 rotation on C2 (TO)
Apical Dens tip → foramen magnum Weak
Tectorial membrane Posterior C2 body → anterior foramen magnum Cephalad continuation of the PLL · atlanto-occipital stability
Anterior AO membrane Foramen magnum → C1 anterior arch Continuation of ALL (TO)
Posterior AO membrane Foramen magnum → C1 posterior arch —

C1-2 joint (TO)near-horizontal facets → 60% of cervical rotation

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Measure Normal / abnormal
X-ray (AP, lateral, odontoid) Low sensitivity (57%)
Powers ratio = BC / OA (basion–posterior arch ÷ anterior arch–opisthion) ~1 normal · > 1 → anterior dislocation · < 1 → posterior AOD, odontoid or atlas ring fracture
Harris rule of 12 BDI or BAI > 12 mm → dissociation
Condyle–C1 interval (CCI) Most sensitive + specific · normal < 2.5 mm
CT Gold standard for bone · midsagittal reconstruction
CT angiogram Vertebral artery injury; anatomy before fusion
MRI Ligament injury with preserved alignment, occult injury, deficits · AOD with normal CT measurements → MRI

Traynelis classification (direction)

Type Displacement
I Anterior occiput
II Longitudinal (distraction)
III Posterior occiput

Harborview classification (instability)

Stage Injury Stability
I Minimal / undisplaced unilateral ligament injury Stable
II Minimally displaced, significant MRI soft-tissue injury (traction test) Stable or unstable
III Gross malalignment (BAI or BDI > 2 mm beyond normal) Unstable

Atlantoaxial instability criteria (TO)

Measure Threshold
ADI (flexion lateral) > 3 mm adult, > 5 mm child → TAL rupture
PADI / SAC Normal 17–29 mm · < 14 mm → cord compression
Rule of Spence (open mouth) C1 lateral mass overhang > 6.9 mm → TAL disruption

Causes of AAI (TO)trauma (hyperflexion), Morquio, Down, skeletal dysplasia, os odontoideum, Grisel (infection), RA, NF

5Management

Occipitocervical instability
  • Traumatic→PROVISIONAL HALO / TONGS (no traction or prolonged collar)
    • Definitive→OCCIPITOCERVICAL FUSION
      (traction only considered in Harborview stage 2 with preserved alignment)
  • Acquired (Down, RA)
    • Asymptomatic→OBSERVE
    • Myelopathy / significant pain / RA basilar invagination / tumour→OCCIPITOCERVICAL FUSION

Traumatic OC dissociation (intubated polytrauma)instrumented occipitocervical fusion
✗ Wrong options: Philadelphia collar · halo 12 wks as definitive · ACDF

Occipitocervical fusion technique

Set-upprone, Mayfield, set O-C2 angle on lateral fluoroscopy before draping · Occiput–C3 instrumentation

C1 dissectionstay < 1 cm lateral to midline above the C1 posterior arch → protects vertebral artery

Occipital screwsunicortical (dural venous sinuses under the external occipital protuberance) · safe zone: 2 cm lateral + 1 cm inferior to EOP along superior nuchal line (Orthobullets) · triangle 2 cm lateral, 2 cm inferior to EOP (OB) — read the options

Thickest occiput5 cm lateral to EOP → too lateral for screws; Mayfield posterior pin site

C1 anterior cortex breachinternal carotid artery (< 3 mm from C1) · also bicortical C1 or C1-2 transarticular screws
✗ Wrong options: vertebral · basilar · spinal arteries

C2pars, pedicle, transarticular or translaminar · C3 lateral mass aimed cephalad + lateral · complications: nonunion, bleeding (internal carotid, vertebral artery)

Atlantoaxial subluxation – Fielding & Hawkins (TO)

Type Injury Treatment
I Rotatory, TAL intact Collar 6–8 wks
II Anterior 3–5 mm, one lateral mass pivot, TAL deficient Collar / surgery
III Anterior > 5 mm C1-2 fusion (unstable)
IV Posterior C1 on C2 (odontoid erosion) C1-2 fusion (unstable)

Surgery (TO)adult: ADI > 5 mm, dynamic instability · child: deficit, persistent ADI > 4 mm, deformity > 3 months, recurrence despite 6 wks immobilisation

AARS (TO)"cock robin" (20° tilt one way, 20° rotation other, 10° flexion) · Grisel syndrome · CT shows rotation · child, trivial fall, transient quadriparesis, recovered → congenital atlantoaxial instability
✗ Wrong options: bifid atlas · ossified flavum · syrinx

How it's asked

"Down syndrome OC fusion — site for 8 mm unicortical screw?"point B, within 2 cm lateral/inferior of the EOP

"C1 drill through anterior cortex endangers?"internal carotid artery

"Tectorial membrane on MRI?"posterior C2 body → anterior foramen magnum (PLL continuation)

"Intubated MVC, OC dissociation — definitive treatment?"occipital-cervical fusion with instrumentation

"10-yr, trivial fall, transient quadriparesis?"congenital atlantoaxial instability (TO)

Odontoid Fracture

Spine · 41 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidencecommonest axis fracture · 10–15% of cervical fractures · commonest cervical fracture in the elderly (> 65)

Bimodalyoung: high-energy head trauma (hyperflexion/extension) · elderly: simple falls, often missed, ↑ morbidity + mortality · children: rare, almost always at the basilar synchondrosis

Displacementanterior (hyperflexion) → TAL failure + AAI · posterior (hyperextension) → C1 anterior arch strikes dens

Anatomy

Ossification5 centres: body, 2 neural arches, odontoid, secondary (tip) centre · subdental (basilar) synchondrosis fuses ~6 yrs (5–7) · secondary centre appears ~3, fuses ~12 yrs → fused synchondrosis + unfused tip = 8–10 yrs

Ligamentstransverse = primary atlantoaxial stabiliser (limits anterior C1 translation) · alar + apical limit rotation

Blood supplywatershed at the waist: apex from internal carotid branches · base from vertebral arteries → type II nonunion

Cervical kinematics

Joint Flex/ext Rotation Lateral bend
Occiput–C1 50 4 8
C1-2 10 50 0
C3–C7 50 50 60
Total 110 100 68

C1-2 fusion~50% loss of rotation

Os odontoideum

Causelikely old trauma (not failed fusion) · looks like a type II fracture · observe if no deficit/instability · C1-2 fusion if deficit or instability

3Presentation

Symptomsneck pain worse with rotation · dysphagia (retropharyngeal haematoma)

Neuro deficitvery rare (large canal at C1-2)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP, lateral, open-mouth Fracture best seen on open-mouth view
Flexion-extension Type I (rule out OC instability) + os odontoideum · instability: ADI > 10 mm, SAC < 13 mm
CT Study of choice — pattern + stability
CT angiogram Vertebral artery course before posterior screws (aberrant VA up to 30%)
MRI Only if neuro symptoms

Anderson & D'Alonzo

Type Fracture Note
I Oblique tip avulsion — alar ligament Rare; rule out atlanto-occipital instability (flexion-extension)
II Waist Highest nonunion (watershed) · 35% of C2 fractures
III Into cancellous C2 body ± C1-2 joint Good blood supply + large cancellous surface → heals

Grauer (type II)

Subtype Pattern Treatment
IIA Undisplaced / minimal, no comminution External immobilisation
IIB Displaced, line anterosuperior → posteroinferior ("anterior oblique") Anterior odontoid screw (good bone)
IIC Anteroinferior → posterosuperior or comminuted Posterior C1-2 stabilisation

Nonunion risk factors (type II)

Factor Threshold
Posterior displacement > 2 mm (strongest predictor) (Orthobullets) · > 5 mm (TO, OB) — read the options
Displacement magnitude ≥ 5 mm (> 50% nonunion) (Orthobullets) · > 6 mm = best predictor (OB)
Age > 40 (Orthobullets) · > 50 (OB) · > 65 (TO) · not age < 40
Delay > 4 days (✗ Wrong options: 36–48 h)
Angulation > 10° (≥ 11° OB) · not 7°
Gap > 1 mm
Others Smoking, comminution, diabetes, osteoporosis
No Gender

Overall nonunion33% (up to 88%) · elderly non-op 60–71%

5Management

Type / patient Treatment (Orthobullets)
Type I Collar (soft collar acceptable, OB)
Type II < 40 (no risk factors) Halo vest (or hard collar)
Type II 40–80 Surgery
Type II > 80 Collar
Type III Collar 6–12 wks (✗ Wrong options: halo advantage)
Type II odontoid
  • Young, no nonunion risk→HALO VEST (or hard collar)
    (halo if displacement < 6 mm, reduced within 1 wk, age < 60 → > 90% union)
  • Risk factors, reducible, oblique IIB, good bone, normal habitus→ANTERIOR ODONTOID SCREW
  • Risk factors otherwise / elderly surgical candidate→POSTERIOR C1-2 FUSION
  • Elderly, comorbid / > 80→RIGID COLLAR (fibrous union acceptable)

Elderly type II

Halohighest morbidity + mortality (aspiration, pneumonia, dysphagia, pin problems) → contraindicated in elderly, especially dementia, COPD

Posterior C1-2 fusion (surgical candidate)↑ union + ↓ mortality vs non-op · ↑ SF-36 · less dysphagia than halo · higher fusion than anterior screw

Not a surgical candidaterigid collar 6–8 wks · fibrous union likely and usually stable enough

> 80 yrshigh morbidity + mortality regardless of treatment · collar recommended

Nonop vs op in elderlynonop → ↑ nonunion
✗ Wrong options: VA occlusion · myelopathy · radiculopathy

Anterior screw in elderlyavoid with barrel chest (COPD), short neck, osteoporosis (cut-out), dysphagia, displaced fractures, fractures > 4 wks old · higher failure than posterior fusion

Anterior odontoid screw

Indicationstype II with nonunion risk AND acceptable/reduced alignment + fracture line perpendicular to screw (anterior oblique, IIB) + habitus allowing trajectory

Prospreserves C1-2 rotation, fewer infections than posterior · single screw adequate

Conshigher failure than posterior C1-2 fusion · not for transverse, posterior oblique, type I/III

Posterior C1-2 fusion

Indicationstype II with nonunion risk (50–80) · type II/III nonunion · os odontoideum with deficit/instability
✗ Wrong options: PT · collar · halo · anterior screw

C1 lateral mass screws10° medial, 22° cephalad · don't breach C1 anterior cortex (internal carotid)

C2pars (commonest), pedicle, laminar · Gallie/Brooks wiring needs halo (rare)

Transarticular C1-2 screwscontraindicated with aberrant vertebral artery

Transoral odontoidectomysevere posterior dens displacement + cord compression + deficit · rarely; C1 laminectomy usually enough · combine with posterior fusion

Halo

Pin-site infectionsuperficial → tighten pins + oral antibiotics

Paediatric synchondrosis fracture

Featurescommonest C-spine fracture < 7 yrs · through basilar synchondrosis · neuro deficit rare · nonunion rare

Treatmentclosed reduction (extension) + Minerva cast or halo 6–8 wks
✗ Wrong options: soft collar · screw · fusion initially

6Complications

Nonuniontype II · symptomatic → posterior C1-2 fusion

Mortality> 80 poor with any treatment, worst with halo

X vs Y

Type II Type III
Site Waist (watershed) C2 body
Union Poor Good
Default Age/risk-based Collar

Ossification of the Posterior Longitudinal Ligament

Spine · 42 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

PopulationEast Asian (Japanese) commonest · Asian 2–3% (up to 4% of cervical patients) · Caucasian 0.2–0.7% (TO) · not European ancestry

Sex / agemen ~2× women (not prognostic) · peak 6th decade

LevelC4-C6 (C4-5, C5-6) · > 95% cervical · then thoracic, lumbar

Risk factors & labs

Risk factor Not a risk factor
Hyperinsulinaemia (even without diabetes) · diabetes HLA-B27 (= AS)
Obesity / BMI (correlate with extent) Traumatic brain injury (= HO)
Fluoride intoxication (fluorosis) — urinary fluoride ∝ severity (TO) Female sex
Hyperparathyroidism, hypophosphataemic rickets, abnormal Ca metabolism —
Growth hormone imbalance, recurrent minor trauma, infection (TO) —
High-salt low-meat diet, poor Ca absorption, mechanical PLL stress —
Genetic linkage —

Pathologyvascular infiltration → hypertrophy → endochondral ossification of PLL → lamellar bone

Associations (TO)DISH, ossified ligamentum flavum, ankylosing spondylitis, fluorosis

3Presentation

Symptomsoften asymptomatic · myelopathy (hand clumsiness, gait instability, Hoffmann, hyperreflexia) · radiculopathy · cord injury from minor trauma (central canal stenosis)

MRI cluecompression at mid-vertebral body levels, not only discs

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Role
Lateral X-ray Diagnosis + sagittal alignment (lordosis) · classification
CT Study of choice for extent + thickness of ossification · screw planning, vertebral artery
MRI Study of choice for cord compression
CT myelogram MRI contraindicated / complex bony stenosis

Pre-op work-up (TO)lateral + dynamic X-rays AND CT (both) · laminectomy only if lordosis maintained

Classification (lateral X-ray) (TO)

Type Pattern
1 Continuous Long lesion over several bodies
2 Segmental Behind each body, interrupted at discs
3 Mixed Continuous + segmental
4 Localised Over disc space, 1–2 segments

Myelopathy riskmixed + continuous progress most · occupancy ratio (OPLL ÷ canal AP) 30–60% predicts myelopathy (TO)

5Management

OPLL
  • Mild symptoms, not a surgical candidate→OBSERVE
  • Significant myelopathy→DECOMPRESS + STABILISE
    • Lordotic spine→POSTERIOR LAMINOPLASTY or LAMINECTOMY + FUSION
    • Kyphotic spine→ANTERIOR CORPECTOMY (OPLL "floated")
    • Dynamic myelopathy→INTERBODY FUSION WITHOUT DECOMPRESSION

Posterior (laminoplasty / laminectomy + fusion)needs lordosis (cord drifts back) · safer, preferred (avoids peeling OPLL off dura) · fuse after laminectomy (prevents post-laminectomy kyphosis) · risk OPLL growth

Anterior corpectomykyphotic spine · "float" the OPLL in the corpectomy site instead of excising → avoids dural tear / CSF leak

6Complications

Post-laminoplasty C5 palsycommonest root palsy (~6%) · deltoid / lateral arm (C5 dermatome) · short C5 root at apex of lordosis, cord drifts back

Recurrence / growthless with complete resection or floating

X vs Y

OPLL DISH AS Fluorosis
Ossification Posterior longitudinal ligament Anterior, flowing, ≥ 4 levels Marginal syndesmophytes Diffuse — PLL, flavum, interosseous membranes
Marker Hyperinsulinaemia Diabetes HLA-B27 Endemic fluoride (Punjab)
Anterior osteophytes Small, non-syndesmotic Large — —

Ossified ligamentum flavum (TO)

Featuresrare cause of thoracic myelopathy, East Asians · posterior compression → spastic gait, LL hyperreflexia, normal UL reflexes

ImagingT2 sagittal MRI · whole-spine screen mandatory (tandem lesions) · fine CT for planning

Treatmentdecompression only (non-op fails) · better prognosis than other thoracic myelopathy · prognosis ← preop severity + time to surgery

How it's asked

"65-yr from Punjab, myelopathy, MRI sagittal — diagnosis?"OPLL (TO)

"65-yr from Punjab, myelopathy, CT neck — diagnosis?"fluorosis (TO) — read the image and options

"Mixed OPLL with myelopathy — pre-op tests?"lateral + dynamic X-rays and CT (TO)

"Known risk factor for OPLL?"hyperinsulinaemia

"Commonest OPLL level?"C4-6

"42-yr myelopathy, cord compressed at mid-body levels, thick ossified PLL?"OPLL

"Image suggestive of OPLL?"ossified band behind the vertebral bodies on CT

"Commonest root palsy after laminoplasty?"C5

"Spastic gait, LL hyperreflexia, normal UL reflexes?"thoracic ossified ligamentum flavum (TO)

Osteoporotic Vertebral Compression Fracture

Spine · 43 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Commonest fragility fracture✓ VCF · 700,000/yr US · 70,000 admissions · $15 billion
✗ Wrong options: distal radius · femoral neck · intertrochanteric · proximal humerus

Prevalence25% > 70 yrs · 50% > 80 yrs (up to 40% of women > 80, OB)

Strongest predictor of further vertebral fractureshistory of 2 VCFs (postmenopausal women)

Osteoporosis

Definitionnormal bone quality, decreased quantity · thin cortices, ↓ trabecular continuity
✗ Wrong options: abnormal quality = osteomalacia

WHOT-score < −2.5 · after a fragility fracture → treat regardless of T-score

Peak lumbar BMDfemales 33–40 · males 19–33 (variable)

Screeningall women ≥ 65 + younger women with osteoporotic fractures

Associations & mortality

Pulmonarykyphosis → each VCF ↓ FVC up to 9% → ↑ death from pulmonary causes (pneumonia) · ↓ chest/abdominal volume → early satiety

Comorbidassociated with hypertension, smoking, calcium/vitamin D use · frailty drives both fracture + death

Mortality Value
1 yr ~15% (7–15%) — less than hip (hip ~25%)
2 yr ~20% — equivalent to hip
Ranking (1 yr) Hip > VCF > distal radius (distal radius: no ↑ mortality)
Medicare cohort ~2× controls · men > women · younger age at fracture → greater risk · HR 1.8 · survival 54% / 31% / 11% at 3 / 5 / 7 yrs

Further fracture risk20% after first VCF (OB) · ~40% within 2 yrs without osteoporosis treatment (OB) · 5× more likely (OB) — read the options

Progressive kyphosisload transfers to the superior adjacent vertebra · deformity progresses even when pain-free · worse gait, satiety, lungs

3Presentation

Painonly 25% seek attention · localised; may wrap around ribs dermatomally · tenderness on deep spinous palpation

Neuro< 1% (SCI very rare; lumbar foraminal root deficits) · no chronic pain in majority

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray whole spine Concomitant fractures 20% · height loss ≥ 20% or ≥ 4 mm
CT Inadequate films, LE deficit, posterior wall
MRI Acuity (STIR/T2 oedema = acute), PLC, cord compression, benign vs metastatic · ✓ next study after X-ray
Bone scan (3-phase) Acuity if MRI contraindicated
No Flexion-extension, tagged WBC scan

Benign vs malignant on MRI

Osteoporotic (benign) Metastatic (malignant)
Fluid sign (fluid cleft) Complete marrow replacement (diffuse homogeneous low T1)
Band-like low T1 / high T2 signal Convex posterior border
Retropulsed fragment Pedicle / posterior column involvement
— Paraspinal or epidural mass

Suspect metastasisabove T5, atypical films, constitutional symptoms, young without a fall

Labs

Work-upCBC, BMP · ESR (infection) · SPEP + UPEP (myeloma)

5Management

Osteoporotic VCF, neuro intact
  • Acute→ANALGESIA + EARLY MOBILISATION ± TLSO
    (calcitonin 4 wks if < 5 days; no bed rest)
  • Severe pain > 6 wks / Kümmell→KYPHOPLASTY
  • Progressive deficit / PLL injury / unstable→DECOMPRESSION + LONG FIXATION
    (multiple fixation points ± anterior support)

Non-operative

First lineoral analgesia (opioid acceptable) + gradual activity, walking unrestricted ± TLSO for comfort · even > 30° kyphosis or > 50% height loss if PLL intact
✗ Wrong options: strict bed rest → DVT, PE, deconditioning, bone loss

AAOS 2010 guidelineonly moderate recommendation: calcitonin 4 wks if presenting within 5 days · only strong recommendation: against vertebroplasty · kyphoplasty = limited/inconclusive option · brace, bed rest + opiates, exercise, ibandronate/strontium, L2 nerve block = inconclusive

TLSORCTs: same pain, disability, radiographs with or without brace · not strongly recommended

Calcitonindirectly inhibits osteoclasts (cell-surface receptor)

Medicalbisphosphonates prevent further fractures (atypical femur fracture risk) · teriparatide ↑ BMD without atypical femur fracture risk · exercise ↑ BMD

Vertebral augmentation

Indicationsevere pain after 6 wks of non-op (narcotic-dependent, sedentary, constipated) · not first line · not for healed VCF with kyphosis (TO)

Evidencedouble-blind sham-controlled RCTs: vertebroplasty no benefit at any time point · surgery meta-analysis (OB): greater pain improvement

Vertebroplasty Kyphoplasty
Cavity None Balloon cavity
Cement viscosity Less viscous More viscous
Injection pressure Higher Lower (TO: "kyphoplasty higher pressure" = false)
Extravasation Higher Lower
Height restoration No Goal; partial reduction
Cost / equipment Less More
Pain relief Comparable (TO) Comparable
Future VCF risk — Yes (TO)

TO trapvertebroplasty cement: high viscosity + more radiopaque than arthroplasty cement (true statement in TO stem)

Mechanismeliminates micromotion

Kümmell disease

Definitiondelayed post-traumatic vertebral body osteonecrosis · pain-free interval (months–years) → new pain, collapse, intravertebral cleft (pathognomonic) · thoracolumbar junction

Treatmentkyphoplasty
✗ Wrong options: continued non-op · vertebroplasty (leak, no correction) · fusion without deficit

Operative stabilisation

Indicationsprogressive neuro deficit, PLL injury / unstable (very rare) · not stable 30% height loss, 15° kyphosis · retropulsion makes cement risky

Techniquelong constructs, multiple fixation points, consider anterior support

6Complications

Cement extravasationinto canal → neuro injury · vertebroplasty > kyphoplasty · check posterior cortex defects

Kümmell diseaseavascular necrosis + nonunion

How it's asked

"71-yr L2 VCF, 30% height loss — management?"analgesia, TLSO, progressive activity

"Ambulatory with oxycodone — first line?"opioids + unrestricted walking

"78-yr T11 20% height loss, walker?"analgesics + progressive rehabilitation

"AAOS-supported treatment within 5 days?"activity modification + calcitonin

"Vertebroplasty vs kyphoplasty — false?"kyphoplasty uses higher cement pressure (TO)

"Cementing techniques — EXCEPT?"healed VCF with kyphosis can be cemented (TO)

"Kyphoplasty — EXCEPT?"cement under high pressure (TO)

"Narcotic-dependent 6 wks after T11 VCF?"percutaneous vertebral augmentation

"Vertebroplasty in sham-controlled RCTs?"no benefit at any time point

"Old VCF with AVN + collapse, failed conservative care?"kyphoplasty

"Surgery vs non-op meta-analysis?"greater improvement in pain

"Old T12 VCF — compared with peers?"greater risk of death from pulmonary conditions

"Acute T12 VCF — true?"2-yr mortality ≈ hip fracture

"Mortality risk after VCF?"greater in men than women

"Fragility fracture mortality, decreasing?"hip > VCF > distal radius

"T-score −3.0 — commonest fragility fracture?"vertebral compression fracture

"VCFs associated with?"20% further fracture risk

"Progressive kyphosis after VCF due to?"load transfer to the superior adjacent vertebra

"Spinal deformity of progressive osteoporotic collapse?"deformity progression

"Untreated, risk of another fragility fracture in 2 yrs?"~40%

"MRI fluid sign?"osteoporotic VCF

"75-yr L1 VCF 8 wks — next imaging?"lumbar MRI

"Pain-free interval then pain 10 months after VCF?"post-traumatic osteonecrosis (Kümmell)

"Breast cancer history — MRI finding favouring osteoporotic?"band of high T2 signal

"Osteoporosis — true?"decreased bone quantity

Pathologic Scoliosis

Spine · 44 / 69

1Basics

Definitionscoliosis from a benign bone tumour, usually in the posterior elements · any age; OO + osteoblastoma typically 2nd decade

Mechanismpainful paraspinal muscle spasm

Osteoid osteoma Osteoblastoma
Size < 2 cm nidus > 2 cm
Site Apex of the concavity of the curve · posterior elements > body Posterior elements
Curve Rigid (✗ Wrong options: flexible) —
Pain Severe, night, relieved by NSAIDs Less severe, NSAIDs help less
Neuro — May cause deficits
Histology Same as peripheral OO (✗ Wrong options: more malignant) Less ossified; ± secondary ABC

Syndrome Scoliosis incidence
Marfan (FBN1, fibrillin-1) Two-thirds — highest
Rett (MECP2) Up to half

Symptomspainful scoliosis, back pain worst at night, NSAID relief · curve usually mild

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
Standing AP + lateral Level + severity · cortical thickening with radiolucent nidus
Fine-cut CT Best for outlining the lesion + planning · low-attenuation nidus with central mineralisation, perinidal sclerosis
MRI Neurovascular proximity · may show only oedema, miss the nidus
Bone scan / SPECT Markedly ↑ uptake

Differential of painful posterior-element lesions

Lesion Clue
Osteoid osteoma Small sclerotic nidus, painful scoliosis
Osteoblastoma Larger, less ossified, ± ABC
ABC Purely lytic, no sclerotic nidus
Eosinophilic granuloma Vertebral body, purely lytic, vertebra plana
Metastasis Mixed blastic-lytic, no nidus

5Management

Pathologic scoliosis
  • Minimal curve→NSAIDs + OBSERVE (up to 36 months to resolve)
    (osteoblastoma rarely responds to NSAIDs)
  • Painful, progressive scoliosis→EN BLOC RESECTION
    (RFA case by case — usually too near neural elements)

Prognosiscurve resolves after resection if done within 15–18 months of curve onset in a child < 11 yrs

How it's asked

"Spinal osteoid osteoma — true?"occurs on the concave side of the curve
✗ Wrong options: flexible curve · never RFA · always body · more malignant

"13-yr painful scoliosis, hot bone scan, sclerotic nidus in posterior element?"osteoid osteoma

"Highest scoliosis incidence?"Marfan syndrome (TO)

Pediatric Cervical Trauma Overview

Spine · 45 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Shareuncommon, but 60% of paediatric spinal injuries · 33% present with neuro deficit · other organs in ~40%

Level< 8 yrs: 87% at C3 or above · > 8 yrs: lower cervical (adult pattern)

MechanismMVA commonest · falls (toddlers, school age) · sports (adolescents)

Why under-8s are vulnerable

Anatomylarge head : trunk, horizontal facets, ligamentous laxity, weak muscles · column stretches 5 cm without rupture

SCIcommoner + more lethal < 8 · recovery better > 8

MortalityC1 injury 17% · C4 ~4% · higher at C3 and above

3Presentation

Examfull exam (associated injuries) · suspect C-spine with head + facial trauma · document sensation incl. sacral sparing, motor, reflexes · repeat — 20% with fractures examine normal initially

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Mandatory X-raysAP, open-mouth odontoid, cross-table lateral · oblique (facets) optional · flexion-extension only under physician supervision

Normal paediatric lateral (diagnostic criteria)

Parameter Normal
Prevertebral soft tissue < 2/3 of adjacent body width
Retropharyngeal space < 6 mm at C2 · at C6: < 22 mm (Orthobullets) · < 14 mm in children, < 22 mm adults (TO) — read the options
Retrotracheal space < 14 mm
ADI < 5 mm child, < 3 mm adolescent
Pseudosubluxation C2-3 / C3-4 < 4 mm if posterior laminar line contiguous
Contour lines Smooth anterior + posterior body, spinolaminar, spinous tips · parallel facets
C3 wedging Normal in 7%
Loss of lordosis Normal in 14%

CTupper cervical fractures, atlantoaxial rotatory subluxation, canal compromise · not as screening study

MRIobtunded / closed head injury, canal compromise

5Management

Transport immobilisation

Boardstandard adult backboard → cervical flexion (kyphosis) — avoid · use occipital recess (head cut-out) or thoracic elevation (torso pad) for < 8 yrs

Alignmentin-line, external auditory meatus in line with the shoulders (relative extension)
✗ Wrong options: EAM anterior/posterior · adult board + sandbag under head

Collarscommercial collars often misfit → sandbags · paediatric collars acceptable

Treatment by injury

Treatment Indications
Observation C2-3 pseudosubluxation
Collar Stable odontoid fracture, AAI, acute AARD, stable subaxial injury
Halo (thin calvaria — many low-torque pins) Unstable odontoid, OC instability, AAI, subacute AARD, Jefferson, unstable subaxial
Surgery Unstable spine + SCI, AAI, chronic AARD

Car seat & airbag safety

Stage Recommendation
Rear-facing, rear centre Until ≥ 1 yr and ≥ 20 lb
Forward-facing seat Until ≥ 4 yrs or ≥ 40 lb
Booster Until 60–80 lb and 8 yrs
Seat belt, rear middle 8–12 yrs
Front seat From 13 yrs · airbag OFF if < 80 lb

How it's asked

"8-yr unconscious after fall — acute stabilisation?"in-line, external auditory meatus in line with shoulders

"Avoid in young children after trauma?"standard backboard

"Consistent car-safety recommendation?"75 lb 13-yr in front seat — airbag OFF

Pediatric Spinal Cord Injury

Spine · 46 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence2–5% of all SCI · 83% cervical · M:F 1.5:1

Age pattern< 8 yrs: cervical (large head-to-body ratio) · > 8 yrs: thoracolumbar · upper cervical (C1-4) > lower (C5-7)

MechanismMVA / RTA commonest · falls, sport, child abuse · sledding + ATV → thoracolumbar

Associationshead injury > 30%, intrathoracic, intra-abdominal

Injury patterns

Region Patterns
Cervical OC / atlantoaxial dislocation, atlantoaxial rotatory fixation, odontoid (synchondrosis), subaxial ligamentous, SCIWORA
Thoracolumbar Compression (commonest), burst, flexion-distraction, fracture-dislocation

Anatomy & biomechanics

Cordends at L3 at birth → L1-2 · adult size by 10 yrs

Flexibilityligamentous laxity, immature supports, shallow horizontal facets, nucleus with ↑ water + ↓ collagen cross-links · column stretches 5 cm without rupture · forces pass through weak physeal cartilage

3Presentation

Primary survey (paediatric ATLS)

CBroselow tape estimates weight

Dlog-roll + immobilisation on paediatric board or adult board with torso pad / head cut-out (avoid C-spine flexion) · Paediatric GCS

Secondary surveymyotomes + dermatomes, rectal + genital exam, bulbocavernosus reflex

Paediatric GCS

Score Motor Verbal Eyes
6 Normal spontaneous movement — —
5 Withdraws to touch Smiles, orients to sound, follows, interacts —
4 Withdraws to pain Cries but consolable, confused Spontaneous
3 Abnormal flexion Inconsistently consolable, moaning To voice / touch
2 Extension Inconsolable, agitated To pain
1 None (flaccid) None None

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Use
X-ray AP + cross-table lateral of whole spine Multilevel injury common · ± swimmer's, open-mouth, flexion-extension
CT Polytrauma, high energy, altered mental state, head/face injury · radiation risk · not a screening tool
MRI Deficit with normal X-rays (SCIWORA) · soft tissue, cord · no radiation

Retropharyngeal space (TO)< 6 mm at C2 (adults + children) · at C6 < 14 mm children, < 22 mm adults

SCIWORA (TO)

Definitionspinal cord injury without radiographic abnormality · deficit with normal X-ray + CT · often recovers (TO stem: quadriplegia, normal imaging, complete recovery in 3 months — answer not keyed by source; SCIWORA is the option matching the definition) · MRI is the study

5Management

Treatment Indications
Analgesia + activity Apophyseal, spinous process, transverse process fractures
Collar 8–12 wks Stable odontoid, AAI, acute AARD, stable subaxial
TLSO 8–12 wks Compression < 50% height loss, burst < 50% retropulsion, no deficit, purely osseous flexion-distraction (Chance)
Halo Unstable cervical (thin calvaria → skull penetration)
Cervical fusion OC, atlantoaxial, subaxial instability (OC fusion, transarticular screws, pedicle screws)
Thoracolumbar fixation Unstable burst, cord compression, irreducible fracture-dislocation, ligamentous flexion-distraction · pedicle screws 1–2 levels above + below

6Complications

Listcomplete deficit, progressive spinal deformity, poor wound healing, CES

Post-injury scoliosiscervical complete SCI before age 10 → paralytic scoliosis in virtually 100% · follow all to maturity

Prognosismost paediatric SCI incomplete · SCI commoner + more lethal < 8 · recovery better > 8

How it's asked

"9-yr complete C5-6 SCI — scoliosis risk?"100%

"Paediatric SCI — EXCEPT?""cervical injury in < 8 from small head-to-body ratio" (it's large) (TO)

"Child quadriplegic, normal X-ray + CT, recovers in 3 months?"SCIWORA (TO)

Pediatric Spondylolysis & Spondylolisthesis

Spine · 47 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Prevalence4–6% of population (defects not present at birth) · 6–7% of adolescent athletes · up to 47% of adolescent LBP · up to 47% in gymnasts, weightlifters, football linemen, divers

Ethnicity↑ Native Americans · possible autosomal dominant inheritance

LevelL5 pars (85–95%), L5 on S1 · then L4, L3 (TO)

Laterality (TO)80% bilateral, 20% unilateral · unilateral defect stresses the contralateral pars

Risk factorsrepetitive hyperextension (+ rotation) · ↑ pelvic incidence, sacral slope, sacral table index · ↓ sacral table angle

Continuum

Stage Definition
Pars stress reaction Sclerosis, no complete break
Spondylolysis Complete pars fracture
Isthmic spondylolisthesis Forward slip through the pars defect
Spondyloptosis 100% translation

Morita stages (TO)early (hairline) → progressive (wider, fragments) → terminal (sclerotic, pseudarthrosis) · healing predictors: unilateral, earlier stage, level other than L5

Progression

Lysis → listhesis~15% (all bilateral defects) · most pars defects never need surgery

Progression risk ↑
Higher grade at presentation (> Meyerding 2, > 50%)
Dysplastic (Wiltse I) slips
Slip angle > 45–50° — best predictor of progression (OB), instability, pseudarthrosis, pain
Young age at presentation

Severity of current slipcorrelates most with pelvic incidence (PI = PT + SS; constant, ~57° adult; high PI → ↑ lumbosacral shear)
✗ Wrong options: SS · PT · slip grade

3Presentation

Classicactive adolescent, activity-related LBP, relieved by rest · many asymptomatic · grade ≠ symptoms

Leghamstring tightness (commonest), buttock pain, L5 radicular pain

Commonest exam findingpain with lumbar extension, especially single-leg-stance extension
✗ Wrong options: pain on flexion · SLR · lost Achilles reflex

High-grade / dysplasticheart-shaped buttocks, flat lumbar lordosis, palpable step-off, ↑ popliteal angle

Gait (Phalen-Dickson)short stride, hips + knees flexed (crouched)

Listhetic crisissevere pain worse with extension, neuro deficit, hamstring spasm · CES rare

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
AP + lateral X-ray Best initial; lateral shows 80% of defects · obliques add 15% (OB) but no extra diagnostic value + more radiation (Orthobullets, OB) · TO: AP, lateral + bilateral obliques
Oblique Scotty dog collar (neck = pars, nose = TP, eye = pedicle, ear = SAP, front leg = IAP)
SPECT Most sensitive after normal X-rays (OB) · "gold standard" (TO) · now rarely used (radiation)
MRI As sensitive + specific as SPECT, no radiation → next test after negative X-rays · STIR oedema = acute · neuro deficit, stenosis
CT (thin cut) Best to delineate the defect · age + healing
Bone scan Most sensitive screen (lesion may be cold)
Flexion-extension Instability (> 3 mm translation abnormal, TO)

Wiltse-Newman

Type Description
I Dysplastic Congenital lumbosacral facets/sacrum, pars intact · more neuro symptoms, more progression
II-A Isthmic Pars fatigue (stress) fracture — lytic
II-B Isthmic Pars elongation (healed microfractures)
II-C Isthmic Acute pars fracture
III Degenerative (commonest overall)
IV Traumatic
V Neoplastic / pathologic
VI Iatrogenic (TO)

Marchetti-Bartolozzidevelopmental (Wiltse I + II) vs acquired (traumatic, post-surgical, pathologic, degenerative)

Meyerding

Grade Slip
I < 25%
II 25–50%
III 50–75%
IV 75–100%
V Spondyloptosis (> 100%)

Low grade I–II · high grade III–V

Slip anglelumbosacral kyphosis

5Management

Spondylolysis / spondylolisthesis
  • Asymptomatic (any grade)→OBSERVE, NO ACTIVITY RESTRICTION
  • Symptomatic lysis / low grade→ACTIVITY MODIFICATION + PT 6 MONTHS
    • Acute stress reaction or failed PT→BRACE (TLSO / LSO) 6–12 WKS
  • Failed non-op
    • L1–L4 defect, multiple defects→PARS REPAIR
    • L5 lysis / low grade (I–II)→L5-S1 POSTEROLATERAL FUSION
    • High grade (III–V)→L4-S1 POSTEROLATERAL FUSION ± reduction

Non-operative

PT6 months: hamstring stretching, pelvic tilts, abdominal strengthening · most improve

Bracingacute pars stress reaction (brace > activity restriction alone; prevents defect formation) · failed PT · chronic L5 lysis failing 3 months PT (no STIR signal) → rigid LSO 6–12 wks · antilordotic (hypolordotic) brace
✗ Wrong options: hyperextension brace

TO stepwise protocolavoid hyperextension → non-rigid corset → after 2–4 wks PT (core) → still painful at 4 wks → TLSO / Boston antilordotic brace · healing 3–6 months · return to play from 8 wks if pain-free

TO answersSPECT-proven bilateral stress fracture → activity modification + rest first · 2-month-old bilateral pars defect → antilordotic brace

Sportstopping sport ≥ 3 months improves outcome · return controversial

Operative criteria

Procedure Indication
Direct pars repair L1–L4 isthmic defect failing non-op, multiple defects · preserves motion · screw, tension wiring, screw + hook · not with disc degeneration (MRI first), facet arthropathy, listhesis
L5-S1 posterolateral fusion ± ALIF L5 spondylolysis failing non-op · low grade (I–II) failing non-op, progressive, deficit or dysplastic · in situ, ± instrumentation, TLSO
L4-S1 posterolateral fusion ± reduction High grade (III–V) · L5-S1 alone fails for > 50% slip
Gaines L5 vertebrectomy Spondyloptosis (grade V)
No Isolated ASF, pars repair with listhesis

TO surgical indicationspain > 6–12 months of conservative care, neuro deficit, worsening symptomatic listhesis, grade ≥ III, progression > 1 grade, instability > 3 mm · fusion avoided in pro athletes (adjacent segment disease: 36% reoperation at 10 yrs after posterior lumbar fusion)

TO acute grade 3 in a gymnast, no deficitobservation initially (then elective fusion) — controversial

Return to sport after fusionnon-contact 3–6 months, contact 6–12 months · ALIF may help · 90% success

Reduction

Prosrestores sagittal alignment, ↓ lumbosacral kyphosis · TO: partial slip-angle reduction favoured for high grade

Conscomplications 8–30% · L5 root commonest injury (weak EHL / great-toe extension, hip abduction, dorsal foot numbness) · sexual dysfunction · catastrophic deficit → neuromonitoring

360° fusion + decompressionmore in-hospital complications

6Complications

ListL5 root injury (commonest neuro), pseudarthrosis, slip progression, hardware failure

X vs Y

Dysplastic (I) Isthmic (II)
Pars Intact (elongated) Defect
Neuro More (intact arch traps cauda) Less
Progression Higher Lower

How it's asked

"Most sensitive test when AP + lateral normal?"SPECT

"13-yr, 6 months back pain, SPECT + CT?"spondylolysis

"Lineman with insidious LBP — exam?"pain with lumbar extension in single-leg stance

"Commonest exam finding in chronic spondylolysis?"pain with lumbar extension

"14-yr, extension pain, negative X-rays — next?"MRI

"15-yr lineman, extension pain — true?"best initial studies are AP + lateral alone

"Gymnast, MRI finding fitting rest + PT?"pars fracture (Figure A)

"Gymnast, SPECT bilateral pars stress fracture — next?"activity modification + rest (TO)

"2-month-old bilateral pars defect?"antilordotic brace (TO)

"13-yr gymnast, acute pain, SPECT hot L5 pars?"molded lumbosacral orthosis

"Asymptomatic low-grade isthmic slip in soccer player?"observe, no activity restriction

"Chronic L5 lysis, failed 3 months PT, no STIR signal?"LSO brace

"7-yr gymnast with L5 pars defect — true?"likely never needs surgery

"Spondylolysis, extension pain, hamstring tightness — initial?"activity modification ± brace + PT

"Grade I isthmic slip in 13-yr gymnast?"short activity modification + structured PT

"Parameter correlating with severity?"pelvic incidence

"Lines measuring the angle best correlating with severity?"pelvic incidence lines (C + D)

"Grade III — predicts progression?"slip angle

"Factor associated with progression?"> Meyerding 2 at presentation

"Gymnast acute grade 3 L5-S1, no deficit?"observation (TO)

"12-yr, 80% slip, failed non-op?"posterior instrumented fusion L4-S1

"Reduction of high-grade L5-S1 slip — complication?"weak great-toe extension (L5)

"14-yr, grade 4, crouched gait — surgery?"posterior fusion L4–S1 ± instrumentation

"Gait pattern in spondylolisthesis?"short stride, hips + knees flexed

"Same lesion at L3 — surgical difference?"pars interarticularis repair indicated

Pseudosubluxation of the Cervical Spine

Spine · 48 / 69

1Basics

Definitionphysiologic anterior translation (up to 4 mm / 40%) in young children

Incidence~20% of children admitted with polytrauma · no association with gender, trauma, intubation or injury severity
✗ Wrong options: commoner in girls

Age< 8 yrs

LevelC2 on C3 commonest · C3 on C4 second

Causehorizontal facet joints → become vertical with age

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Viewslateral + flexion-extension

Swischuk's linespinolaminar line C1 → C3 · C2 spinolaminar point within 1.5 mm = pseudosubluxation

Pseudosubluxation vs true subluxation

Pseudosubluxation True traumatic subluxation
Reduces on extension Persists
Spinolaminar line within 1.5 mm of C2 > 1.5 mm
No significant trauma history/exam Trauma (e.g. Hangman's fracture)
No anterior soft-tissue swelling Prevertebral swelling

5Management

Observationno ↑ morbidity or mortality

How it's asked

"Pseudosubluxation — EXCEPT?"more common in female children (TO)

"5-yr, no trauma, C2-3 step reduces on extension?"pseudosubluxation of the cervical spine

Rheumatoid Cervical Spondylitis

Spine · 49 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Prevalencecervical involvement in 90% of RA (often missed) · cervical spine = 2nd commonest RA site after peripheral joints (TO) · C1-C2 (synovial joint) = commonest segment (TO)

Risk factors (TO)older age at onset, active synovitis, ↑ CRP, rapidly erosive peripheral disease, early joint subluxation

DMARDshave ↓ cervical surgery rates

Three patterns

Pattern Frequency (Orthobullets) Frequency (TO)
Atlantoaxial subluxation (commonest) 50–80% 19–70%
Basilar invagination / cranial settling 40% 38%
Subaxial subluxation 20% 7–29%

Others (TO)inflammatory discitis, atraumatic odontoid erosion / fracture

Genes & lab markers

Anti-CCPmost sensitive + specific RA marker (✓ for C2 pannus)
✗ Wrong options: CRP/WBC (non-specific) · HLA-B27 (AS) · M-spike (myeloma)

3Presentation

Symptomsneck pain (craniocervical, 69%), stiffness · occipital headache (lesser / greater occipital nerve, C2), mastoid pain · myelopathy (hand clumsiness, gait instability, hyperreflexia) · vertebrobasilar: tinnitus, vertigo, visual change, dysphagia (TO)

C1-2 facet arthrosis / O-C1 ankylosisaxial neck pain + ↓ rotation with occipital headache
✗ Wrong options: Spurling · Hoffmann · Lhermitte

Ranawat classification (neurologic)

Class Findings
I Pain, no deficit
II Subjective weakness, hyperreflexia, dysaesthesia
IIIA Objective weakness + UMN signs, ambulatory
IIIB Objective weakness + UMN, non-ambulatory → do NOT operate (poor results)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Flexion-extension X-raysalways before elective surgery (anaesthesia)

CTbony anatomy, vertebral artery course before transarticular screws

MRIcord compression + myelomalacia (study of choice) · cervicomedullary angle

Atlantoaxial subluxation – diagnostic criteria

Measure Threshold
AADI normal < 3 mm adult (< 5 mm child)
Instability > 3.5 mm change flexion↔extension (Orthobullets) · AADI > 3 mm not fixed (TO) — not alone an indication for surgery
> 7 mm motion Alar ligament disruption
AADI surgery > 10 mm (Orthobullets, OB) · 6–10 mm or > 8 mm cited (TO)
PADI / SAC surgery < 14 mm (Orthobullets, OB) · < 13–14 mm (TO) · PADI < 14 mm 97% sensitive for deficit
PADI predicting recovery > 13 mm (Orthobullets, OB, TO) · > 14 mm (OB) — read the options

Best predictor of neuro deficitPADI · cord 10 mm + 1 mm dura + 1 mm CSF each side → PADI ≥ 14 mm (TO)
✗ Wrong options: AADI

Mechanismpannus between dens and C1 ring → destroys transverse ligament + dens · usually anterior C1 on C2

Basilar invagination – lines

Line Landmarks Abnormal
Ranawat C1-2 index Centre of C2 pedicle → C1 arch line Normal 17 mm men / 15 mm women · < 13 mm = impaction (Orthobullets) · < 15 mm in men (TO) · most reproducible
McGregor Posterior hard palate → most caudal occiput Dens tip > 4.5 mm above · routine screening line (TO)
Chamberlain Dorsal hard palate → posterior foramen magnum (opisthion) Dens > 5 mm above · hard to see
McRae Foramen magnum opening (basion → opisthion) Dens below = no impaction (asymptomatic)
Fischgold-Metzger AP view — digastric grooves Normally 10.7 mm above dens tip (TO)

Best combination for cranial settling (TO)Clark station + Ranawat + Redlund-Johnell

MRIcervicomedullary angle < 135° → impending deficit → surgery

Subaxial subluxation

Featuresmultilevel, with upper instability · ↑ with steroids, males, seropositive, nodules, severe RA · pannus + facet/Luschka laxity

Cord compressionslip > 4 mm or > 20% · cervical height index (body height ÷ width) < 2.0 → ~100% sensitive + specific for deficit

5Management

Goalprevent progression; may not reverse existing deficit · pharmacotherapy first

Operative criteria

Pattern Indication Operation
AAS AADI > 10 mm or PADI < 14 mm (even without deficit), progressive myelopathy · not 3.5 mm change alone Posterior C1-2 fusion (reducible; transarticular screws avoid halo; CT for VA)
AAS + basilar invagination / irreducible AAS — Occiput–C2 fusion ± C1 posterior arch resection
Basilar invagination Progressive migration > 5 mm, deficit, CMA < 135° Occiput–C2 fusion · transoral / retropharyngeal odontoid resection for brainstem compromise
Subaxial > 4 mm / > 20% slip + intractable pain + neuro Posterior fusion + wiring

Pannusresolves after posterior fusion (stability) → posterior fusion ± decompression for periodontoid pannus myelopathy · odontoidectomy only for residual anterior compression after fusion
✗ Wrong options: transoral resection first (CSF leak, wound, more morbidity)

TO basilar impressionsurgery by symptoms, not degree · anterior impingement → occiput-C1-2 fusion in extension ± later anterior odontoid excision · posterior impingement → suboccipital craniectomy + C1 (± C2) laminectomy

6Complications

Failure to improveRanawat IIIB

Pseudarthrosis10–20% · ↓ by extending to occiput

Adjacent level degeneration

How it's asked

"Commonest RA cervical complication?"atlantoaxial instability (TO)

"Commonest instability in RA?"atlanto-axial (TO)

"RA cervical — FALSE?""PADI more than 13–14 mm is a surgical indication" (it's less than) (TO)

"Most useful measure predicting deficit?"PADI (TO)

"RA cervical involvement occurs as?"C1-2 instability (TO)

"Most frequently involved segment?"C1-C2 (TO)

"Spastic quadriplegia — good prognostic parameter?"PADI > 13 mm (TO)

"RA myelopathy + AAS on flexion-extension?"posterior C1-C2 instrumented fusion

"Predicts neural recovery after decompression?"PADI > 13 mm

"Surgical indications EXCEPT?"3.5 mm ADI change on flexion-extension

"Predicts full recovery with stabilisation?"PADI > 14 mm

"RA periodontoid pannus myelopathy — treatment?"posterior fusion ± decompression

"Hard palate → most caudal occiput?"McGregor line (TO)

"RA, occipital headaches, C1-2 arthrosis — exam?"axial neck pain + ↓ rotation

"Radiographic surgical indication?"cervicomedullary angle 120°

"C2 pannus — lab?"positive anti-CCP

Sacroiliac Joint Dysfunction

Spine · 50 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidenceoverlooked; 15–30% of outpatient LBP

Risk factorsprevious lumbar fusion, especially > 3 levels (≈ adjacent segment disease) · pregnancy + vaginal delivery (3rd-trimester oestrogen/relaxin → hypermobility) · pelvic trauma · iliac crest graft harvest
✗ Wrong options: age > 65 · male sex · THA · prior SI injection

Causes

Mechanism Examples
Idiopathic (commonest) Repetitive trauma → capsule/ligament tension, shear, hypo/hypermobility
Arthritis ~100% of spondyloarthropathies (AS, Reiter's) · HLA-B27
Infection Haematogenous, unilateral · S. aureus, Pseudomonas, Cryptococcus, TB · IVDU, endocarditis, immunosuppression
Metabolic CPPD, gout, ochronosis, hyperparathyroidism, renal osteodystrophy, acromegaly
Tumour Metastasis commonest (pelvis 40% of bony mets, 2nd to spine) · primary rare: GCT, PVNS, chondrosarcoma
Extra-articular Enthesopathy (posterior ligaments), insufficiency fractures, post-traumatic (lateral compression pelvic injuries)

Anatomy & biomechanics

Jointlargest axial joint, ~17.5 cm² · called synovial though superior 75% non-synovial · flat until puberty → ridges by 30 → erodes by 50 · ankylosis common in men by 50

Ligamentsthin anterior capsule · posterior interosseous ligament (posterior capsule rudimentary) · sacrotuberous (→ ischial tuberosity), sacrospinous (→ ischial spine)

Innervationanterior L2–S2 ventral rami + sacral plexus · posterior L4–S4 dorsal rami (RFA targets L5–S3 lateral branches)

Biomechanicstriplanar shock absorber · "keystone in arch" · takes 6× medial load of lumbar spine but fails at 1/20 of its axial load · motion < 4° rotation, 1.6 mm translation · nutation (nodding) = commonest motion (backward rotation of ilium on sacrum)

3Presentation

Painjust inferior to the PSIS · only 4% above L5 · may radiate past knee · relieved by a tight belt

Tendernesssacral sulcus (most) > PSIS (2nd)

Neurointact (pain-inhibited weakness only)

Provocative tests

Ruleno single test 100% · > 3 positive = highly suggestive
✗ Wrong options: 2

Test Technique
FABER (Patrick) Flexion, abduction, ER → SI pain (groin pain = hip/iliopsoas)
Fortin finger Patient points twice to area inferomedial to PSIS
Gaenslen Affected hip extended off the table, contralateral hip + knee flexed → shear
SI compression Lateral decubitus, medial force on iliac crest
Anterior sacral thrust Prone, anterior force on sacrum
SI distraction Supine, posterior force on ASIS
SLR Usually negative; may hurt > 60° (SI motion)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria – SI injection

Result Meaning
> 75% pain relief after a single injection Confirms diagnosis
> 50% relief after two injections Diagnostic
Injection response Does not predict pain relief after fusion

Guidancefluoroscopy/US (blind injection reaches joint only 22%) · max 3 in 6 months / 4 per year

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray: pelvis AP, inlet, outlet, obliques, SI views, lumbar flexion-extension Narrowing, sclerosis, cysts, osteophytes, ankylosis · 25% of asymptomatic > 50 abnormal
Flamingo views Single-leg standing → pelvic instability
CT Surgical planning · poorer than injection diagnostically
MRI Exclude tumour, infection
Bone scan Specificity 90%, sensitivity 12%, PPV 86%, NPV 72%

Differential (top 5)lumbar stenosis, DDD, hip OA, hip labral tear, disc herniation

5Management

SI joint dysfunction
  • 1st line→NSAIDs, PT (core), PELVIC BELT, ± prolotherapy (≥ 4 wks)
  • 2nd line→SI CORTICOSTEROID INJECTION (60% relief at 6 months)
  • 3rd line→RADIOFREQUENCY ABLATION (lateral sacral branches)
  • Confirmed generator, failed non-op→SI ARTHRODESIS
    • Normal anatomy→MINIMALLY INVASIVE (triangular titanium implants)
    • Dysmorphic sacrum / revision / aberrant anatomy→OPEN POSTERIOR

Pelvic belt4–8 inch belt around greater trochanters (medial compression) · best after pregnancy

Prolotherapyphenol/glucose at ligament bases → scarring · best for ligamentous laxity

Injectionlower success after lumbar fusion · not first line

RFAlimited (can't denervate anterior joint)

MIS vs open arthrodesisMIS: shorter stay, smaller incision, less blood loss, earlier full weight bearing, fewer SSIs (theory) · "fusion" by ongrowth onto implants · open: posterior approach, protected WB 12 wks

6Complications

Complication Risk
SSI Immunocompromise, smoking, diabetes
Wound Open (dependent wound)
Nerve injury (L5, S1, S2) MIS, sacral dysmorphism
Pseudarthrosis Up to 5% → open revision

QoLworse than COPD + mild heart failure; ≈ hip/knee OA

How it's asked

"Buttock pain after spine fusion, >75% relief with injection — risk factor?"spine fusion

"Post-fusion SI pain, positive provocative tests — confirm with?"diagnostic SI joint injection

"SI dysfunction after 3-level fusion — true?"injection less successful after lumbar fusion

"SI joint pathology — exam finding?"pain extending hip off table with contralateral hip/knee flexed (Gaenslen)

Sacroiliitis

Spine · 51 / 69

1Basics

Definitioninflammation of the SI joint → LBP + posterior pelvic pain

Demographicsteens to middle age · M > F

Causeschronic inflammation (AS, Reiter's), infection, trauma, overuse, pregnancy · may end in fibrosis + ossification

SepticIVDU, haematogenous · gram-positive cocci (Staph ± Strep)

Genes & lab markers

Marker Fact
HLA-B27 Ankylosing spondylitis · 1–2% of all people carry it
RF Usually negative in AS
ESR / CRP Usually ↑
WBC Usually normal; ↑ in infection
Blood cultures Positive in ~50% of septic cases

Reiter'soligoarthritis + conjunctivitis + urethritis

3Presentation

Symptomspain with prolonged standing, difficulty climbing stairs, generalised LBP, ipsilateral hip weakness, morning stiffness

ExamFABER → posterior (SI) pain with otherwise painless hip ROM · AS: thoracolumbar flexion deformity

Septicfever, leukocytosis, limp; may mimic hip sepsis (hip US/exam normal) · L5 root irritation possible

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
X-ray Non-specific erosions, sclerosis, calcification
MRI with gadolinium Study of choice — T2 fluid / inflammation in SI joint, abscess
Nuclear medicine Historical

5Management

Cause Treatment
Infection IV antibiotics (until symptoms + CRP settle) → oral · surgery only if antibiotics fail or large abscess
IVDU septic shock (GPC clusters + chains) Vancomycin + clindamycin (✗ Wrong options: 1st-gen cephalosporin alone · immediate I&D)
Trauma / overuse Rest, activity modification, NSAIDs, steroid injection
Pregnancy Observe — resolves after delivery
Spondyloarthropathy Aggressive PT, NSAIDs, TNF inhibitors / biologics
Septic sacroiliitis
  • No abscess→EMPIRIC BROAD-SPECTRUM IV ANTIBIOTICS, narrow on cultures
  • Failed antibiotics / large abscess→SURGICAL DEBRIDEMENT

Prognosismost resolve

X vs Y

Septic sacroiliitis Septic hip Transient synovitis
Hip ROM Painless Painful Painful
FABER Posterior pain Groin pain —
Hip US Normal Effusion Effusion
Treatment IV antibiotics I&D NSAIDs

How it's asked

"IVDU, septic shock, SI fluid without abscess, GPC clusters + chains?"vancomycin + clindamycin

"12-yr febrile, painful FABER, normal hip, SI fluid on MRI?"broad-spectrum IV antibiotics + monitor

Scheuermann's Kyphosis

Spine · 52 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence0.4–8.3% · commonest structural kyphosis in adolescents · 2nd commonest adolescent deformity after idiopathic scoliosis (TO)

Age / sexonset 10–12 yrs (diagnosed 12–17) · M:F 2:1 to 7:1

Locationthoracic (commonest) · thoracolumbar/lumbar less common

Genes & pathology

Inheritanceautosomal dominant

Theory (most accepted)developmental error in collagen aggregation → abnormal endplate (defective endochondral ossification) · others: ring apophysis osteonecrosis, disc herniation, relative osteoporosis, biomechanical growth arrest

Histology (TO)abnormal endplate cartilage, irregular mineralisation, ↓ collagen : proteoglycan ratio

HLA-B27association stated in one OB explanation

Associations

Association Rate
Lumbar hyperlordosis Compensatory
Lumbar spondylolysis 33% (Orthobullets) · 50–54% (TO) — read the options
Scoliosis 33%
Dural cysts —
Tight hamstrings, iliopsoas, anterior shoulder —
Pulmonary compromise Curves > 100°

3Presentation

Symptomscosmesis, dull thoracic/lumbar pain (worse with activity, better with rest) · neuro deficit rare

Examrigid kyphosis, sharper angulation on forward bending · doesn't correct prone/extension · compensatory cervical/lumbar lordosis

Normal thoracic kyphosis20–45° (Orthobullets) · 20–50° T5-T12 (OB) — 38° in a 13-yr = normal · any thoracolumbar kyphosis abnormal

4Diagnosis & Classification

Diagnostic criteria (Sorensen)

Criterion
Anterior wedging > 5° of 3 consecutive vertebrae (at apex)
Irregular endplates / apophyseal lines
Schmorl's nodes (disc into endplate)
Disc space narrowing
TO: + thoracic kyphosis > 50°

✗ Wrong options: Traps — posterior wedging, lumbar hypolordosis, preserved disc height, marginal syndesmophytes (AS)

Key radiology findings

Study Use
AP + lateral standing Wedging, endplates, Schmorl's, scoliosis · C7 plumb line (sagittal balance)
Hyperextension lateral (supine over bolster) Separates postural kyphosis · Scheuermann's is inflexible
MRI Pre-op (controversial) — disc herniation, epidural cyst, cord, stenosis · any neuro deficit
Lumbar films Spondylolysis if LBP

Types

Thoracic (typical, type 1) Thoracolumbar / lumbar (atypical, type 2)
Curve T1/2–T12/L1, apex T6–T8 T4/5–L2/3, apex TL junction
Frequency Commonest Less common
Radiograph Wedging Endplate irregularity, Schmorl's, narrow discs — less wedging
Course Better prognosis More pain, more progressive
Surgical threshold 70–75° 25–30° (OB)

5Management

Scheuermann's kyphosis
  • < 60° (TO: < 50°), mild symptoms→OBSERVE + PT (extension, hamstring stretch)
  • 60–80° with growth left→EXTENSION BRACE (Jewitt / Milwaukee)
  • > 75°, neuro deficit, cord compression, severe adult pain→POSTERIOR SPINAL FUSION ± PONTE / SPO

Bracing

Thresholds60–80° with growth remaining (Orthobullets) · 50–74° skeletally immature (OB) · > 50° with ≥ 1 yr growth + 40–50% flexibility (TO) — read the options

Braceextension orthosis (Jewitt, high chest pad) · Milwaukee (TO) full time 12–18 months then part time · TLSO if apex T7–T9 or lower · Boston for < 70°

Outcomestops progression; best if < 65° and > 15° correction in brace · compliance poor
✗ Wrong options: Charleston night brace · electrical stimulation

TO answer12-yr, 55°, pain, no deficit → Milwaukee brace

Operative criteria

Indication Source
Kyphosis > 75° Orthobullets, OB
> 70–75° + pain not relieved + unacceptable cosmesis — all TO
> 60° + persistent pain OB
Neuro deficit, cord compression All
Severe pain in adults Orthobullets
Not if painless < 75° OB

Techniqueposterior-only PSF, dual rods, pedicle screws ± hooks · cantilever or compression across posterior anchors · Ponte (posterior shortening) for stiff curves: remove spinous processes, flavum, facets · anterior release rarely now · MEP + SSEP monitoring
✗ Wrong options: intracanal hooks at apex

Osteotomy choiceSmith-Petersen for long, sweeping kyphosis (~10°/level, less in rigid Scheuermann)
✗ Wrong options: PSO (AS, 30–40°/level) · VCR (sharp angular kyphosis)

Goalfinal kyphosis 40–50° · correct ≤ 50% of original

Fusion levels

Proximalwhole kyphotic Cobb

Distal (LIV)sagittal stable vertebra (SSV): most proximal body bisected by / ≥ 50% anterior to the posterior sacral vertical line · old first lordotic disc method → more DJK

Too long (e.g. L5 when SSV is L3)protects against DJK but excessively stiff → early disc degeneration

Outcomes60–90% pain improvement (not correlated with correction) · residual > 75° → worse function

6Complications

Complication Fact
Neurologic 0.6–0.8% (> AIS surgery) · cord stretch → ensure posterior shortening · reverse correction on signal change
Distal junctional kyphosis 20–30% · avoid: fuse to SSV, don't overcorrect > 50%
Proximal junctional kyphosis Overcorrection + negative sagittal balance (less common)
Others Pseudarthrosis, hardware failure, loss of correction, SMA syndrome

Junctional kyphosisCobb > 10° between fused and unfused segments

Prognosisat least some progression in 80% · adult pain rarely limits activity (mean 71°) · > 75° → severe thoracic pain

X vs Y

Scheuermann's Postural kyphosis
Rigidity Rigid Corrects on hyperextension
Wedging > 5° × 3 None
Forward bend Sharp angulation Smooth

How it's asked

"Not true regarding Scheuermann's?""cannot be corrected by postural control" (TO key — read carefully; Orthobullets calls it rigid)

"13-yr, 38° thoracic kyphosis?"normal range

"16-yr chronic back pain, wedged vertebrae — expected finding?"Schmorl's nodes

"15-yr athlete, mid-back pain — underlying condition?"anterior wedging > 5° across three consecutive vertebrae

"12-yr, 55° kyphosis with pain, no deficit?"Milwaukee brace (TO)

"Risser 2, kyphosis corrects to 50°?"extension-type spinal orthosis

"Surgical indication?"all — > 70° curve, pain + rigidity, unacceptable cosmesis (TO)

"Fixed 80° kyphosis, no correction on hyperextension?"Smith-Petersen osteotomies + posterior arthrodesis

"Fusion stopped at L5 beyond SSV (L3)?"protects against DJK but excessively stiff

"14-yr Risser 4, rigid 90° kyphosis?"posterior spinal fusion + instrumentation

Spinal Cord Anatomy

Spine · 53 / 69

1Basics

Cord overview

Length~45 cm adult · foramen magnum (continuous with medulla) → lower border of L1 (TO)

Conus medullarisL3 at birth → L1 at maturity · lower L1 in women, L1 pedicle in men (OB)

Filum terminalemodification of pia mater (TO) · conus → adheres to dura → inserts on first coccygeal segment (TO)

Thecal sacdura containing CSF, roots, cauda equina

Meningesdura (outer) → arachnoid → pia (inner) · CSF in subarachnoid space (arachnoid–pia)

Embryology

Structure Becomes
Neural tube Spinal cord · failed closure cranially → anencephaly · distally → spina bifida occulta, meningocele, myelomeningocele
Neural crest (dorsal to tube) PNS, pia mater, spinal ganglia, sympathetic trunk
Notochord (ventral to tube) Vertebral bodies, nucleus pulposus (annulus from sclerotome)

Autonomic

Sympathetic chain22 ganglia (3 cervical, 11 thoracic, 4 lumbar, 4 sacral) · cervical: superior, middle, stellate · middle ganglion at C6, medial edge of longus colli → injury → Horner's

Hypogastric plexusS2–S4 parasympathetic + lumbar sympathetic splanchnics

Functional tracts

Tract Function
Dorsal columns Deep touch, proprioception, vibration
Lateral spinothalamic Pain + temperature · cordotomy site
Ventral spinothalamic Light touch
Lateral corticospinal Main voluntary motor · UE fibres medial (central) → central cord: arms > legs
Ventral corticospinal Voluntary motor

CSF

Productionchoroid plexus (3rd, 4th, lateral ventricles) · ultrafiltrate of plasma · ~500 mL/day · total ~150 mL → turned over 3–4×/day · mechanical + immunological protection

Blood supply

Vessel Supply
Anterior spinal artery (single) Anterior 2/3 incl. lateral + ventral corticospinal tracts — predominant supply
Posterior spinal arteries (paired) Posterior 1/3 — dorsal columns
Artery of Adamkiewicz Largest anterior segmental (radicular) artery · from left posterior intercostal · lower 2/3 of cord via ASA

Adamkiewicz levelleft T8–L1 in 75% (Orthobullets) · left T9–L1 (OB, TO) · T9–T11 in 80% (TO, Dommisse) · range T5–L4, either side · "anywhere between T8 and T12" (TO true statement) — read the options

Adamkiewicz injuryparaplegia NOT common (TO EXCEPT) · thoracic watershed ischaemia with sacral sparing · ASA infarct → anterior cord syndrome (motor loss, preserved sensation/proprioception) · e.g. after wide left anterolateral corpectomy approach

Critical vascular zone (TO)T4–T9: narrowest canal + poorest supply → paraplegia risk

Dommisse principles (TO)3 longitudinal channels (1 anterior, 2 posterolateral) · largest at cervical + lumbar enlargements (grey matter demand) · 2–17 anterior and 6–25 posterior medullary feeders · vertebral arteries supply 80% of neck radicular arteries · reversible flow like circle of Willis

Nerve root anatomy

Region Exit
Cervical Above same-numbered pedicle (C5 above C5) · horizontal · extra C8 root
Thoracic Below same-numbered pedicle (T4 between T4-5)
Lumbar Below pedicle (L5 below L5) · descend vertically
Intrathecal lumbar Exiting roots ventral; motor fibres ventral to sensory

Why thoracic/lumbar roots lie below their vertebrae (TO)7 cervical vertebrae, 8 cervical nerves · 31 pairs total

Vertebral artery (TO)through transverse foramina C1–C6
✗ Wrong options: C7 (foramen present but artery usually outside)

Saddle anaesthesia, no other LL deficit (TO)lesion below S2 (S3: inner thighs, buttocks, perianal)

Neurogenic bladder (TO)

Lesion Bladder
Above pons (micturition centre in pons, locus coeruleus) Loss of voluntary inhibition, mild detrusor overactivity, no DSD, normal volume + pressure, no hypertrophy
Above S2 (suprasacral cord) Detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia, high pressures → functional outlet obstruction
Below S2 / pelvic nerves Detrusor areflexia, no DSD, high volume, low pressure

Neuroanatomy – brain & cranial nerves (TO)

Topic Fact
Trigeminal-associated ganglia Ciliary (V1), otic + pterygopalatine (V2 per TO), submandibular (V3) — all four · trigeminal ganglion is sensory
Brodmann 4 primary motor · 6 premotor/SMA · 3,1,2 somatosensory · 17 visual · 41,42 auditory · 44 Broca · 8 frontal eye field · Wernicke = 39 + 40
Internal acoustic meatus Transverse crest (horizontal) + Bill's bar (vertical, deeper) · "7 up, Coke down" — facial + nervus intermedius anterosuperior, cochlear anteroinferior (✗ Wrong options: superior), vestibular nerves posterior
Vernet (jugular foramen) IX, X, XI — taste posterior 1/3, palate/pharynx/larynx, trapezius + SCM · not tongue deviation (XII, hypoglossal canal)
Cavernous sinus Inside: VI (abducens) + ICA + sympathetic plexus · lateral wall: III, IV, V1, V2 · "OH TOM CAT"
Annulus of Zinn Through: III (sup + inf), VI, nasociliary, optic nerve + ophthalmic artery · outside: frontal, lacrimal, trochlear, superior ophthalmic vein
Facial nerve branches 1st = greater superficial petrosal (geniculate) → stapedius → chorda tympani → posterior auricular, digastric, stylohyoid → 5 terminal
Facial muscles Orbicularis oris/oculi, buccinator · not lateral pterygoid (V3)
Facial nerve blood External carotid branches
Olfaction Unmyelinated, cribriform plate, entorhinal cortex · only sense not relaying in thalamus (✗ Wrong options: VPL)
Left optic tract Right homonymous hemianopia (temporal right + nasal left)
Posterior pituitary Neuroectoderm (3rd ventricle floor) · anterior = Rathke's pouch
Asterion Lambdoid + occipitomastoid + parietomastoid sutures
Kocher's point 1 cm anterior to coronal suture, 2–3 cm from midline (mid-pupillary) — EVD into frontal horn
Vein of Labbé Sylvian fissure (superficial middle cerebral vein) → transverse sinus
Pineal infratentorial supracerebellar approach Sacrifice superior vermian + precentral cerebellar veins · not basal vein of Rosenthal

X vs Y

Cervical Thoracic / lumbar
Root vs pedicle Above Below
Reason 7 vertebrae, 8 nerves —
