"Cage placed, SSEP + MEP loss?"remove strut cage, monitor

"80% SSEP drop during correction, causes excluded?"reverse correction ± wake-up test

"DMD, diffuse MEP drop, MAP 55?"restore MAP > 90, retest

"PSO — most concerning change?"> 100 V MEP threshold increase

"Isolated right cortical SSEP loss during ACDF?"cerebral ischaemia from retraction

"Commonest non-anaesthetic reversible cause?"patient positioning

"Signal loss — steps EXCEPT?"lower BP to reduce blood loss (TO)

"SEP drop from hypothermia — fastest fix?"warm saline into the field (TO)

"L3 screw stimulates at 11 mA?"intact pedicle

"L4-5 surgery — which monitoring?"triggered EMG (TO)

Spinal Cord Tumors

Spine · 56 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Primary CNS tumoursmostly intracranial · only 2–4% spinal (850–1,700/yr US)

Compartmentsintradural extramedullary (60–70%) · intradural intramedullary (20–30% adults, 50% children) · extradural

3Presentation

Compartment Symptoms
Intradural extramedullary Cord compression, radicular pain, deep back pain worst at night, cauda equina-like
Intramedullary Local/radicular pain, motor (65%), sensory (60%), sphincter dysfunction (38%) — often first symptom

4Diagnosis & Classification

Intradural extramedullary

Schwannoma Meningioma
Frequency Commonest intradural tumour (with meningioma) 25% of primary spinal tumours
Demographics 40s–50s, M = F Women 80%, 5th–6th decades
Site Dorsal nerve root · cervical 31%, cauda equina 24%, thoracic 22% Thoracic
Risk NF2 NF2, prior radiation
MRI T1 iso, T2 hyperintense Dura-based, T1 iso/hypo, T2 slightly hyper, homogeneous enhancement
Histology Antoni A (hypercellular) + B (hypocellular), S-100+ Lobulated, meningothelial whorls
Treatment Resection ± RT if malignant Resection (curative if complete); RT for recurrence

Intramedullary

Ependymoma Astrocytoma
Frequency Commonest intramedullary (adults) (Orthobullets) Commonest in children (80–90%) · commonest overall (TO)
Age Adults Peak 3rd decade
Site Cervical commonest spinal site (TO) · myxopapillary in filum terminale Cervicothoracic junction (children)
Types Myxopapillary 40–50%, cellular 42% (benign) · anaplastic 30% benign
MRI T2 hyper, heterogeneous · myxopapillary: scalloping, foraminal enlargement, scoliosis, sacral seeding · cellular: polar cysts, haemorrhage Fusiform, irregular margins, infiltrative
Histology Rosettes + pseudorosettes Hypercellular, mitoses, eosinophilic granules
Treatment Gross total resection — good prognosis (clean plane) GTR difficult → RT ± chemo

TOpaediatric ependymoma intracranial (4th ventricle); adult spinal

Extradural & metastatic

Metastasisextramedullary = advancing disease · drop metastases via CSF · intramedullary rare (< 1%) from lung > breast, cervical + conus · intramedullary mets → life expectancy < 3 months · RT/chemo

GBM drop metastasis (TO)intradural extramedullary · 1–2% of GBM · CSF spread (debulking ↑ seeding) · lag ~8.5 months · steroids for rapid cord compression

Lymphomaimage whole neuraxis · T2 ill-defined hyperintense, marked homogeneous enhancement, little cord enlargement · cervical · methotrexate

5Management

Metastatic cord compression

FrameworksSINS (instability) + NOMS (neurologic, oncologic/radiosensitivity, mechanical, systemic) · Tokuhashi 9–11 → 6–12 months

Renal cell metastasis with anterior compression, myelopathypre-op embolisation → corpectomy + instrumented fusion → post-op RT
✗ Wrong options: posterior decompression alone · surgery without embolisation · pre-op RT

Unkeyed TO itemstructure spared and cut last during intramedullary tumour removal (denticulate ligament / midline myelotomy / functional root / DRG) — answer not provided by source

Brain tumour facts (TO)

Fact Answer
Commonest brain tumour Metastases (> 50%)
Commonest primary (adult CNS) GBM
Commonest primary non-glial Meningioma
Commonest paediatric CNS tumour Astrocytoma (~50%), then medulloblastoma
Commonest paediatric brain malignancy / vermian tumour Medulloblastoma
Brainstem glioma presentation Cranial neuropathies first (then weakness/ataxia; headache, vomiting late) · 77% < 20 yrs
Petroclival meningioma Feeders from ICA dural branches (meningohypophyseal trunk) · PVA particles not more complications · liquid agents (NBCA, Onyx) infrequent
Arachnoid cyst Commonest Sylvian fissure (middle fossa) · M:F 4:1, left · EMA+, CEA− · middle fossa cysts bleed (bridging veins) · suprasellar → hydrocephalus, precocious puberty, bobble-head doll · incidental adult → 1 repeat scan at 6–8 months

How it's asked

"Renal cell met with cord compression, 6–12 months expected?"embolisation, corpectomy + fusion, post-op RT

"Commonest site for spinal ependymoma?"cervical (TO)

"Commonest intramedullary tumour?"astrocytoma (TO — overall; ependymoma in adults)

"GBM drop metastases?"intradural extramedullary (TO)

"Petroclival meningioma — true?"feeders from internal carotid artery (TO)

"Brainstem glioma first presents with?"cranial neuropathies (TO)

"Commonest arachnoid cyst location?"Sylvian fissure (TO)

Spinal Epidural Abscess

Spine · 57 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Definitionpus / granulation tissue between dura and epidural fat

Patientsusually > 60 yrs · 2–10 per 10,000 admissions (TO) · location usually dorsal thoracolumbar · cervical SEA commoner in IVDU injecting arms/neck (TO)

Risk factorsIVDU, immunodeficiency, HIV, malignancy, immunosuppressants, recent spinal procedure, diabetes, endocarditis

Pathogenesis

Routeshaematogenous ~50% (commonest non-iatrogenic) · contiguous discitis ~33% · direct inoculation

Nidus (TO)skin commonest
✗ Wrong options: respiratory

OrganismsStaph aureus 50–65% · gram-negatives (E. coli 18%) · Pseudomonas in IVDU

Associationspondylodiscitis (SEA in ~18% of spondylodiscitis)

Neurologicdeficit in 33% · permanent paralysis 4–22% (compression or cord infarction) · risk highest cervical, lowest lumbar (TO)

3Presentation

Symptomssevere insidious pain (87%) · fever only ~50% · sicker than osteomyelitis · rapid neuro progression (days) (TO) · meningism, radicular pain · sequence: back ache → root pain → weakness → paralysis

Examradiculopathy or myelopathy (clonus, Hoffmann → look above)

Labs

Marker Fact
WBC Mean 22,000; raised in only ~42%
ESR Raised > 90%
CRP Raised 90%

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray Usually normal
CT Poor sensitivity
CT myelogram 90% sensitive, invasive
MRI + gadolinium Study of choice · ring-enhancing lesion = pathognomonic, T2 bright · pus vs CSF · extent, osteomyelitis, compression

Whole-spine MRI (skip lesions)

Indication
Concomitant infection outside the spine
Delayed presentation > 7 days (strongest predictor among listed options ; ✗ Wrong options: IVDU · diabetes · CRP · fever)
ESR > 95 mm/h
Neuro signs not explained by the lesion found (e.g. Hoffmann with lumbar SEA; clonus with L2-3 discitis)

5Management

Spinal epidural abscess
  • Neuro intact, small abscess, minimal compression, can be observed→ADMIT, IV ANTIBIOTICS ± BRACE
    (also non-surgical candidates)
  • Deficit / cord compression / failure risk / persistent infection / instability→SURGICAL DECOMPRESSION ± FUSION
    • Posterior abscess, no discitis→LAMINECTOMY (avoid wide facetectomy)
    • Anterior abscess / spondylodiscitis→ANTERIOR DEBRIDEMENT + STRUT ± POSTERIOR FIXATION

Failure of medical treatment

Predictor Note
Neurologic deficit Strongest predictor
Diabetes —
CRP > 115 mg/L —
WBC > 12–12.5 k —
Positive blood cultures —
Age > 65 —
MRSA —
No Smoking, IVDU

> 40% of medically managed patients fail (pain, deficit)

Neuro-intact 53-yr, MSSA bacteraemiaadmit, brace, IV cefazolin 6 wks · serial exams
✗ Wrong options: discharge on oral linezolid · laminectomy

Most likely to need surgery67-yr diabetic, leg weakness, MRSA bacteraemia (all risk factors)

Operative

Indicationsneuro deficit, cord compression on imaging, failure risk factors (> 65, diabetes, new deficit, MRSA), persistent infection, deformity/instability

Antibiotics after surgery2–4 wks if no bone involvement · 6 wks if bony involvement

Cervical anterior abscess + deficit (IVDU)anterior decompression + fusion
✗ Wrong options: posterior · steroids · antibiotics alone

Cervical spondylodiscitis C5-7 + kyphosis + deficit + sepsisanterior debridement, corpectomy, fusion + posterior instrumentation (instrument despite infection)

Lumbar 3-level destruction + abscess after endocarditisI&D, corpectomy, fusion

Antibiotic timinghold until intraoperative cultures if stable (OB 213125)

Prognosis

Best predictorpre-operative neuro deficit · mortality ~5% · early diagnosis key · only 18% with frank abscess / 23% with paralysis fully recover

Intracranial infections (TO)

Brain abscesscongenital cyanotic heart disease, endocarditis, lung, sinusitis (frontal/ethmoid → frontal lobe), otitis, trauma · overall Streptococcus; trauma S. aureus; transplant fungi; post-neurosurgery S. epidermidis/aureus · stages: early cerebritis 1–3 d → late cerebritis 4–9 d → early capsule 10–13 d → late capsule > 14 d · IV antibiotics 6–8 wks then oral · medical alone if < 3 cm, cerebritis stage, < 2 wks symptoms

NeurocysticercosisTaenia solium larvae (pork / contaminated vegetables) · commonest CNS parasite, commonest acquired epilepsy in low-income countries · C. cellulosae: 5–15 mm transparent cyst with scolex · stages: vesicular → colloidal → granular nodular → calcific · seizures commonest · EITB serology ~100% specific · MRI choice (scolex pathognomonic) · treatment: albendazole + steroids (start before) + antiepileptics ± surgery/shunt

Pneumorrachis (TO)

Definitionair in the spinal canal (traumatic, iatrogenic, spontaneous) · mostly cervical · from pneumothorax/pneumomediastinum tracking via retropharynx

Gas from necrotising fasciitis / emphysematous osteomyelitishypointense epidural lesion · CT best (can't separate air from gas) · usually resolves conservatively

How it's asked

"53-yr neuro-intact SEA, MSSA?"admission, bracing, IV cefazolin 6 wks

"Most likely to need surgery?"67-yr diabetic, leg weakness, MRSA bacteraemia

"Strongest predictor of failure in IVDU with weakness?"lower extremity weakness

"Lumbar SEA + positive Hoffmann — next?"MRI cervical + thoracic spine

"IVDU, diffuse weakness, anterior cervical abscess?"anterior decompression + fusion

"Post-endocarditis, 3-level lumbar destruction, weakness?"I&D, corpectomy, fusion

"C5-7 spondylodiscitis, kyphosis, deficit, sepsis?"anterior debridement, corpectomy, fusion + posterior instrumentation

"Cervical anterior abscess, 1 month fevers, weakness?"anterior decompression + fusion

"SEA — EXCEPT?"commonest nidus is respiratory infection (TO — it's skin)

"Post-endocarditis back pain, clonus — imaging?"thoracic ring-enhancing epidural collection (Figure B)

"L2-3 discitis, neuro signs above — next?"MRI of the entire spine

"Factor predicting skip lesions?"duration of symptoms (≥ 7 days)

"Large fronto-parietal lesion + congenital heart disease?"brain abscess (TO)

"Multiple white transparent cysts?"neurocysticercosis (TO)

"Necrotising fasciitis, hypointense epidural lesion?"pneumorrachis (TO)

Spinal Tuberculosis

Spine · 58 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Pott's diseaseM. tuberculosis spondylitis · ↑ with immunocompromise (HIV, CD4 50–200)

Frequency15% of TB extrapulmonary · spine = commonest extrapulmonary site (thoracic) · 5% of all TB

Level (TO)lower thoracic > lumbar > upper thoracic > cervical > sacral · children: anterior lower thoracic (OB)

Pathoanatomy

Seedinghaematogenous to end-arterioles in the anterior vertebral body next to the endplate (metaphysis) · commonest pattern: anterior (TO key) · spreads under the ALL → multilevel, skip lesions (15%), paraspinal abscess (50%) — large, anterior (≫ pyogenic)

Discspared early (vs pyogenic disc-first) → may mimic tumour

Aneurysmal phenomenon (TO)long-standing abscess → concave anterior scalloping of bodies · thoracic abscess = bird-nest shadow

End result in spine (TO)bony ankylosis (weight-bearing + cancellous ends) vs fibrous in limb joints

Kyphosis

Adultsstatic after healing · mean 15° non-op; > 60° in 5%

Childrenprogresses in 40% after healing (growth) · anterior + posterior element involvement = best predictor of progression
✗ Wrong options: age · level count · bracing timing; thoracic-only does better than thoracolumbar

Rajasekaran progression Course
Type I ↑ until growth stops → surgery
Type II Decreasing progression with growth
Type III Minimal change

3Presentation

Onsetinsidious · malaise, night sweats, weight loss · back pain often late

Earliest (TO)symptom back pain · sign paraspinal muscle spasm · first neuro sign ankle clonus (motor before sensory — anterior disease near motor tracts), then extensor plantar

Neuro deficit10–47% · abscess, granulation, debris, caseation, instability, ossified flavum near severe kyphosis · upper thoracic commonest (narrow canal) · Pott's paraplegia 10–30% (TO)

Pott's paraplegia (TO)

Early onset Late onset
Timing Active disease, < 2 yrs > 2 yrs, healed disease
Cause Oedema, granulation, abscess, caseation, ischaemia Deformity (internal gibbus), debris, sequestra, stenosis
Prognosis Better Worse
Treatment ATT ± decompression Deformity correction + stabilisation

Commonest cause of late-onset paraplegiadeformity (TO)

Prognosis by cause (Orthobullets)abscess / bony sequestra better than meningomyelitis

Modified Tuli stage Findings
1 Subtle — clonus, brisk reflexes, Babinski
2 UMN deficit + spasticity, ambulatory
3 Bedridden, spastic, lateral column sensory loss
4 Bedridden, severe sensory loss (posterior + lateral), sores
5 Stage 4 + bladder/bowel, flexor spasms, flaccidity

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
CXR Abnormal in 66% — always obtain
X-ray early Anterior body involvement, disc preserved
X-ray late Collapse, disc destruction, kyphosis
MRI + gadolinium Preferred · T1 low, T2 high · septated smooth-walled para/prevertebral abscess with subligamentous spread · multilevel 16–70% · endplate disruption (sens 100%, spec 81%), paravertebral shadow (97/85) · cord oedema, myelomalacia, syrinx
CT Lesions < 1.5 cm; fragmentary, osteolytic, subperiosteal, sclerotic destruction · poor for epidural extent
Tc + gallium scan Highest sensitivity for infection

Disc-confined lumbar abscesspyogenic, not TB (TO)

Spine-at-risk signs (Rajasekaran)

✓ Sign
Facet subluxation / separation at apex
Retropulsion (posterior displacement)
Lateral translation
Toppling
✗ Wrong options: kyphosis angle (> 50°, > 55°) · abscess · neuro worsening

Labs & tissue

Test Fact
CBC Relative lymphocytosis, low Hb
ESR Usually ↑ (normal in 25%)
PPD + in ~80%
CT-guided biopsy Most specific test (TO) · AFB stain (smear + 52%), culture + 83% (up to 10 wks), PCR 95% sensitive
No Empirical antibiotics without biopsy (TO)

DifferentialActinomyces, Nocardia, Brucella, fungi (Coccidioides, Blastomyces, Cryptococcus, Aspergillus), syphilis

Pyogenic vs TB (TO)

Treat as pyogenic Treat as TB
Acute, septic, severe pain Subacute / chronic
Lumbar, disc-predominant, no big abscess Skip / multiple lesions
Older, immunocompromised Paraspinal abscess
— Thoracic / thoracolumbar

5Management

Medical

RegimenHRZE 2 months → HR 9–18 months (Orthobullets) · HRZE 2 months → HRE ≥ 10 months, total 12–16 months, daily (TO, India) · with surgery: continue H, R, Z

HIV + cervical Pott's with Ziehl-Neelsen +isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, streptomycin

Orthosispain + deformity prevention · steroids only for arachnoiditis/meningitis (TO)

Operative criteria

Indication Detail
Neuro deficit Worsening deficit, acute severe paraplegia, not responding to ≥ 3 wks ATT (TO)
Instability Panvertebral disease ± subluxation · Ganga score > 3, ≥ 2 spine-at-risk signs (TO)
Kyphosis > 60° adults (Orthobullets) · thoracic > 40°, lumbar > 30° (TO) · progressive in a child · ≥ 3 vertebrae with loss of ≥ 1.5 bodies (thoracic) · child ≤ 7 with ≥ 3 bodies + ≥ 2 at-risk signs
Late-onset paraplegia Kyphosis correction
Others Caseation blocking drugs, failure after 3–6 months, uncertain diagnosis, > 4 levels (TO), large abscess

Benefitsless kyphosis, faster healing, fewer sinuses, better neuro recovery · implant colonisation rarer than pyogenic · autograft or allograft struts fine

Approach

Situation Approach
Classic Anterior decompression / corpectomy + strut ± posterior instrumentation ± posterior shortening (single-stage transpedicular, two-stage, extrapleural anterolateral)
TO modern Posterior / transpedicular preferred (lung already involved; diseased body poor for screws) · posterior abscess (e.g. D6-7) → posterior laminectomy · lumbar: anterior abscess drainable via laminectomy (dura retractable)
TO progressive deficit, T7-9, no paravertebral collection Posterior decompression + stabilisation
TO worsening paraplegia + bladder after 3 wks ATT Decompression + evaluate for MDR-TB (✗ Wrong options: add streptomycin · steroids · repeat MRI)
Cervical kyphosis Halo traction → anterior decompression, graft, plate
Lumbar kyphosis Pedicle subtraction osteotomy
Healed thoracic/TL kyphosis Internal kyphectomy (cord moved anteriorly)
Transthoracic Left side (aorta easier than IVC)
Atypical TB (granuloma) Laminectomy (extradural/subdural) · myelotomy (intramedullary)

Rajasekaran (TO)active disease: posterior stabilisation then anterior debridement + graft · healed rigid deformity: anterior first then posterior

Drug-resistant TB (TO)

Rif-resistant on Xpertnext second-line LPA / DST for fluoroquinolone resistance (MDR vs pre-XDR) · XDR work-up: SL-LPA + gene sequencing (Bdq/Lzd)

WHO 2022 group Drugs
A (all three) Levofloxacin/moxifloxacin, bedaquiline, linezolid
B Clofazimine, cycloserine/terizidone
C Ethambutol, delamanid, pyrazinamide, amikacin/streptomycin, ethionamide

Regimensshorter all-oral 9–12 months (FQ-sensitive) · longer 18–20 months · BPaLM 6 months · injectables last resort · never add a single drug to a failing regimen

XDR (2021)MDR + FQ resistance + bedaquiline or linezolid resistance

Monitoringbedaquiline → QTc (ECG) · linezolid → myelosuppression (CBC)

6Complications

Deformityhighest after anterior graft alone (graft slips/breaks, ≥ 2 levels) · lowest with anterior + posterior fusion

Othersretropharyngeal abscess (dysphagia, hoarseness), TB arteritis/pseudoaneurysm, costopelvic impingement, sinus formation, Pott's paraplegia

Atypical spinal TBcompressive myelopathy without visible deformity · intraspinal granuloma, neural arch disease, concentric collapse, sclerotic bridging

Spina ventosa (TO)tubercular dactylitis: phalanges of the hand, destruction + new bone, spindle-shaped finger swelling

How it's asked

"Aneurysmal phenomenon in dorsal Pott's?"long-standing abscess (TO)

"MRI NOT suggestive of TB?"abscess confined to lumbar disc space (TO)

"Commonest TB spine pattern?"anterior (TO)

"End result of TB spine?"bony ankylosis (TO)

"9-yr gibbus — true?"vertebral body involved, disc spared

"Commonest site in children?"anterior lower thoracic

"Early finding in Pott's?"preserved disc space

"Late-onset paraplegia cause?"deformity (TO)

"T7-9 lesion, progressive deficit, no collection?"posterior decompression + stabilisation (TO)

"Worsening paraplegia + bladder after 3 wks ATT?"decompression + evaluate for MDR TB (TO)

"D6-7 posterior abscess failing ATT — approach?"posterior laminectomy (TO)

"HIV, cervical Pott's, ZN positive — drugs?"INH, rifampin, PZA, streptomycin

"Predicts kyphosis progression after healing in child?"anterior + posterior element involvement

"Xpert: rifampicin resistance — next?"second-line LPA / DST for fluoroquinolones (TO)

"Group A drugs?"bedaquiline, linezolid, fluoroquinolone (TO)

"Monitoring on bedaquiline + linezolid?"ECG QTc + weekly CBC (TO)

"Tool for FQ + Group A resistance?"SL-LPA + targeted gene sequencing (TO)

"Spine-at-risk EXCEPT?"kyphosis > 55° / > 50° (TO)

"Spine-at-risk sign?"facet subluxation (TO)

"First neurological sign?"ankle clonus (TO)

"Earliest sign of Pott's?"paraspinal muscle spasm (TO)

"Most specific test for spinal infection?"biopsy (TO)

"Spina ventosa?"tubercular dactylitis (TO)

Spine Biomechanics

Spine · 59 / 69

1Basics

Functional spinal unit

FSUcephalad + caudad vertebrae, disc, facet joints · physiologic motion + neural protection · intradiscal pressure varies with position

Stabilityno abnormal strain or excess motion under physiologic load · maintained by FSU, muscle tension, abdominal/thoracic pressure, rib cage

Denis three columns

Column Structures
Anterior ALL, anterior 2/3 of body + annulus
Middle PLL, posterior 1/3 of body + annulus
Posterior Pedicles, facets, ligamentum flavum, spinous process, PLC

Instabilitymiddle column injury (widened interpedicular distance, loss of posterior body height) · PLC disruption + anterior and/or middle column injury · model only moderately reliable

Ligaments

Ten ligamentsrestrict FSU motion, absorb energy · all type I collagen except ligamentum flavum (mostly elastin) · viscoelastic, non-linear

Posterior ligamentous complex

Componentssupraspinous, interspinous, ligamentum flavum, facet capsules
✗ Wrong options: facet joints · PLL

Ruptured ifbony Chance fracture, interspinous widening, progressive kyphosis on non-op care, facet diastasis · ambiguous: MRI signal between spinous processes

TLICSPLC intact 0 / indeterminate / ruptured 3 · burst without deficit + intact PLC = 2 → non-op (< 4) · + ruptured PLC = 5 → surgery (> 4)

4Diagnosis & Classification

Sagittal balance

Segment Normal
Cervical lordosis 20–40°
Thoracic kyphosis Average 35° (20–50°)
Lumbar lordosis Average 60° (20–80°) · 75% at L4–S1, 47% at L5-S1

Gravity linecentre of C2 → anterior T7 → mid T12-L1 disc → posterior to L3 → posterosuperior S1 · radiograph: C7 plumb line to posterosuperior S1

Negative balanceaxis behind sacrum (lumbar hyperlordosis) · positive — axis in front (hip flexion contracture, flat back)

Best radiographic predictor of good outcome after adult deformity surgerysagittal balance correction
✗ Wrong options: coronal · rotation · listhesis · PI

Pelvic incidencefixed angle; correlates with lumbar lordosis, thoracic kyphosis, sagittal alignment (PT + SS change with pelvic rotation)

Facet orientation & motion

Region Coronal / sagittal angle Motion
Cervical (C3-7) 0° coronal, 45° sagittal Flex-ext, lateral bend, rotation
Thoracic 20° coronal, 55° sagittal Rotation; little flex-ext (ribs) · protects heart/lungs
Lumbar 50° coronal, 90° sagittal Flex-ext, minimal rotation

Instantaneous axis of rotation

Definitiontheoretical axis about which an FSU rotates at an instant · not fixed; moves with bending/rotation · 3 axes, 6 degrees of freedom

Pathologydegeneration, fracture, ligament injury shift the IAR outside the FSU (enlarged)
✗ Wrong options: fixed · 12 DOF · from AP/lateral films

Not IARCORA (deformity correction) · vertebral incidence (L5) · plumb line (C7)

Pedicle anatomy

Region Fact
C2, C7 Pedicle screws viable
C3–C6 Small pedicles → lateral mass screws
Thoracic wall Medial wall 2× thicker than lateral
T4 Narrowest diameter + shortest (14 mm) (T1 20 mm, T10 20 mm)
T7 Narrow on concave side in AIS
T12 vs L1 T12 larger than L1
Transverse angle 10° mid-thoracic → 30° L5
Sagittal angle 15–17° cephalad thoracic · neutral lumbar · caudal at L5
Lumbar L1 smallest lumbar diameter · medial angulation L1 12°, L5 30°, S1 39° · S1 ~19 mm
Landmarks Transverse process midpoint (cranio-caudal) · lateral pars border (medio-lateral)

5Management

Instrumentation (TO)

System Approach
Harrington rods Posterior
Luque rods (sublaminar wires) Posterior
Steffee plate + pedicle screws Posterior
Dwyer, Zielke Anterior (scoliosis)
Pedicle screw Posterior, spans all three columns (Boucher 1959)

Sacropelvic fixation (TO)

Direct iliac screw S2 alar-iliac (S2AI)
Entry Posterior iliac crest near PSIS → ASIS Between S1 and S2 foramina (Kebaish 2007)
Prominence Prominent → pain, wounds, removal 15 mm deeper → seldom removed
Alignment Needs connector In line with lumbar screws
Muscle Iliac devascularisation Less
SI joint Less violation More SI joint violation (main drawback)

How it's asked

"PLC components?"supraspinous, interspinous, flavum + facet capsules

"Theoretical axis of FSU rotation at an instant?"instantaneous axis of rotation

"IAR — true?"altered by degeneration, fracture, ligament injury

"Best predictor of good outcome after adult deformity surgery?"sagittal balance correction

"Angle correlating with lordosis, kyphosis, sagittal alignment?"pelvic incidence (A)

"Not a posterior instrumentation?"Dwyer-Zielke (TO)

"S2AI vs iliac screws — incorrect?""less SI joint violation" (it's more) (TO)

Spine Surgical Site Infections

Spine · 60 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

SSIcommonest early post-op hospital-acquired infection · cost ~$200,000/patient · ↑ morbidity, reoperation, stay, mortality

Procedure Rate
Overall spine 0.7–16% (orthopaedic spine 2.0%)
Microdiscectomy 0.7% with prophylaxis · 1.4% with microscope
Elective instrumented fusion 2.8–6%
Trauma Up to 10% (multilevel, delay > 160 h, complete deficit, cognitive impairment)
Posterior vs anterior Posterior higher · combined A/P not higher than posterior alone

Average time to infection14 months

Risk factors

Category Factors
Medical > 70 yrs, ASA, diabetes, CVD, cancer, steroids, prior lumbar surgery, COPD, immunocompromise, prior infection, pre-op stay > 1 wk, prior radiation
Lifestyle Obesity, smoking, alcohol, malnutrition (15×)
Intra-op Transfusion, instrumentation, staged surgery, levels fused, OR traffic, > 3 h, blood loss > 1 L

Pathogenesis

Implantsseroma + inflammation · glycocalyx biofilm shields bacteria from host + antibiotics · metallosis granulomas

Organism Context
S. aureus (73%) Commonest · MRSA 5–18%
S. epidermidis Increasing
E. coli, Enterococcus Incontinence / faecal soiling
Cutibacterium acnes Low virulence, late hardware infection, immunocompromised
Gram-negative rods Trauma (ISS > 18), neuromuscular scoliosis
Polymicrobial Post-op wound contamination (neuromuscular)

3Presentation

Commonest presentationwound drainage · pain increasing instead of settling · night sweats

Fevercommonest general sign · > 39 °C worrying for deep infection

Sepsis / organ failureemergent debridement

Woundsuperficial: erythema, discharge · deep may look clean · clear persistent drainage = seroma; purulent = infection

4Diagnosis & Classification

Classifications

Anatomic
Superficial Skin + subcutaneous, fascia intact
Deep Fascia/muscle — won't settle with antibiotics alone
Timing
Early < 3 wks
Late > 4 wks
Latent Years
Mechanism
Direct inoculation > 10⁵ organisms; within 30 days
Early outside-in Drains, seroma, soiling
Late haematogenous Dental work, UTI, bacteraemia
Thalgott Host Infection
A / B / C Normal / local-systemic disease (incl. smoking) / immunocompromised —
1 — Single organism
2 — Deep, polymicrobial (~3 debridements)
3 — Deep, polymicrobial + myonecrosis (poor)

Labs

Marker Behaviour
CRP Peaks day 2–3, normal by 5–21 days (~2 wks) · most sensitive marker · persistent rise or second peak = infection
ESR Peaks day 4–11, elevated up to 6 wks · rising after day 4 suggests infection
WBC Unreliable
Malnutrition Albumin < 3.5 g/dL, transferrin < 150
HbA1c Pre-op diabetics < 7.0

ResolutionCRP normalised + ESR improving

TOpost-discectomy severe pain + spasm, no fever, normal TLC/ESR, high CRP → infective discitis

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray Early normal · late: disc-height loss, endplate erosion, lucency around hardware
CT Fusion status, implant position, peri-screw lysis, pseudarthrosis
MRI + gadolinium Most useful · rim-enhancing large collection = pathognomonic · epidural collection, bony destruction, progressive marrow change · post-op change mimics infection
WBC-labelled / bone scan No MRI (pacemaker)

Culturesintra-operative tissue = gold standard (ideally before antibiotics) · superficial swabs unreliable · culture removed hardware in latent cases

Differentialadjacent segment disease, inadequate decompression, seroma, haematoma

Failed back surgery syndrome (TO)

Timing Commonest cause
Immediate / early Incorrect diagnosis
Few weeks (e.g. 3 wks) Spinal infection
Months Recurrent herniation, epidural fibrosis, arachnoiditis
Years Degeneration, instability, stenosis, adjacent segment disease

Overall commonestpoor patient selection · patient factors: workers' comp, smoking, obesity, stress

5Management

Spine SSI
  • Mild superficial→ORAL ANTIBIOTICS + WOUND CARE
    (Keflex MSSA · Bactrim MRSA · clindamycin PCN allergy · Augmentin anaerobes)
  • Deep / most cases → URGENT I&D + CULTURES→TARGETED IV ANTIBIOTICS 6 WKS
    • Loose hardware / refractory / latent with solid fusion→REMOVE HARDWARE
    • Unstable / not fused→RETAIN (± upsize, extend) — titanium preferred

Surgery indicationsmost cases, antibiotic failure, deformity, deficit, MRI progression

Debridementold incision, layered, pulse lavage, remove necrotic muscle + loose graft + loose hardware · multiple debridements · NPWT, flaps, SPY perfusion, non-braided tension-free closure, beads, drains

Antibiotics6 wks IV (PICC), follow ESR/CRP · serial MRI not needed · prolonged IV antibiotics may allow fusion before hardware removal

6Complications

Listwound breakdown, pseudarthrosis, neuro deficit, sepsis, organ failure, death, worse outcomes

Prevention

Phase Measures
Pre-op Weight loss, stop smoking, alcohol abstinence, glucose control, treat UTI, povidone-iodine nasal swab for MRSA carriers
Prep Clippers (✗ Wrong options: razor), alcohol prep
Antibiotics Within 1 h of incision, redose if > 4 h · cefazolin 2 g (3 g if > 100 kg / BMI > 35) · PCN allergy/MRSA risk: vancomycin (start > 1 h before) or clindamycin · stop at 24 h
Intra-op ↓ time, blood loss, invasiveness · ↓ traffic · dilute betadine 0.3% × 2 min · release retractors every 2 h · debride necrotic muscle · haemostasis
Vancomycin powder ↓ SSI · if infection occurs → ↑ gram-negative + culture-negative collections (✗ Wrong options: VRE · nonunion · ↑ cost)
Drains Evacuate haematoma; NASS: not routine after single-level surgery

How it's asked

"Post-discectomy pain + spasm, only CRP high?"infective discitis (TO)

"Most sensitive marker of post-op inflammation?"CRP

"Resolution of wound infection?"CRP normalised, ESR improving

"Topical vancomycin powder leads to?"more gram-negative SSI if infection occurs

"Commonest cause of FBSS at 3 weeks?"spinal infection (TO)

Subaxial Cervical Vertebral Body Fractures

Spine · 61 / 69

1Basics

Fracture types

Type Features Stability / treatment
Compression Anterior body fails, posterior cortex intact, no retropulsion · often PLC injury Mild + intact PLC → collar 6–12 wks
Burst Through posterior cortex, retropulsion · ± PLC injury · complete/incomplete SCI Unstable → surgery
Flexion teardrop Anterior column fails in flexion-compression, posterior fragment retropulsed / retrolisthesis, posterior column fails in tension · large anterior lip = quadrangular fracture Unstable → surgery · SCI
Extension teardrop avulsion Small fleck off anterior endplate (ALL/annulus), usually C2, no malalignment Stable → collar, no SCI

Flexion teardrop (TO)

Featurestriangle off anteroinferior body · commonest C5 · posterior displacement of the upper column · frequently with sagittal body + laminar fractures · anterior cord syndrome characteristic
✗ Wrong options: "unusual"

Quadrangular fractureflexion-compression · anterior third fragment, posterior subluxation, kyphosis, interspinous widening, facet subluxation

Mechanism

Mechanism Usual injury
Hyperextension Central cord
Flexion-distraction Facet dislocation
Rotation Soft tissue / high cervical
Pure axial load Burst
Rear-end collision Extension–distraction (TO)

Commonest subaxial injury pattern (TO)distractive flexion, commonest C6–C7 · stages: 1 PLC failure → 2 unilateral facet dislocation → 3 bilateral → 4 floating vertebra

4Diagnosis & Classification

Classifications

Allen-Ferguson (research, mechanism)
Flexion-compression · vertical compression · flexion-distraction · extension-compression · extension-distraction · lateral flexion

Clinical descriptivecompression, burst, flexion-distraction, facet dislocation (uni/bilateral), facet fracture

SLIC (TO example)

Component Example score
Morphology (translation/rotation) 4
Discoligamentous complex disrupted 2
Neurology (incomplete cord 3 + continuous compression 1) 4
Total 10 → surgery

Key radiology findings

MRIPLC status · TO: before reduction of fracture-dislocation · MRI within 48 h most sensitive for oedema/haemorrhage · obtain if CT shows prevertebral soft tissue > 5 mm at C3, disc-space widening > 1–2 mm, interspinous widening

5Management

Operative criteria

Procedure Indication
Collar 6–12 wks Mild compression (intact PLC, no kyphosis), extension teardrop avulsion
Halo Stable pattern
Anterior corpectomy + strut + plate Compression with ≥ 11° angulation or 25% height loss · unstable burst/teardrop with cord compression · minimal posterior injury
Posterior decompression + fusion Significant posterior element injury, no anterior decompression needed

Timingearly decompression < 24 h improves neuro outcome · delay → pulmonary complications, ulcers, DVT
✗ Wrong options: steroids · delay to avoid "second hit" · collar

TO conflictawake, alert C4-5 jumped facets within 30 min → hard collar + MRI first (TO) · ASIA C C4-5 fracture-dislocation with MRI done, SLIC 10 → immediate reduction + stabilisation (TO)

Anterior approach (TO)

Planecarotid sheath (lateral) vs trachea + oesophagus (medial) · no internervous plane

Sideleft preferred: right RLN loops under subclavian, less protected · left RLN under aortic arch (ligamentum arteriosum) in tracheo-oesophageal groove

Commonest complicationvocal cord palsy (RLN) → hoarseness
✗ Wrong options: superior laryngeal

C5 palsy (TO)

Definitiondeltoid ± biceps weakness ≥ 1 grade after decompression without myelopathy worsening · mostly posterior surgery, also anterior (similar incidence)

Featureshalf with C5 pain/sensory change · 92% unilateral · within 1 wk · good prognosis · theories: segmental cord ischaemia-reperfusion vs root tethering (favoured)

Prevention / treatmentprophylactic C5 foraminotomy, limit posterior cord shift + excess lordosis · physiotherapy, steroids

Cervical disc replacement (TO)

Prospreserves motion + IAR, avoids pseudarthrosis + adjacent segment disease · equal neuro outcomes, fewer reoperations than ACDF

Idealmature patient, 1–2 level radiculopathy ± myelopathy C3–C7 from soft disc / foraminal stenosis

Avoiddisc height < 3 mm, major spondylosis, axial neck pain alone, facet arthrosis, ≥ 3 levels, instability (> 3 mm translation, > 11° rotation), osteoporosis, obesity, kyphosis

How it's asked

"Flexion teardrop — EXCEPT?"sagittal body + laminar fractures are unusual (TO)

"Quadrangular fracture mechanism?"flexion-compression

"Large anteroinferior fragment + retrolisthesis?"unstable flexion compression injury

"Awake C4-5 jumped facets at 30 min?"hard collar + MRI (TO)

"ASIA C C4-5 fracture-dislocation, MRI done?"immediate surgery: reduction + stabilisation (TO)

"Diving C6 burst, ASIA C — best recovery?"urgent decompression < 24 h

"Bilateral deltoid weakness day 2 after C5-6 surgery?"C5 palsy (TO)

"Anterior cervical approach — side?"left (right RLN more at risk) (TO)

"Hoarseness + unilateral cord palsy after ACDF?"recurrent laryngeal nerve palsy (TO)

"Anterior cervical approach plane?"carotid sheath laterally, trachea/oesophagus medially (TO)

"Rear-end crash, quadriparesis?"extension–distraction (TO)

"Commonest subaxial injury type?"distractive flexion (TO)

"Image after ACD with articulating device?"disc replacement (TO)

Synovial Facet Cyst

Spine · 62 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidencerare · lumbar

LevelL4-5 60–89% (most mobile segment) · L3-4 ~14% · L5-S1 ~12% · cervical rare: C5-6, C7-T1
✗ Wrong options: "L5-S1 commonest (sagittal facets", TO)

Pathophysiology

Causefacet microinstability → synovium extrudes through capsule · myxoid degeneration, fibroblast proliferation, ↑ hyaluronic acid · trauma controversial

Associations (TO)facet OA (40.5%), degenerative spondylolisthesis (43.4%), disc degeneration (13.2%)

Contentsganglion, flavum cysts; serous, mucoid, haemorrhagic

Histology

Cyst Lining
Synovial cyst Synovial (cuboidal) cells over vascular granulation stroma · clear/xanthochromic fluid
Ganglion No synovial lining, gelatinous myxoid content
✗ Traps Concentric fibrocollagen lamellae = annulus · gelatinous matrix with oval chondrocytes = nucleus · nidus + halo = osteoid osteoma · storiform atypia = UPS

3Presentation

Symptomsmechanical back pain, radicular leg pain (lateral recess → traversing root, e.g. L5: EHL + hip abductor weakness), neurogenic claudication · sudden deterioration = cyst haemorrhage · CES rare

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Finding
X-ray AP, lateral, flexion-extension Usually normal · segmental instability
MRI Axial + sagittal T2 · T2 hyperintense centre, hypointense rim · T1 hypointense core · rim enhancement · contiguous with degenerative facet, narrowing lateral recess
Positional MRI Supine 89% → standing 97% sensitivity (cysts enlarge standing)
T2 signal → aspiration High T2 (synovial fluid) → better CT rupture success · low T2 (gelatinous/calcified) → worse

Differentialligamentum flavum, Tarlov cyst, pseudomeningocele, HNP

5Management

Symptomatic facet cyst
  • 1st line (6–8 wks)→NSAIDs, PT, rest, ESI
  • 2nd line→CT-GUIDED CYST RUPTURE + facet steroid (50–75% relief at 1 yr; ~39–50% need surgery)
  • Surgery
    • Unilateral, stable→LAMINOTOMY / LAMINECTOMY + CYST EXCISION
    • Instability / spondylolisthesis, bilateral, central stenosis, recurrence→FACETECTOMY + INSTRUMENTED FUSION

Operative criteria

Procedure Indication / outcome
Decompression + cyst excision Persistent symptoms, unilateral · TO: surgery of choice without instability · 80–90% success · risk: high recurrent back pain + cyst within 2 yrs, iatrogenic listhesis
Facetectomy + instrumented fusion Recurrence after laminectomy, bilateral symptoms, central stenosis, instability / degenerative spondylolisthesis · some: first-line surgery · lowest recurrent back pain + cyst recurrence · 80–90% resolution
✗ Wrong option: ALIF Doesn't remove cyst or decompress lateral recess
✗ Wrong option: uninstrumented fusion + facetectomy ↑ pseudarthrosis
✗ Wrong option: aspiration High failure, short-term only

Techniqueunilateral laminotomy + medial facetectomy (burr) · develop dura–cyst plane, Allis traction, Epstein curette/Woodson · pedicle screw at superior TP border × mid superior articular process

6Complications

Recurrencehigh with excision alone → fusion

Iatrogenic spondylolisthesislaminectomy without fusion → instrumented fusion

Dural tearrevision, cyst adherent to dura (50–55%) → partial excision (88% success, ↓ durotomy)

How it's asked

"Facet cyst — wrong statement?"commonest at L5-S1 (it's L4-5) (TO)

"Hip abductor + EHL weakness, MRI lesion off facet?"facet synovial cyst

"Histology of this lesion?"synovial cells over vascular granulation stroma

"Arrow on T2 at a degenerative facet, bright cyst in lateral recess?"lumbar synovial cyst

"Recurrent synovial cyst after laminectomy?"revision laminectomy + instrumented fusion

"Lowest recurrent back pain at 2 yrs?"decompression + facetectomy + instrumented fusion

"Unstable L4-5 listhesis + cyst, EHL weakness — definitive?"laminectomy + cyst excision + instrumented fusion

"Grade 1 listhesis + cyst — best long-term?"L4-5 posterior instrumented fusion, laminectomy, cyst excision

Syrinx & Syringomyelia

Spine · 63 / 69

1Basics

Definitions

Syringomyeliafluid cavity in the cord that expands → deficits · syringobulbia — in the brainstem

Epidemiology8.4 per 100,000 · M > F · 3rd–4th decades; insidious from adolescence to 45

Causes (partial CSF obstruction)

Cause Fact
Chiari malformation > 50% · tonsils act as pistons at foramen magnum → pressure wave each heartbeat · syrinx in 69% of adult, 40% of child Chiari (TO)
Spinal cord trauma 4–10% of SCI, may appear years later
Cord tumour 30% develop syrinx (TO: tumour cysts not true syringomyelia)
Post-infectious Arachnoiditis (meningitis, subarachnoid blood)

Primary spinal syringomyelia (TO)pathology entirely within the spine (trauma, arachnoid cysts/webs, meningitis scarring) (✗ Wrong options: "secondary")

Chiari syrinx mechanism (TO)Oldfield: pulsatile tonsils on a closed spinal subarachnoid space drive CSF into the cord (+ arterial pulsations, Stoodley) · (✗ Wrong options: "pressure differential from partial block" (TO EXCEPT key)) · old "communicating" 4th-ventricle theory abandoned · cause: small posterior fossa relative to cerebellum

Associations

Condition Fact
Scoliosis 25–80% of syringomyelia · neural axis anomaly in 1.8–25% of "idiopathic" curves
Klippel-Feil —
Charcot joint < 5%, usually shoulder

3Presentation

Symptomsoccipital headache worse with Valsalva (cough, sneeze — tussive, dural stretch), back + radicular pain · central cord syndrome picture

Dissociated sensory losspain + temperature lost in a cape over neck/shoulders · light touch, vibration, position preserved

Reflexesasymmetric abdominal reflexes, asymmetric/hyperactive reflexes, asymmetric sweating, spasticity

Motor / deformityasymmetric bulk, weakness, LE contractures, scoliosis

SyringobulbiaCN XII tongue atrophy, XI SCM/trapezius, IX–X dysphagia/dysarthria, VII facial palsy

Chiari brainstem signs (TO)nystagmus, hoarseness, swallowing/speech problems, sleep apnoea, imbalance

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray Lack of thoracic apical lordosis suggests syrinx · scoliosis series
MRI Diagnostic · gadolinium to exclude tumour
CT myelogram Post-traumatic syrinx / arachnoid webs — shows obstruction (TO)

Diagnostic criteria – MRI in scoliosis

Indication
Atypical curve — left thoracic apex, double curve
Neurologic deficit (e.g. ↓ flank/hand pinprick, absent abdominal reflex)
Infantile or juvenile onset
Male with atypical or large curve
Thoracic kyphosis > 30°
Valsalva headache

Histologygrey matter cavitation, near/continuous with central canal, gliotic lining

Differentialhydromyelia, glioependymal cyst, myelomalacia, cystic tumour, persistent central canal

Neuropathic shoulder

Cluesevere destruction both sides of joint, little pain, minor trauma, chronic dislocation · MRI brain + cervical spine → Chiari / syrinx
✗ Wrong options: RF, HLA-B27, synovial fluid, bone scan

5Management

Syrinx ± Chiari
  • Asymptomatic, not enlarging→OBSERVE (± brace during work-up)
  • CN deficit, weakness, cerebellar signs, sensory deficit→FORAMEN MAGNUM DECOMPRESSION ± SHUNT
    • Scoliosis→FUSION 3–6 MONTHS AFTER decompression

Chiari decompression (TO)suboccipital craniectomy to uncover tonsils + C1 laminectomy + intradural inspection ± tonsil reduction + duraplasty

Primary spinal syringomyelia (TO)shunting (pleura/peritoneum) has high failure (cavity collapses on tube) → resect focal obstruction

Scoliosisdecompression alone may improve curve but large curves / > 8 yrs still progress

6Complications

Undiagnosed syrinx + fusion↑ neurological deterioration / paralysis

How it's asked

"Arnold-Chiari — EXCEPT?""partial CSF block pressure differential" as syrinx cause (TO)

"Syringomyelia — EXCEPT?""pathology wholly in spine = secondary" (it's primary) (TO)

"62-yr painless destroyed shoulder — confirm with?"MRI brain + cervical spine

"Chronic dislocation + destruction after minor trauma — positive test?"MRI of upper cervical spine

"5-yr, 20° left thoracic, hand pinprick loss?"MRI of the entire spine

"12-yr left thoracic curve + Valsalva headaches — MRI?"syrinx + tonsillar herniation (Chiari I)

Thoracic Disc Herniation

Spine · 64 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Frequency~1% of all HNP (Orthobullets) · 0.25–1% (TO) · rare: limited motion, short discs, rib cage

Age4th–6th decades (Orthobullets, TO) · 3rd–5th (OB)

Levellower third · T11-12 commonest (maximal mobility, thin PLL) · 75% at T8–12 · T1–5 only 1% · flexion + torsion concentrate T10–L1 (floating ribs 11, 12)

Risk factorScheuermann's disease (also ↑ neuro deficit)

Asymptomatic prevalenceabnormalities 73%, frank herniation 37%, cord compression 29% (Orthobullets) · ~40% (OB) — read the options

Natural historymost symptomatic cases resolve non-operatively; most imaging lesions never cause symptoms; large herniations shrink

Classification

Type Definition
Bulge Annulus intact
Extruded Through annulus, contained by PLL
Sequestered Free in canal
Location Central · posterolateral · lateral

3Presentation

Commonest symptomaxial back / chest pain (dorsal backache, TO)

Radicularband-like chest/abdominal pain along the intercostal nerve, around or straight through to the anterior chest wall · T10 dermatome commonest (TO) · upper thoracic + lateral discs · arm pain with T2–5

MyelopathyLE hyperreflexia with normal UE reflexes (T11-12 cord), clonus, Babinski, wide-based spastic gait, paraparesis, rectal tone · bowel/bladder 15–20% · sexual dysfunction

Horner'sT2–5 herniation

Work-up of LE UMN signs with normal UEthoracic MRI · hip OA + wide-based gait → thoracic + lumbar MRI before THA
✗ Wrong options: cervical/lumbar surgery

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
Lateral X-ray Disc narrowing, calcification
MRI Most useful · tumour, intradural pathology, myelomalacia · may under-show calcified disc · high false positives
CT / CT myelogram Calcified component

5Management

Thoracic disc herniation
  • Axial / radicular pain→REST, PT, NSAIDs, gabapentin, injections (6–12 wks)
  • Myelopathy / progressive deficit / intolerable pain→DISCECTOMY ± FUSION
    • Central (calcified)→TRANSTHORACIC (± VATS)
    • Lateral, extruded, sequestered→COSTOTRANSVERSECTOMY / LATERAL EXTRACAVITARY
    • Posterior→TRANSPEDICULAR + instrumented fusion
      (NOT LAMINECTOMY ALONE)

Operative criteria

Indication
Myelopathy from the disc (sensory loss + clumsy gait → discectomy), especially progressive
Persistent intolerable pain
✗ Wrong option: most cases need surgery (TO EXCEPT)

Approaches

Approach Fact
Posterior laminectomy Avoid — highest neurological deterioration — cord can't be retracted; thoracic kyphosis drapes cord over disc
Transthoracic (transpleural, T4–T12; VATS) Best for central discs · T12-L1 needs diaphragm takedown · complication intercostal neuralgia
Costotransversectomy Lateral, extruded, sequestered · no pleural violation · hard for calcified discs/osteophytes
Lateral extracavitary More bone resection + blood loss, less neuro risk than laminectomy
Transpedicular + instrumented fusion Posterior option for myelopathy with cord signal change

Thoracic stenosis (degenerative, flavum/facet)laminectomy appropriate (posterior compression) — differs from disc

FVCtransthoracic not safe with FVC 20%

6Complications

Intercostal neuralgiacommonest after transthoracic / endoscopic surgery (portal compression)

X vs Y

Thoracic disc Thoracic stenosis
Compression Anterior (disc, often calcified) Posterior (flavum, facets)
Approach Anterior / lateral / transpedicular Laminectomy

How it's asked

"Commonest thoracic disc level?"T11-12 (TO)

"Large T11-12 disc with imbalance — reflexes?"normal UE, hyperreflexic LE

"Posterolateral thoracic disc pain pattern?"around/through the chest to the anterior wall

"Thoracic disc herniation — true?"found in 40% of asymptomatic people

"New LE weakness, UMN signs, normal arms — next?"thoracic MRI

"Thoracic discs typically occur?"lower third

"Hip pain + wide-based gait + LE weakness — next?"thoracic + lumbar MRI, spine referral

"Thoracic disc — EXCEPT?"most cases need surgery (TO)

"2 wks dermatomal rib-cage pain, normal neuro?"PT + NSAIDs

"Axial pain, thoracic disc — true?"usually resolves with conservative care

"T7-8 disc, sensory loss, clumsy gait?"discectomy (TO)

"Endoscopic transthoracic surgery — commonest complication?"intercostal neuralgia

"29-yr thoracic myelopathy from disc?"thoracic discectomy

"Thoracic disc + myelopathy — approach with most neuro morbidity?"posterior laminectomy

"Thoracic myelopathy, cord signal change, normal cervical MRI?"laminectomy + transpedicular discectomy + instrumented fusion

"Thoracic stenosis with claudication, hyperreflexia, incontinence?"laminectomy

Thoracic Spine Anatomy

Spine · 65 / 69

1Basics

Alignment

Thoracic kyphosisaverage 35°, normal 20–50° (✗ Wrong options: 40–50°) · apex T5–T8 (T6-7) (✗ Wrong options: T4) · adolescents 30–40° (TO)

Lumbar lordosismajority below L3 (TO) · L4-S1 ~60% (TO) · 75% (Orthobullets, previous chapter) · discs give 78% (47°), bodies 12° · apex L3-L4 · range 20–60° (TO) · 20–80° (Orthobullets)

Frontal planestraight; > 10° = scoliosis
✗ Wrong options: < 20° normal

Plumb line (sagittal vertical axis)from C7 centre → posterosuperior corner of S1 (within 2 cm) · anterior = positive · posterior = negative · ideally from dens: anterior to thoracic, posterior to lumbar

Pelvissacral slope < 35° + low PI → flat short lordosis · > 45° + high PI → long curved lordosis (TO)

Conus (TO)lower border of L2 adult, L3 neonate (Orthobullets: L1) · filum to first coccygeal segment

Osteology

Vertebra prominensC7 spinous process

Costal facetsbodies + transverse processes T1–T9 · ribs → most rigid region of axial skeleton

Bodiessize ↑ T1 → T12 · canal dimension varies

Facet orientation & motion

Region Coronal / sagittal Motion
Cervical 0° / 45° All planes
Thoracic 20° / 55° (coronal facets), 6 DOF Rotation; little flex-ext (ribs) · protects heart/lungs
Lumbar 50° / 90° (sagittal facets) Flex-ext; little rotation

Pedicles

Measure Fact
Wall Medial 2× thicker than lateral
Narrowest diameter T4 (Orthobullets, OB) · T4–T6 dip; T6 smallest among T1/T6/T12/L3/L5 (OB)
Largest T1 (upper) · T12 (> L1) at TL junction · T10, T11 > L1 · L5 largest lumbar · C7 largest cervical
T7 Narrow on concave side in AIS
Length T1 20 mm → T4 14 mm (shortest) → T10 20 mm
Transverse angle T1 25° medial, T2 15°, T3–T10 ~10° (5–7°), minimal T11-12 (OB) · 10° mid-thoracic → 30° L5 (Orthobullets)
Sagittal angle 15–17° cephalad thoracic · neutral lumbar · caudal L5

Interpedicular distance (TO)

MinimumT4–T8 · narrowed: achondroplasia (caudal narrowing), thanatophoric dysplasia · widened: diastematomyelia, burst fracture (middle column)

Erector spinae

Functiontrunk extension · dorsal rami

Muscle (medial → lateral) Attachment
Spinalis (medial) Spinous → spinous process
Longissimus (intermediate) Transverse → transverse process
Iliocostalis (lateral) Ilium + ribs → ribs + TPs

Mnemonic"I Love Spine" lateral → medial

5Management

Thoracic approaches

Approach Access
Laminectomy / hemilaminectomy Posterior (not for central disc)
Transpedicular Posterolateral
Costotransversectomy Lateral
Lateral extracavitary Lateral, more ventral
Anterior intracavitary Thoracotomy / VATS — central

How it's asked

"Interpedicular distance minimal at?"T4–T8 (TO)

"Smallest average transverse pedicle diameter?"T6 (of options)

"Greatest pedicle isthmic width?"T12

"Thoracic pedicle transverse angulation?"25° T1, 15° T2, 10° T3–T10

"Plumb line passes through?"posterosuperior margin of S1 (TO)

"Correct normal alignment statement?"majority of lumbar lordosis below L3 (TO)

Thoracolumbar Burst Fractures

Spine · 66 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology & mechanism

Mechanismhigh-energy axial load ± flexion (falls, MCC) · anterior + middle column compression → retropulsion

Levelthoracolumbar junction T10–L2 (commonest L1, OB key L2 among L2–S1) · > 50% of thoracic + lumbar fractures (T12–L2) · TL junction = stress riser between rigid ribbed thoracic (T1–10) and mobile lumbar spine · T10–12 floating ribs

Neuromaximal canal occlusion at impact (recoil after) · retropulsed fragments resorb, rarely cause late deterioration · deficit type depends on level vs conus: cord / conus / cauda equina

Associated injuries

Injury Fact
Non-contiguous spine fractures 20% → image whole spine
Traumatic durotomy Laminar fracture, split spinous process (entrapped roots)
Thoracic fracture + deficit 1/3 haemopneumothorax, great-vessel injury, diaphragm rupture
Flexion-distraction / dislocation Bowel rupture, vessels, kidney, liver, spleen, pancreas
Long-bone fractures Hamper rehab

Anatomy

Denis columnsanterior (ALL, anterior 2/3 body) · middle (PLL, posterior 1/3 body) · posterior (pedicles, laminae, facets, flavum, spinous process, PLC)

Instabilitymiddle column injury (widened interpedicular distance, posterior body height loss) · PLC + anterior/middle column injury

PLCsupraspinous, interspinous, flavum, facet capsule · clearly ruptured: bony Chance, interspinous widening, progressive kyphosis, facet diastasis · indeterminate: MRI signal only

Conusends L1-2; L1 fracture → conus medullaris syndrome (bowel/bladder paralysis ± mixed UMN/LMN LE)

3Presentation

Vitalshypotension + bradycardia = neurogenic · + tachycardia = haemorrhage

Examlog roll · palpable gap / ↑ interspinous distance (posterior injury) · full neuro incl. reflexes · absent BCR = spinal shock (up to 72 h) · hyperactive BCR = disinhibition, complete cord injury

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray (whole spine; often trauma CT) AP: widened pedicles, coronal deformity · lateral: retropulsion (underestimated), kyphosis · flexion-extension once stable: spinous process diastasis = PLC injury
CT Best for retropulsion (axial), comminution · more sensitive than X-ray
MRI PLC (STIR/T2 signal, sagittal), conus level, compression, cord oedema/haematoma · investigation of choice (TO) · nearly every case unless CT/X-ray already shows instability
CT myelogram MRI contraindicated; durotomy

MRI prognosiscord oedema > 2 levels or haematoma (low T2 with oedema halo) → poor motor recovery

Denis burst types

Type Pattern
A Both endplates, retropulsion
B Superior endplate — commonest (axial + flexion)
C Inferior endplate
D Burst-rotation (mimics fracture-dislocation)
E Burst-lateral flexion (↑ interpedicular distance vs lateral compression)

TLICS

Category Points
Morphology Compression 1 · burst 2 · rotation/translation 3 · distraction 4
Neurology Intact 0 · root 2 · incomplete cord/conus 3 · complete cord/conus 2 · cauda equina 3
PLC Intact 0 · indeterminate 2 · disrupted 3 (largest single contribution)
Total < 4 non-op · 4 either · > 4 surgery

TO rulesmax 10 · score each level, highest decides · translation/rotation always with PLC (3 + 3 = 6) · posterior distraction always PLC (4 + 3 = 7) · incomplete scores more than complete

Not in TLICS% canal compromise

Worked scoresburst + intact PLC + intact neuro = 2 → non-op · burst + indeterminate PLC + root = 6 → surgery · burst + incomplete + PLC = 8 → surgery · burst + translation + PLC + ASIA B = 9

5Management

TL burst fracture
  • Neuro intact + PLC intact (TLICS ≤ 3)→MOBILISE AS TOLERATED ± TLSO
    (no bed rest or traction)
  • PLC injury / progressive kyphosis / polytrauma, intact→POSTERIOR INSTRUMENTED STABILISATION
  • Deficit with compression / TLICS ≥ 5→DECOMPRESSION + STABILISATION
    • Below conus, PLC injured, fracture-dislocation→POSTERIOR (transpedicular, posterior corpectomy, cage)
    • Anterior compression above conus (≤ L2), comminution, kyphosis > 30°, > 4–5 days→ANTERIOR / LATERAL CORPECTOMY ± posterior

Non-operative

Neuro intact + stableequivalent outcomes (pain, function, RTW, kyphosis, fragment resorption at 5 yrs; better PROs at 16–22 yrs) with fewer complications than surgery · surgery → ↑ complications and disability

TLSOno clear advantage; use for comfort · may begin early ambulation without brace if pain controlled · older teaching: TLSO/body cast 3–6 months

Old thresholdskyphosis > 30°, height loss > 50% (controversial; no longer indications alone)

Operative criteria

Indication Detail
PLC injury on MRI Indication even in a compression fracture (TO) (✗ Wrong options: radiculopathy · middle column · kyphosis 15°)
Neuro deficit + compression > single root level (TO) · complete and incomplete
Progressive deficit / CES after bracing Anterior decompression + strut → posterior instrumentation
Progressive kyphosis, polytrauma Stabilise
Laminar fracture + unilateral L5–S4 deficit Laminectomy, free entrapped roots, repair dura, posterior fusion

TO burst L4 with deficitposterior decompression + stabilisation
✗ Wrong options: laminectomy alone · bed rest · brace

Near-complete canal compromise, incomplete deficit at L1anterior decompression + stabilisation

L1 burst + incomplete cord + PLC (TLICS 8)lateral L1 corpectomy + T12-L2 fusion (cord at level → avoid posterior retraction)

Technique

Posteriorpedicle screws · historically 3 above / 2 below; modern 1 above / 1 below (short segment) — not at TL junction · "fuse short, instrument long" · percutaneous option · two above + two below (T11–L3) for L1 burst with deficit (OB) · no laminectomy alone (destabilises)

Indirect decompressiondistraction + lordosis → ligamentotaxis (PLL + annulus pull fragments) · less effective after 4–5 days · monoaxial screws give more distraction · equal to anterior + 360° in studies · overdistraction → pseudarthrosis, pain, deformity

Direct posteriortranspedicular removal below conus (L2) · above conus → cord retraction risk

Anteriorcorpectomy to contralateral pedicle wall · strut (tricortical iliac, allograft, expandable cage) · lumbar: retroperitoneal (below L1) · T11–L1: left lateral lumbotomy (avoid liver) · above T11: right thoracotomy (avoid great vessels) · diaphragm level

Pedicle screw entry (TO)intersection technique commonest free-hand (lateral facet line × TP bisector) · mammillary point more lateral

Unkeyed TO item1 above + 1 below with index-level screw = short-segment construct (source gives no key)

6Complications

Complication Fact
Pain Commonest · overdistraction
Dural tear / entrapped roots Laminar fractures · fewer with anterior approach
Progressive kyphosis Missed PLC/PLL injury, comminution
Flat back Pain, stooped posture; post-traumatic syrinx
SSI Up to 10%
Pseudarthrosis Overdistraction
Iatrogenic neuro injury 1% — medial screws, cord manipulation
Approach Ileus (transperitoneal), pleural effusion (thoracotomy), loss of correction (posterior)

Chance fracture (TO)

Mechanismflexion-distraction around an axis anterior to the spine (lap belt, "jack-knife") · middle + posterior columns fail in distraction · no body crush · bony Chance → hyperextension brace; ligamentous/unstable → surgery

How it's asked

"ASIA E L1 burst, intact PLC — surgery?"increased complication rates

"Neuro intact, PLC intact — advantage of surgery?"none; equivalent outcomes

"68-yr L3 burst, ASIA E, 20% canal occlusion?"orthosis + early mobilisation

"Stable burst, no deficit — long-term surgery outcome?"more disability + complications

"L1 burst, 25% canal, intact PLC?"ambulation ± TLSO

"L1 burst, intact PLC — true?"early ambulation without brace if pain controlled

"L2 burst TLICS 2?"external orthosis

"Snowboarder, stable burst, non-op?"equivalent clinical outcome

"5-yr difference surgery vs non-op?"incidence of complications

"Rock climber L1 burst, 20° kyphosis, intact?"TLSO / body cast with mobilisation

"Intoxicated fall, displaced L3 burst, intact?"early mobilisation ± TLSO

"Fall from tree, L1 burst, intact?"early mobilisation ± orthosis

"Stable L1 burst — true?"non-op: fewer complications, similar outcomes

"L1 burst + L2 compression, intact, TLICS < 4?"immediate mobilisation in TLSO

"Elderly fall — non-operative candidate?"T12 burst, intact PLC, intact neuro

"Flexion-distraction picture — true?"PLC injury confirmed (TO)

"Burst, indeterminate PLC, root deficit?"TLICS 6, surgery

"Greatest TLICS contribution?"PLC disruption

"Not in TLICS?"% canal compromise

"L2 burst, intact PLC + neuro?"TLICS 2, non-op

"L1 compression 40% — surgical indication?"PLC injury on MRI (TO)

"L4 burst + deficit?"posterior decompression + stabilisation (TO)

"L4 burst in TLSO, new CES day 5?"anterior decompression + strut + posterior instrumentation

"L1 burst, multi-root weakness, fall on feet?"decompression + T11–L3 fusion with postural reduction

"Compression fracture + MRI PLC disruption, intact?"posterior instrumented fusion

"L1 burst, ~100% canal, sacral sparing?"anterior decompression + stabilisation

"L2 burst, unilateral L5–S4 deficit, laminar fracture?"L2 laminectomy + T11–L4 posterior fusion

"L1 burst + translation, ASIA B, PLC oedema?"corpectomy + cage + posterior instrumentation

"L1 burst, incomplete cord + PLC injury?"lateral L1 corpectomy + T12-L2 fusion

"L1 burst, mixed UMN/LMN, can't void?"conus medullaris injury

"Chance fracture mechanism?"flexion distraction (TO)

"Chance — columns failing?"middle + posterior in distraction (TO)

"Commonest site of compression/burst fractures?"L2 (of options)

"Burst fracture, intact PLC — mechanism?"axial load

"Investigation of choice for burst fracture?"MRI (TO)

"Commonest pedicle screw technique?"intersection technique (TO)

Thoracolumbar Fracture-Dislocation

Spine · 67 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Incidence~4% of SCI at level 1 centres · 50–60% have SCI · M:F 4:1

Mechanismhigh energy (MVA commonest, falls, sport, violence) · acceleration/deceleration → hyperflexion + rotation + shear

Sitethoracolumbar junction T10–L2 (kyphosis → lordosis transition; vulnerable to shear) · injures cord, conus or cauda equina by level

Associationshead, thoracic, abdominal injuries

3Presentation

Pre-hospitalbackboard + collar · ATLS · inspect for open injury, kyphosis · palpate step-off, crepitus · ASIA score, rectal exam, GCS

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
AP + lateral X-ray Fracture type + dislocation
CT Pattern (unilateral facet dislocation), canal, screening in blunt trauma
MRI Cord + ligaments (PLL) · deficit level not matching bony level

TLICS

Thresholds≤ 3 non-op · 4 indeterminate · ≥ 5 surgery

Translation/rotation3 + PLC 3 = ≥ 6 → surgery

Example (OB)T12 burst (2) + incomplete cord (3) + intact PLC (0) = 5 → posterior decompression + arthrodesis

5Management

Posterior open reduction + instrumented fusionmost fracture-dislocations · pedicle screws for distraction-reduction · 2 levels above + 2 below · post-op CT/MRI for residual anterior compression · early surgery > delayed or non-op

Avoid isolated anterior procedureALL usually intact; anterior surgery further destabilises; anterior reduction difficult · no closed reduction + TLSO or bed rest

With deficitposterior fusion + instrumentation + posterolateral decompression

Pedicle screws (TO)

Entryintersection technique commonest (free-hand) · lumbar: line just lateral to inferior articular process × TP bisector · thoracic: mid inferior facet × mid TP insertion · S1: inferolateral base of superior articular process · mammillary point more lateral than intersection, more lateral than pars point

Osteoporotic bone — best pull-outlarger thread depth · > 60% of hold from pedicle cortex · fine threads proximally, coarse distally · insertion at 0–10° better · cement augmentation (PMMA, CaP, HA), expandable screws
✗ Wrong options: cannulation · larger core (reduces thread depth) · larger pitch (narrower pitch better)

Failureloosening 0.6–11% · breakage 2.6–60% · pedicle screws span all 3 columns (Boucher 1950s, Roy-Camille 1980s)

Left retroperitoneal lumbar approach (TO)

Structure Fact
Ureter Adherent to posterior peritoneum, falls away from psoas
Sympathetic trunk On the medial border of psoas (✗ Wrong options: lateral) → injury: warm red ipsilateral (left) foot, so the normal right foot seems cold + pale
Ilioinguinal nerve Emerges from lateral psoas border → quadratus lumborum

Perched facets (TO)

Mechanismdistractive flexion (subaxial) · spectrum: sprain → perched → unifacet → bifacet dislocation → floating vertebra

6Complications

Post-traumatic paincommonest; ↑ with kyphosis

Deformityscoliosis, progressive kyphosis (missed PLL injury), flat back (pain, stooped posture, fatigue), post-traumatic syrinx

Othersneuro injury, CES, DVT, nonunion

X vs Y

Burst Fracture-dislocation
Mechanism Axial load ± flexion Flexion + rotation + shear
Alignment Adjacent levels aligned Translation
PLC Variable Disrupted
Treatment Often non-op if intact Posterior reduction + fusion

How it's asked

"T12 burst, incomplete cord, PLC intact — TLICS + treatment?"5; posterior decompression + arthrodesis

"Fracture-dislocation with quad weakness — definitive?"posterior instrumented fusion + posterolateral decompression

"Commonest pedicle screw technique?"intersection technique (TO)

"Pedicle screw entry point?"mammillary process, lateral to pars (TO)

"Osteoporotic bone — screw property?"larger thread depth (TO)

"Left retroperitoneal approach — EXCEPT?"sympathetic trunk on the lateral psoas border (it's medial) (TO)

"Perched facets seen in?"distractive flexion (TO)

Traumatic Spondylolisthesis of Axis (Hangman's Fracture)

Spine · 68 / 69

1Basics

Definitionbilateral C2 pars (or pedicle) fractures → traumatic spondylolisthesis of C2 on C3

Mechanismprimary hyperextension (pars fracture) + secondary flexion (tears PLL + disc → anterolisthesis) · MVA/falls: hyperextension-compression · judicial hanging: hyperextension-distraction (cord transection)
✗ Wrong options: primary hyperflexion (TO)

Associations30% other C-spine fracture · vertebral artery injury (MRA if suspected)

3Presentation

Neck painusually neurologically intact (fragments separate → canal widens) · deficit mainly type III

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
CT Study of choice for pattern
Flexion-extension X-ray Subluxation
MRA Vertebral artery injury

Levine-Edwards

Type Mechanism Features Treatment
I Axial compression + hyperextension < 3 mm displacement, no angulation, C2-3 disc intact, stable Rigid collar 4–6 wks
II Hyperextension + axial load → rebound flexion > 3 mm, significant angulation, vertical fracture line, disc + PLL torn < 5 mm: traction + extension → halo 6–12 wks · > 5 mm: surgery or prolonged traction · usually auto-fuse C2 on C3
IIA Flexion-distraction No displacement, significant angulation, horizontal/oblique line No traction (worsens) → gentle axial compression + hyperextension → compression halo 6–12 wks
III Flexion-distraction → hyperextension Type I + bilateral C2-3 facet dislocation Open reduction + fusion (posterior)

Halo duration6–12 wks (Orthobullets) · 8–12 wks · "2 months" (TO)

5Management

Hangman's fracture
  • Type I→RIGID COLLAR 4–6 wks
  • Type II
    • 3–5 mm → TRACTION + EXTENSION→HALO
    • > 5 mm, severe angulation→SURGERY
  • Type IIA → NO TRACTION · hyperextension + compression→HALO
  • Type III→OPEN REDUCTION of facets + C2-3 FUSION

Surgical optionsanterior C2-3 interbody fusion (disc injury) · posterior C1-3 fusion · bilateral C2 pars screw osteosynthesis · anterior facet fixation for failed posterior fusion

X vs Y

Type II Type IIA
Displacement > 3 mm None
Angulation Significant Severe
Fracture line Vertical Horizontal
Reduction Traction + extension No traction — compression + extension

How it's asked

"Polytrauma, undisplaced C2 pars fractures, intact disc?"type I — rigid collar

"Hangman's — EXCEPT?""primary hyperflexion mechanism" (TO)

"CT shows bilateral C2 pars fracture — called?"Hangman's fracture (TO, unkeyed — options Jefferson, Hangman's, odontoid II, flexion teardrop)

Vertebral Artery Injury

Spine · 69 / 69

1Basics

Epidemiology

Setting Rate
All trauma 0.5% · 70% of blunt VAI have a cervical fracture
Cervical spine fractures 33–39%
Anterior cervical surgery 0.3%
Posterior cervical surgery 4.1–8.2% (C1-2 transarticular screws)

Anatomy

Segment Course Risk
V1 Subclavian → anterior to C7 TP → enters C6 transverse foramen Highest blunt injury risk at C6 entry
V2 Foramina C6 → C1 Most traumatic injuries; lateral mass/pedicle screw drilling
V3 Over C1 arch → foramen magnum C1 lateral exposure, laminectomy, caudal-lateral transarticular screws (2nd highest risk at C1-2)
V4 Intradural → joins opposite VA → basilar —

Anomalies2.7% in anterior surgery (foramen medial to / within 1.5 mm of uncovertebral joint) · 2.3–20% at C1-2: high-riding C2 foramen, C2 pedicle erosion, thin lateral mass → CTA before transarticular screws

Safe zones

C1 posterior dissectionstay < 1.5 cm lateral to midline above the C1 arch (Orthobullets, OB) · < 1 cm (OC fusion chapter) · retraction > 2 cm past midline injures VA (OB) — read the options · dissect subperiosteally on the inferior arch

C1 lateral mass screw10° medial, 22° cephalad → inferior + medial to the VA · C2 root crossed first (some sacrifice it)

3Presentation

HistoryC1/C2 fractures, subaxial facet fractures/dislocations, cervical/CVJ surgery

Vertebrobasilar insufficiencydizziness, vertigo, nausea, diplopia, blindness, ataxia, bilateral weakness, dysphagia · onset variable (days–years); many asymptomatic

4Diagnosis & Classification

Key radiology findings

Study Fact
X-ray Fracture patterns raise suspicion
CT angiography Sensitivity ~100% · screening test
MRA 93.9% · deficits from VA/basilar ischaemia
Intra-op / post-op angiography Collaterals before ligation, fistula/pseudoaneurysm embolisation

Diagnostic criteria – CTA screening (any one)

Indication
C1, C2 or C3 fracture · fracture into transverse foramen (≥ 1 mm displacement) · subluxation (VAI in 20%)
Cervical SCI · hanging injury · occipitocervical dissociation, basilar skull fracture
Unexplained central/lateralising deficit · acute infarct on CT · GCS < 9 · diffuse axonal injury
Le Fort II/III or facial fracture · major thoracic injury / 1st rib fracture

C1 floating lateral mass + visual change, nausea, dysphagia, dizzinessCT angiogram first
✗ Wrong options: traction → may be lethal

Biffl grading

Grade Lesion
I Dissection / intramural haematoma < 25% stenosis
II Thrombus, intimal flap, > 25% stenosis
III Pseudoaneurysm
IV Occlusion
V Transection

5Management

Vertebral artery injury
  • Traumatic→ANTICOAGULATION / ANTIPLATELET (aspirin first line, heparin)
    (CI: major intracranial infarct, intraspinal haematoma/AVF) · stabilise fracture as needed
  • Intra-operative bleeding→TAMPONADE (haemostatic agents, bone wax, insert the screw)
    • Controlled→consider DIRECT MICROVASCULAR REPAIR
    • Uncontrolled + adequate collaterals→ENDOVASCULAR EMBOLISATION / clip / ligation

Tamponade risksdelayed haemorrhage, fistula

Ligationangiogram first (collaterals; left VA usually dominant) · risks cerebellar infarct (PICA), cranial nerve palsy, hemiplegia

Complex right VA injury during O-C3 fusion, tamponade failed, segmental defect, good collateralsembolisation
✗ Wrong options: antifibrinolytic · direct repair · graft

Post-opobserve or immediate angiography (fistula, pseudoaneurysm, collaterals)

6Complications

LateAV fistula, delayed haemorrhage, pseudoaneurysm, thromboembolism, stroke (persistent vertigo), death

Cerebrovascular (TO)

Topic Fact
SAH work-up NCCT → LP if negative · CTA first for source · DSA = gold standard (finds source in 80–85%)
Aneurysm sites Carotid system 85–95%: ACoA (commonest, 30%), PCoA 25%, MCA 20% · posterior 5–15% (basilar tip commonest) · multiple in 20–30%
PCoA aneurysm 3rd nerve palsy (ptosis, dilated pupil) in ~9% · new CN III palsy = impending rupture
ISUIA > 10 mm → ~5%/yr rupture · 7–12 mm 2.6%, 13–24 mm 14.5%, ≥ 25 mm 40% (5-yr) · anterior commoner, posterior ruptures more
AVM No capillary bed, no intervening parenchyma in nidus · 2–4%/yr bleed · small AVMs bleed more (higher feeder pressure) · death 1%/yr · rebleed 6–18% in yr 1, evenly spread (✗ Wrong options: highest in first 2 wks) · treatment: embolisation, stereotactic radiosurgery, surgery — all
Spetzler-Martin Size < 3 / 3–6 / > 6 cm = 1/2/3 · eloquent 1 · deep drainage 1
Cavernoma No intervening brain parenchyma
Anterior choroidal occlusion Contralateral hemiplegia, hemihypaesthesia, homonymous hemianopia · cognition spared
Carotid endarterectomy Symptomatic 50–99%, asymptomatic 60–99% → endarterectomy (less stroke than stenting, more MI) · clamp release ECA → CCA → ICA
Subclavian steal Left subclavian occluded proximal to the vertebral origin
ICA : ECA flow 80 : 20
Malignant MCA infarct + haemorrhagic transformation Decompressive craniectomy

Spinal vascular malformations (TO)

Type Fact
I — dural AVF Commonest, acquired, slow progressive myelopathy from venous congestion + steal (✗ Wrong options: recurrent bleeding) · treat by ligating/embolising the draining (leptomeningeal) vein · 15–20% other AVMs
II — glomus Intramedullary nidus (congenital)
III — juvenile Intramedullary-extramedullary, high flow, SAH risk
IV Perimedullary (intradural extramedullary) fistula

Aggressivenessleptomeningeal vein (fistulous) determines outcome
✗ Wrong options: location · symptoms

Unkeyed TO itemsSAH CT appearance (classic: hyperdensity in basal cisterns + sulci) and basilar tip aneurysm treatment (coiling usual) — answers not provided by source

How it's asked

"C1 lateral mass screw lies relative to VA?"inferior + medial

"VA injured during C1 dissection — where?"cephalad to C1 arch > 1.5 cm lateral to midline

"Structure injured > 2 cm lateral on atlas arch?"vertebral artery — from subclavian through transverse foramina

"Uncontrolled VA bleeding, segmental defect, good collaterals?"embolisation

"C1 floating lateral mass + VBI symptoms — next?"CT angiogram

"AVM without intervening parenchyma?"cavernous haemangioma (TO)

"Clamp removal after CEA?"ECA, CCA, ICA (TO)

"5 mm ACoA aneurysm — ISUIA true?"> 10 mm ≈ 5%/yr rupture (TO)

"Anterior choroidal occlusion?"hemiplegia, hemihypaesthesia, hemianopia (TO)

"Perimesencephalic SAH — gold standard?"DSA (TO)

"AVMs — EXCEPT?"rebleed highest in first 2 weeks (TO)

"Subclavian steal?"left subclavian occluded proximal to vertebral origin (TO)

"Frontal AVM — options?"all (embolisation, radiosurgery, combined) (TO)

"80% symptomatic ICA stenosis?"open endarterectomy (TO)

"Malignant MCA infarct with bleed, hemiplegia?"decompressive craniectomy (TO)

"Headache, neck pain, right ptosis?"PCoA aneurysm SAH (TO)

"ICA : ECA flow?"80 : 20 (TO)

"Spinal dural AVF — EXCEPT?"recurrent bleeding causes symptoms (TO)

"Aggressive spinal AVM factor?"leptomeningeal vein (TO)

"Glomus AVM?"type II (TO)
